# Questions – case-style

Answer in the [interactive quiz](https://mazur-andrej.github.io/Kviz/) to get marked automatically. Answers are hidden below each question.


## Case-style practice · W01-D1 · Sepsis-3, SOFA and SOFA-2

**1.** Five patients have received adequate fluid resuscitation. Which one meets the Sepsis-3 criteria for septic shock?

- A. Acute cholangitis; MAP 58 mmHg after 30 mL/kg crystalloid, noradrenaline 0.12 µg/kg/min started, MAP now 67 mmHg, lactate 2.9 mmol/L
- B. Pneumonia; noradrenaline 0.08 µg/kg/min needed to keep MAP 66 mmHg, lactate 1.5 mmol/L
- C. Faecal peritonitis; MAP 72 mmHg without vasopressors, lactate 4.6 mmol/L
- D. Ruptured abdominal aortic aneurysm; noradrenaline 0.3 µg/kg/min, lactate 6.0 mmol/L
- E. Pyelonephritis; SBP 88 mmHg on arrival, MAP 75 mmHg after 1 L crystalloid, lactate 2.4 mmol/L

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.**

Septic shock (Sepsis-3) = sepsis **plus** a vasopressor requirement to keep MAP ≥ 65 mmHg **and** lactate > 2 mmol/L despite adequate fluids. B lacks the lactate criterion (sepsis only), C has no vasopressor need, D is not an infection (haemorrhagic shock), E responded to fluids.

**EDIC trap:** Hyperlactataemia alone, or a vasopressor alone, is not septic shock.

**Ref:** Singer M, et al. Sepsis-3. *JAMA* 2016;315:801–810.

</details>

**2.** A 79-year-old nursing-home resident on the ward has fever, new confusion, RR 24/min, SBP 104 mmHg and SpO₂ 93 % on air. Regarding screening and the diagnosis of sepsis:

- A. Her qSOFA score is 2. — T / F
- B. SSC 2026 recommends qSOFA rather than NEWS/NEWS2 as the single screening tool for sepsis. — T / F
- C. Had her qSOFA been < 2, sepsis would have been excluded. — T / F
- D. If her baseline SOFA is unknown and she has no known pre-existing organ dysfunction, the baseline is assumed to be 0. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – T.**

- A (True) – RR ≥ 22 (1) + altered mentation (1); SBP ≤ 100 is not met (0) → qSOFA 2.
- B (False) – SSC 2026 recommends **against** qSOFA as a single screening tool compared with NEWS/NEWS2, MEWS or SIRS (strong, moderate certainty).
- C (False) – qSOFA is prognostic, not diagnostic; a low score does not rule sepsis out.
- D (True) – Sepsis-3 convention – sepsis = acute rise in SOFA ≥ 2 attributable to infection.

**EDIC trap:** qSOFA predicts poor outcome; it neither defines nor excludes sepsis.

**Ref:** Singer M, et al. Sepsis-3. *JAMA* 2016; Surviving Sepsis Campaign 2026.

</details>

**3.** A 54-year-old man with septic shock is on noradrenaline 0.3 µg/kg/min plus vasopressin, invasive ventilation with P/F 140 mmHg, continuous RRT and platelets 70 × 10⁹/L. Scoring him with SOFA-2 (JAMA 2025):

- A. His kidney score is 4 because he is receiving RRT. — T / F
- B. Platelets of 70 × 10⁹/L give the maximum haemostasis score of 4. — T / F
- C. SOFA-2 adds a sixth, gastrointestinal organ system. — T / F
- D. In the cardiovascular system, noradrenaline 0.3 µg/kg/min plus a second vasopressor scores higher than noradrenaline 0.3 µg/kg/min alone. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – T.**

- A (True) – Receiving (or meeting criteria for) RRT = 4 kidney points.
- B (False) – SOFA-2 platelet cut-offs are 150 / 100 / 80 / 50; 4 points only at ≤ 50 × 10⁹/L, so 70 scores 3.
- C (False) – SOFA-2 keeps 6 systems – brain, respiratory, cardiovascular, liver, kidney, haemostasis; GI and immune dysfunction were not included.
- D (True) – Combined NA + adrenaline 0.2–0.4 µg/kg/min = 3; any other vasopressor or inotrope adds a level (→ 4).

**EDIC trap:** SOFA-2 has the same 6 systems with new names and modern organ support – no GI system.

**Ref:** Ranzani OT, et al. SOFA-2. *JAMA* 2025.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W01-D2 · Initial septic shock resuscitation

**1.** A 58-year-old woman arrives in the emergency department with obstructive pyelonephritis. BP 78/42 mmHg (MAP 54), HR 124/min, capillary refill time 5 s, lactate 5.1 mmol/L. Regarding her initial management according to the Surviving Sepsis Campaign 2026:

- A. A balanced crystalloid is suggested in preference to 0.9 % saline for the initial fluid resuscitation. — T / F
- B. After the initial fluid, the decision to give more should be guided by dynamic measures (e.g. passive leg raising with stroke-volume measurement) and capillary refill time rather than by a CVP target of 8–12 mmHg. — T / F
- C. Antimicrobials may reasonably be delayed until two sets of blood cultures have been obtained, even if this takes 90 minutes. — T / F
- D. Noradrenaline should not be started until a central venous catheter has been inserted. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – F.**

- A (True) – SSC 2026 suggests balanced crystalloids over 0.9 % saline (conditional, moderate certainty); the exception is TBI.
- B (True) – Static CVP targets came from early goal-directed therapy, which ProCESS, ARISE and ProMISe did not support; dynamic measures and capillary refill time (ANDROMEDA-SHOCK) are suggested to guide further fluid.
- C (False) – In possible septic shock antimicrobials are recommended immediately, ideally within 1 h; cultures are drawn first only if this causes no substantial delay.
- D (False) – Noradrenaline may be started peripherally rather than delaying for central access (conditional, very low certainty).

**EDIC trap:** "CVP 8–12 mmHg" is an outdated EGDT target.

**Ref:** Prescott HC, et al. Surviving Sepsis Campaign 2026. *Intensive Care Med* 2026; PRISM investigators. *N Engl J Med* 2017;376:2223–2234.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W01-D3 · Resuscitation fluids and early vasopressors

**1.** Concerning landmark trials of resuscitation fluids in critically ill adults:

- A. In ALBIOS, giving 20 % albumin to keep serum albumin ≥ 30 g/L reduced 90-day mortality in severe sepsis. — T / F
- B. In 6S, hydroxyethyl starch 130/0.42 increased 90-day mortality and the need for renal replacement therapy compared with Ringer's acetate in severe sepsis. — T / F
- C. In CLASSIC, restricting IV fluid after initial resuscitation of septic shock reduced 90-day mortality. — T / F
- D. In the traumatic brain injury subgroup of SAFE, 4 % albumin was associated with higher mortality than saline. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – F, B – T, C – F, D – T.**

- A (False) – ALBIOS showed no difference in 28- or 90-day mortality (only a post-hoc signal in septic shock).
- B (True) – 6S: more deaths and more RRT with HES 130/0.42 than with Ringer's acetate; HES licences were suspended in the EU in 2022.
- C (False) – CLASSIC: no mortality difference (42.3 % vs 42.1 %); SSC 2026 therefore suggests either a liberal or a restrictive approach after the initial 30 mL/kg.
- D (True) – SAFE-TBI: higher 24-month mortality with 4 % albumin, probably via raised ICP.

**Ref:** Caironi P, et al. ALBIOS. *N Engl J Med* 2014;370:1412–1421; Perner A, et al. 6S. *N Engl J Med* 2012;367:124–134; Meyhoff TS, et al. CLASSIC. *N Engl J Med* 2022;386:2459–2470; SAFE Study Investigators. *N Engl J Med* 2007;357:874–884.

</details>

**2.** A 34-year-old woman (60 kg) with pyelonephritis has received 2 L of balanced crystalloid over 90 minutes. MAP is 56 mmHg, HR 128/min, capillary refill time 5 s and lactate 4.6 mmol/L. A passive leg raising test increases stroke volume by only 4 %. Antibiotics were given within the first hour. A central venous catheter is not yet in place. What is the most appropriate next step?

- A. A further 1000 mL of 0.9 % saline over 30 minutes
- B. Start noradrenaline through a well-sited large peripheral cannula now
- C. Wait for ultrasound-guided central venous access before starting any vasopressor
- D. Give 400 mL of 20 % albumin
- E. Start dobutamine 5 µg/kg/min

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

**Why B:** She has received > 30 mL/kg, remains hypotensive with hypoperfusion, and a PLR rise in SV of only 4 % (< 10 %) shows she is unlikely to respond to more fluid. SSC 2026 recommends noradrenaline first line and suggests starting it peripherally rather than delaying for a central line (large proximal vein, extravasation checks, switch to central access if prolonged).

**Why not the others:**

- A – More saline is unlikely to raise SV in a fluid non-responder and adds chloride and oedema.
- C – Waiting for central access needlessly prolongs hypotension.
- D – Albumin is only suggested after large crystalloid volumes and would not help a fluid non-responder.
- E – Dobutamine is for proven cardiac dysfunction with hypoperfusion despite adequate MAP.

**Ref:** Prescott HC, et al. Surviving Sepsis Campaign 2026. *Intensive Care Med* 2026; Monnet X, et al. *Crit Care* 2015;19:18.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W01-D4 · Vasopressors in septic shock

**1.** Regarding vasopressin in adult septic shock:

- A. Its vasoconstrictor effect is relatively preserved in acidaemia, unlike that of catecholamines. — T / F
- B. Its pressor effect is mediated mainly by V2 receptors in the renal collecting duct. — T / F
- C. SSC 2026 suggests titrating vasopressin upwards like noradrenaline, to doses of about 0.1 U/min. — T / F
- D. In VANISH, early vasopressin significantly increased kidney failure–free days compared with noradrenaline. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – F.**

- A (True) – V1a signalling (Gq → IP3 → Ca²⁺, plus K-ATP channel inhibition) is less affected by acidosis than adrenergic receptors – one rationale for its catecholamine-sparing role.
- B (False) – V2 receptors mediate antidiuresis; vasoconstriction is via **V1a** receptors.
- C (False) – Vasopressin is added at a fixed dose, usually up to 0.03 U/min (sometimes 0.04); higher doses cause digital, mesenteric and myocardial ischaemia. SSC 2026 suggests adding it when noradrenaline requirements escalate.
- D (False) – VANISH found no difference in kidney failure–free days; less RRT use with vasopressin was a secondary finding.

**Ref:** Gordon AC, et al. VANISH. *JAMA* 2016;316:509–518; Russell JA, et al. VASST. *N Engl J Med* 2008;358:877–887; Prescott HC, et al. Surviving Sepsis Campaign 2026. *Intensive Care Med* 2026.

</details>

**2.** A 52-year-old man with severe community-acquired pneumonia has received 40 mL/kg of balanced crystalloid and appropriate antibiotics. MAP is 57 mmHg, HR 118/min, lactate 3.8 mmol/L. Bedside echocardiography shows a small, hyperdynamic left ventricle with a collapsible IVC variation that has disappeared after the fluid. Which is the most appropriate initial vasopressor strategy?

- A. Noradrenaline titrated to a MAP of 65 mmHg
- B. Dopamine titrated to a MAP of 65 mmHg
- C. Vasopressin monotherapy at 0.03 U/min
- D. Phenylephrine titrated to a MAP of 80 mmHg
- E. Adrenaline titrated to a MAP of 80 mmHg

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.**

**Why A:** SSC 2026 recommends noradrenaline as the first-line vasopressor, with an initial MAP target of 65 mmHg (strong; 60–65 mmHg suggested if ≥ 65 years). He is 52, adequately filled (IVC no longer collapsible) and vasoplegic with a hyperdynamic LV.

**Why not the others:**

- B – Dopamine causes more arrhythmias than noradrenaline (SOAP II) and is recommended against.
- C – Vasopressin is an add-on when noradrenaline requirements escalate, not monotherapy.
- D – Phenylephrine is not a recommended agent, and 80 mmHg gave no survival benefit in SEPSISPAM.
- E – Adrenaline is a second agent (raises lactate, tachycardia), and the 80 mmHg target is too high.

**Ref:** Prescott HC, et al. Surviving Sepsis Campaign 2026. *Intensive Care Med* 2026; De Backer D, et al. SOAP II. *N Engl J Med* 2010;362:779–789; Asfar P, et al. SEPSISPAM. *N Engl J Med* 2014;370:1583–1593.

</details>

**3.** A 67-year-old woman with acute cholangitis has received 35 mL/kg of crystalloid and piperacillin–tazobactam. MAP remains 55 mmHg, CVP 13 mmHg, ScvO2 62 %, haemoglobin 82 g/L, lactate 4.2 mmol/L. An ERCP slot is available in 2 hours. Her capillary refill time is 4 s and urine output 0.2 mL/kg/h. Each of the following is appropriate EXCEPT:

- A. Starting noradrenaline with an initial MAP target of 60–65 mmHg
- B. Arranging biliary decompression as soon as she is stabilised enough to travel
- C. Transfusing red cells to a haemoglobin above 100 g/L to raise ScvO2 above 70 %
- D. Inserting an arterial line for continuous blood pressure monitoring
- E. Reassessing perfusion with capillary refill time and serial lactate

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.**

**Why C:** Transfusion to a haematocrit > 30 % (and dobutamine) to reach ScvO2 ≥ 70 % were elements of early goal-directed therapy; ProCESS, ARISE and ProMISe found no benefit. TRISS showed a threshold of 70 g/L is as safe as 90 g/L in septic shock, and SSC recommends a restrictive transfusion strategy – so this is the inappropriate action.

**Why the others are appropriate:**

- A – Noradrenaline with an initial MAP target of 60–65 mmHg is suggested for age ≥ 65 years.
- B – Timely biliary decompression is source control, ideally within 6 h.
- D – An arterial line is reasonable once vasopressors are needed and doses may escalate.
- E – Capillary refill time and serial lactate are the suggested perfusion markers.

**Ref:** Holst LB, et al. TRISS. *N Engl J Med* 2014;371:1381–1391; PRISM investigators. *N Engl J Med* 2017;376:2223–2234; Prescott HC, et al. Surviving Sepsis Campaign 2026. *Intensive Care Med* 2026.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W01-D5 · Corticosteroids and adjuncts in septic shock

**1.** A 66-year-old woman with faecal peritonitis has had source control. Despite adequate fluids she has needed noradrenaline 0.35 µg/kg/min for 5 hours to keep MAP 66 mmHg. Regarding corticosteroids:

- A. Hydrocortisone 200 mg/day is indicated. — T / F
- B. A short Synacthen (ACTH) test should be done first to select patients who will benefit. — T / F
- C. In APROCCHSS, hydrocortisone plus fludrocortisone reduced 90-day mortality. — T / F
- D. In ADRENAL, hydrocortisone reduced 90-day mortality. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – SSC: septic shock with NA/adrenaline ≥ 0.25 µg/kg/min for ≥ 4 h → IV hydrocortisone 200 mg/day.
- B (False) – No ACTH test – it does not identify responders.
- C (True) – APROCCHSS (with fludrocortisone 50 µg) – lower 90-day mortality.
- D (False) – ADRENAL – no mortality difference, but faster shock resolution and shorter ventilation.

**EDIC trap:** ADRENAL = faster shock reversal, not lower mortality.

**Ref:** Venkatesh B, et al. *N Engl J Med* 2018;378:797–808; Annane D, et al. *N Engl J Med* 2018;378:809–818.

</details>

**2.** A 58-year-old man with severe community-acquired pneumonia (no influenza) is admitted to the ICU on high-flow nasal oxygen, FiO₂ 0.6. He needs no vasopressors. Which statement about corticosteroids is best supported?

- A. Corticosteroids are contraindicated because he is not in shock
- B. Hydrocortisone 200 mg/day can be considered; in CAPE COD it reduced 28-day mortality in severe CAP without shock
- C. Give fludrocortisone alone
- D. Measure a random cortisol and treat only if it is < 10 µg/dL
- E. Give methylprednisolone 1 g/day for 3 days

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

CAPE COD (severe CAP in ICU, septic shock and influenza excluded) – hydrocortisone reduced 28-day mortality. Random cortisol does not select patients; pulse methylprednisolone is not the studied regimen.

**EDIC trap:** Corticosteroids are for septic **shock** (NA ≥ 0.25 for ≥ 4 h) and for severe CAP **without** shock – not for sepsis without shock in general.

**Ref:** Dequin PF, et al. CAPE COD. *N Engl J Med* 2023;388:1931–1941.

</details>

**3.** Regarding adjunctive therapies in septic shock (SSC 2026):

- A. High-dose IV vitamin C is suggested. — T / F
- B. Sodium bicarbonate is suggested for patients with pH ≤ 7.2 and AKI stage 2–3. — T / F
- C. Sodium bicarbonate is suggested to improve haemodynamics in hypoperfusion-induced lactic acidaemia. — T / F
- D. Intravenous immunoglobulins are suggested in adult septic shock. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – F, B – T, C – F, D – F.**

- A (False) – Suggested against (LOVIT – harm signal).
- B (True) – BICAR-ICU subgroup – fewer deaths and less RRT with pH ≤ 7.2 and AKI 2–3.
- C (False) – Not to improve haemodynamics or reduce vasopressor dose.
- D (False) – Suggested against.

**EDIC trap:** Bicarbonate only for pH ≤ 7.2 **with** AKI 2–3 – never "for the blood pressure".

**Ref:** Surviving Sepsis Campaign 2026; Jaber S, et al. BICAR-ICU. *Lancet* 2018;392:31–40.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W02-D1 · Antimicrobial dosing and microbiology basics

**1.** A 76-year-old man with an estimated creatinine clearance of 40 mL/min (CKD stage 3b) is admitted with sepsis. Regarding his antimicrobial dosing:

- A. Linezolid requires no dose reduction for his renal function. — T / F
- B. Ceftriaxone requires no dose adjustment at this level of renal function. — T / F
- C. If gentamicin is used, the dosing interval should be extended and guided by drug levels. — T / F
- D. Flucloxacillin must be halved at this creatinine clearance. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – linezolid is cleared mainly by non-renal oxidation; metabolites accumulate in severe CKD, but the dose is unchanged.
- B (True) – ceftriaxone has dual biliary and renal elimination and needs no adjustment unless hepatic and renal failure coexist.
- C (True) – aminoglycosides are renally cleared and nephrotoxic: give a full weight-based dose and extend the interval according to trough levels.
- D (False) – flucloxacillin needs reduction only in severe impairment (CrCl < 10 mL/min).

**EDIC trap:** in sepsis the first (loading) dose of any hydrophilic agent is never reduced, because Vd is increased.

**Ref:** Roberts JA et al., Lancet Infect Dis 2014;14:498–509; Abdul-Aziz MH et al., Intensive Care Med 2020;46:1127–1153.

</details>

**2.** Regarding Gram-stain classification of clinically important organisms:

- A. Enterococcus faecium is a Gram-positive coccus. — T / F
- B. Corynebacterium diphtheriae is a Gram-positive rod. — T / F
- C. Neisseria meningitidis is a Gram-positive diplococcus. — T / F
- D. Legionella pneumophila is a Gram-positive rod. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – F.**

- A (True) – enterococci are Gram-positive cocci in pairs/chains, intrinsically resistant to cephalosporins.
- B (True) – corynebacteria are Gram-positive "club-shaped" rods.
- C (False) – *Neisseria* species are Gram-negative diplococci.
- D (False) – *Legionella* is a Gram-negative rod that stains poorly; diagnosis is by urinary antigen (serogroup 1) or PCR rather than Gram stain.

**Ref:** Murray PR, Rosenthal KS, Pfaller MA. *Medical Microbiology*, 9th ed. Elsevier 2020; Martin-Loeches I et al., ERS/ESICM/ESCMID/ALAT severe CAP guidelines, Intensive Care Med 2023;49:615–632.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W02-D2 · De-escalation, duration of therapy and procalcitonin

**1.** A 70-year-old ventilated man developed VAP. BAL grew *Klebsiella pneumoniae* susceptible to ceftriaxone. On day 3 of meropenem plus amikacin he is afebrile and his oxygenation is improving.

- A. Switching to ceftriaxone monotherapy is de-escalation. — T / F
- B. A total course of 8 days is reasonable. — T / F
- C. In PneumA, 8 days of treatment increased mortality compared with 15 days. — T / F
- D. A procalcitonin fall of ≥ 80 % from its peak supports stopping antibiotics. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – T.**

- A (True) – Narrower spectrum and fewer drugs = de-escalation (SSC 2026: strong recommendation to reassess and de-escalate daily).
- B (True) – PneumA – 8 days as good as 15 days for mortality.
- C (False) – No mortality difference; only recurrence was higher with non-fermenting Gram-negatives.
- D (True) – Stopping rule: PCT < 0.5 µg/L or ≥ 80 % fall from peak.

**EDIC trap:** The non-fermenter recurrence signal in PneumA applies to *Pseudomonas*/*Acinetobacter*, not to *Klebsiella*.

**Ref:** Chastre J, et al. PneumA. *JAMA* 2003;290:2588–2598.

</details>

**2.** A 64-year-old woman has *E. coli* bacteraemia from obstructive pyelonephritis. The obstruction was drained, she is afebrile by day 4 and not immunosuppressed. What is the shortest total duration of antibiotics supported by trial evidence?

- A. 3 days
- B. 5 days
- C. 7 days
- D. 14 days
- E. Until CRP has normalised

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.**

BALANCE: 7 days was non-inferior to 14 days for 90-day mortality (14.5 vs 16.1 %) in bloodstream infection; *S. aureus* and candidaemia were excluded. CRP-guided duration is not supported (ADAPT-Sepsis).

**EDIC trap:** 7 days does **not** apply to *S. aureus* bacteraemia (≥ 14 days).

**Ref:** BALANCE Investigators. *N Engl J Med* 2025.

</details>

**3.** Regarding biomarker-guided antibiotic therapy:

- A. Procalcitonin is best used to decide when to stop antibiotics rather than whether to start them in suspected septic shock. — T / F
- B. In ADAPT-Sepsis, CRP-guided stopping shortened antibiotic duration as well as PCT-guided stopping. — T / F
- C. In PRORATA, PCT guidance gave more antibiotic-free days without a significant increase in mortality. — T / F
- D. In SAPS, PCT guidance shortened antibiotic duration and was associated with lower mortality. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – T, D – T.**

- A (True) – Do not delay antibiotics waiting for PCT in septic shock.
- B (False) – Only PCT reduced duration; CRP did not.
- C (True) – PRORATA – +2.7 antibiotic-free days, mortality non-inferior.
- D (True) – SAPS – shorter duration and lower mortality.

**EDIC trap:** PCT is a **stopping** tool; CRP is not.

**Ref:** Bouadma L, et al. PRORATA. *Lancet* 2010; de Jong E, et al. SAPS. *Lancet Infect Dis* 2016; ADAPT-Sepsis. *JAMA* 2025.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W02-D3 · Multidrug resistance and anti-MRSA therapy

**1.** Regarding antimicrobial resistance and MRSA treatment:

- A. Extensively drug-resistant (XDR) means non-susceptibility to at least one agent in all but two or fewer antimicrobial categories. — T / F
- B. Daptomycin is an appropriate first-line agent for MRSA pneumonia. — T / F
- C. Ceftaroline is a β-lactam with activity against MRSA. — T / F
- D. Oral vancomycin is well absorbed and can be used to complete treatment of MRSA bacteraemia. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – international consensus definitions: MDR = non-susceptible to ≥ 1 agent in ≥ 3 categories; XDR = non-susceptible to ≥ 1 agent in all but ≤ 2 categories; PDR = non-susceptible to all agents in all categories.
- B (False) – daptomycin is inactivated by pulmonary surfactant and must not be used for pneumonia; use linezolid or vancomycin.
- C (True) – ceftaroline (and ceftobiprole) bind PBP2a and are the only β-lactams active against MRSA.
- D (False) – oral vancomycin is essentially not absorbed (used only for *C. difficile*); linezolid, by contrast, has ~100 % oral bioavailability.

**Ref:** Magiorakos AP et al., Clin Microbiol Infect 2012;18:268–281; Silverman JA et al., J Infect Dis 2005;191:2149–2152.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W02-D4 · Candidaemia and aspergillosis in the ICU

**1.** A 62-year-old man, 9 days after bowel surgery and with a central venous catheter, has *Candida albicans* in two blood cultures.

- A. An echinocandin is the first-line treatment. — T / F
- B. The central venous catheter should be removed. — T / F
- C. An ophthalmological examination should be done within the first week. — T / F
- D. Treatment should last 14 days from the first positive blood culture. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – Echinocandin first line; step down to fluconazole after 5–7 days if stable, cultures negative and the isolate is susceptible.
- B (True) – Remove the CVC.
- C (True) – Look for endophthalmitis within the first week.
- D (False) – 14 days from the **first negative** blood culture (clearance), not from the first positive.

**EDIC trap:** Duration counts from blood-culture clearance.

**Ref:** Pappas PG, et al. IDSA candidiasis guideline. *Clin Infect Dis* 2016;62:e1–e50.

</details>

**2.** A 55-year-old ventilated man is being investigated for VAP. Endotracheal aspirate grows *Candida albicans* (moderate growth) and *Staphylococcus aureus*. He has no other risk factors for invasive candidiasis and blood cultures are negative. Regarding *Candida*:

- A. Start fluconazole
- B. Start an echinocandin
- C. No antifungal treatment – this is colonisation
- D. Start liposomal amphotericin B
- E. Treat if serum β-D-glucan is positive in 1 hour

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.**

*Candida* in airway secretions is almost always colonisation; *Candida* pneumonia is exceptionally rare. Treat the bacterial VAP.

**EDIC trap:** *Candida* in the airway ≠ *Candida* pneumonia.

**Ref:** Pappas PG, et al. *Clin Infect Dis* 2016.

</details>

**3.** A 48-year-old woman with severe influenza A pneumonia on invasive ventilation has BAL galactomannan 1.4 (ODI) on day 3 in the ICU. She has no classical host factors.

- A. A diagnosis of influenza-associated pulmonary aspergillosis (IAPA) requires classical host factors (e.g. neutropenia, transplant). — T / F
- B. BAL galactomannan ≥ 1.0 supports a diagnosis of probable aspergillosis in this setting. — T / F
- C. Voriconazole or isavuconazole is first-line treatment. — T / F
- D. *Aspergillus* in BAL in influenza ICU patients is almost always colonisation and need not be treated. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – F, B – T, C – T, D – F.**

- A (False) – The IAPA definition does not require host factors (~19 % of ICU influenza patients develop IAPA).
- B (True) – BAL GM ≥ 1.0 (or serum GM > 0.5, or positive BAL culture) = probable.
- C (True) – Voriconazole/isavuconazole first line; liposomal amphotericin B if azole resistance.
- D (False) – IAPA carries high mortality and should be treated.

**EDIC trap:** Severe influenza is itself a risk factor – no host factors needed.

**Ref:** Verweij PE, et al. IAPA consensus. *Intensive Care Med* 2020;46:1524–1535.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W02-D5 · Ventilator-associated pneumonia

**1.** Regarding ventilator-associated pneumonia (VAP):

- A. A pneumonia first appearing 24 hours after intubation meets the usual definition of VAP. — T / F
- B. Subglottic secretion drainage reduces the incidence of VAP. — T / F
- C. Scheduled routine changes of the ventilator circuit reduce VAP. — T / F
- D. In a patient without risk factors for resistance, early-onset VAP is more likely than late-onset VAP to be caused by S. pneumoniae, H. influenzae or MSSA. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – F, B – T, C – F, D – T.**

- A (False) – VAP requires > 48 h of invasive ventilation (ATS/IDSA 2016; ERS/ESICM/ESCMID/ALAT 2017).
- B (True) – meta-analyses show fewer VAP episodes with subglottic secretion drainage, without a clear mortality effect.
- C (False) – circuits should be changed only when visibly soiled or malfunctioning; routine changes do not reduce VAP.
- D (True) – early-onset VAP (< 5 days) more often involves community flora; late onset and prior antibiotics increase the risk of *Pseudomonas*, *Acinetobacter*, ESBL Enterobacterales and MRSA.

**Ref:** Kalil AC et al., ATS/IDSA HAP/VAP guidelines, Clin Infect Dis 2016;63:e61–e111; Torres A et al., Eur Respir J 2017;50:1700582.

</details>

**2.** A 48-year-old man has been ventilated for 5 days after a motorcycle collision with pulmonary contusions that had been resolving. Today he has a temperature of 38.9 °C, purulent tracheal secretions, a WBC of 17 × 10⁹/L and a new right lower-lobe consolidation on chest X-ray. His PaO2/FiO2 has fallen from 280 to 170 mmHg. Echocardiography shows normal LV function. Which is the most likely diagnosis?

- A. Ventilator-associated tracheobronchitis
- B. Acute pulmonary embolism
- C. Ventilator-associated pneumonia
- D. Lobar atelectasis from a mucus plug
- E. Worsening of the pulmonary contusions

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.**

**Why C:** A new, persistent infiltrate after > 48 h of ventilation plus fever, purulent secretions, leucocytosis and worsening oxygenation is clinical VAP; his CPIS would exceed 6. CPIS has only moderate sensitivity and specificity, so guidelines rely on clinical diagnosis supported by (semi-)quantitative respiratory cultures rather than CPIS alone.

**Why not the others:**
- A – ventilator-associated tracheobronchitis has no new infiltrate.
- B – pulmonary embolism does not cause purulent secretions or a new consolidation with leucocytosis.
- D – atelectasis from a mucus plug does not explain fever and purulence.
- E – contusions appear within 24 h of injury and resolve over days; these had been resolving.

**Ref:** Kalil AC et al., ATS/IDSA HAP/VAP guidelines, Clin Infect Dis 2016;63:e61–e111; Torres A et al., Eur Respir J 2017;50:1700582.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W03-D1 · CRBSI/CLABSI

**1.** A ventilated patient with a 9-day-old jugular CVC develops fever. Paired blood cultures grow *Enterobacter cloacae*: the CVC sample becomes positive after 10 h, the peripheral sample after 13 h. No other source is found.

- A. This is a CLABSI, but CRBSI cannot be diagnosed without culturing the catheter tip
- B. CRBSI is confirmed: the differential time to positivity is ≥ 2 h
- C. The result most likely represents contamination
- D. CRBSI requires a differential time to positivity of ≥ 5 h
- E. CRBSI is excluded because the peripheral culture is also positive

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

DTP ≥ 2 h (CVC positive earlier than peripheral) proves the catheter is the source = CRBSI (alternatives: tip ≥ 15 CFU, quantitative ratio ≥ 3:1). CLABSI is the surveillance definition (CVC > 2 days, no other source).

**EDIC trap:** CLABSI = surveillance; CRBSI = proof the catheter is the source.

**Ref:** Mermel LA, et al. IDSA CRBSI guideline. *Clin Infect Dis* 2009;49:1–45.

</details>

**2.** A 60-year-old woman has *Staphylococcus aureus* (MSSA) bacteraemia from a 6-day-old jugular CVC.

- A. The catheter must be removed. — T / F
- B. She needs at least 14 days of IV therapy after the first negative blood culture. — T / F
- C. Transoesophageal echocardiography should be performed. — T / F
- D. Antibiotic lock therapy with catheter retention is an acceptable alternative. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – *S. aureus* = always remove.
- B (True) – ≥ 14 days for uncomplicated infection, longer if complicated.
- C (True) – TEE to exclude endocarditis.
- D (False) – Lock therapy/retention is not acceptable for *S. aureus*.

**EDIC trap:** *S. aureus*: remove, ≥ 14 days, TEE.

**Ref:** Mermel LA, et al. *Clin Infect Dis* 2009.

</details>

**3.** Regarding the prevention of CVC infections:

- A. Chlorhexidine–alcohol skin antisepsis is superior to povidone-iodine–alcohol. — T / F
- B. The subclavian site has the lowest rates of infection and thrombosis but more pneumothorax. — T / F
- C. Transparent dressings must be changed every 48 hours. — T / F
- D. The femoral site is preferred to reduce infection risk. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – F.**

- A (True) – CLEAN – HR 0.21 for CRBSI.
- B (True) – 3SITES.
- C (False) – Transparent/CHG dressings every 7 days (or if soiled or loose).
- D (False) – Avoid femoral when possible.

**EDIC trap:** Fewest infections = subclavian; most = femoral.

**Ref:** Mimoz O, et al. CLEAN. *Lancet* 2015; Parienti JJ, et al. 3SITES. *N Engl J Med* 2015.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W03-D2 · CNS infection

**1.** A 44-year-old woman presents with 2 days of fever, word-finding difficulty and a focal seizure. Herpes simplex encephalitis is suspected. Regarding her management:

- A. IV aciclovir should be started before MRI or lumbar puncture results are available. — T / F
- B. A CSF HSV PCR obtained within the first 72 hours can be falsely negative, so a repeat sample is advised if suspicion persists. — T / F
- C. CT is more sensitive than MRI for early temporal lobe changes. — T / F
- D. The usual aciclovir dose with normal renal function is 10 mg/kg IV every 8 hours. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – T.**

- A (True) – Delay in aciclovir is the main modifiable determinant of poor outcome; start it empirically within hours of presentation, before MRI or LP results.
- B (True) – CSF HSV PCR in the first 72 h may be falsely negative; repeat 3–7 days later if HSE remains likely, continuing aciclovir meanwhile.
- C (False) – **MRI** (FLAIR/DWI hyperintensity of the medial temporal lobes and insula) is far more sensitive than CT, which is often normal early.
- D (True) – 10 mg/kg IV 8-hourly for 14–21 days, reduced in renal impairment; ensure hydration to avoid crystal nephropathy.

**EDIC trap:** A negative day-1 PCR never justifies stopping aciclovir when the clinical picture fits.

**Ref:** Solomon et al., ABN/BIA viral encephalitis guidelines, J Infect 2012; Tunkel et al., IDSA encephalitis guidelines, Clin Infect Dis 2008.

</details>

**2.** A 63-year-old woman with a renal transplant, on tacrolimus and prednisolone, presents with 3 days of fever, headache, unsteadiness and new diplopia with a right facial palsy. CSF: WBC 450/µL (60 % neutrophils), protein 1.4 g/L, CSF/serum glucose ratio 0.35; Gram stain shows no organisms. Which empirical regimen should be started, together with dexamethasone?

- A. Ceftriaxone plus vancomycin
- B. Ceftriaxone plus ciprofloxacin
- C. Ceftriaxone alone
- D. Ceftriaxone plus high-dose amoxicillin
- E. Liposomal amphotericin plus flucytosine

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.**

**Why D:** Immunosuppression (and age > 50) is a risk factor for *Listeria monocytogenes*, which typically causes rhombencephalitis – brainstem signs, cranial nerve palsies, ataxia – and the Gram stain is often negative or misread. Cephalosporins have no activity against *Listeria*, so high-dose amoxicillin/ampicillin must be added to a third-generation cephalosporin (co-trimoxazole if severe penicillin allergy).

**Why not the others:**

- A – Vancomycin covers penicillin-resistant pneumococci but not *Listeria*.
- B – Ciprofloxacin is not a recommended anti-*Listeria* agent for meningitis.
- C – Ceftriaxone alone leaves *Listeria* uncovered.
- E – Amphotericin + flucytosine fits cryptococcal meningitis (lymphocytic CSF, raised opening pressure), not this neutrophilic picture.

**EDIC trap:** *Listeria* meningitis is treated for ≥ 21 days.

**Ref:** van de Beek et al., ESCMID guideline, Clin Microbiol Infect 2016; Charlier et al., MONALISA, Lancet Infect Dis 2017.

</details>

**3.** A 36-year-old man with newly diagnosed HIV (CD4 90 cells/µL) has a 3-week history of headache, night sweats and weight loss, and now a left sixth nerve palsy. Sodium is 127 mmol/L. CSF: lymphocytes 220/µL, protein 2.1 g/L, CSF/serum glucose ratio 0.3; Gram stain and cryptococcal antigen are negative. MRI shows basal meningeal enhancement and mild hydrocephalus. Which test is most likely to confirm the diagnosis rapidly?

- A. CSF India ink stain
- B. Serum Toxoplasma IgG
- C. CSF VDRL
- D. CSF cytology for lymphoma
- E. Xpert MTB/RIF Ultra on a large-volume CSF sample

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**E.**

**Why E:** Subacute lymphocytic meningitis with very high protein, low glucose, basal meningeal enhancement, hydrocephalus, sixth nerve palsy and hyponatraemia in advanced HIV points to tuberculous meningitis. WHO recommends Xpert MTB/RIF Ultra as the initial CSF test (sensitivity improves with ≥ 6 mL of CSF); a negative result does not exclude TBM.

**Why not the others:**

- A – India ink is less sensitive than cryptococcal antigen, which is already negative.
- B – Serum *Toxoplasma* IgG shows prior exposure and does not diagnose meningitis; toxoplasmosis causes ring-enhancing lesions.
- C – CSF VDRL tests for neurosyphilis, which does not explain this subacute basal picture with hydrocephalus.
- D – CSF cytology for lymphoma has low sensitivity and is not the most likely diagnosis.

**EDIC trap:** In TBM, ART is deferred ~4–8 weeks (early ART increased severe adverse events), and adjunctive dexamethasone did not improve survival in HIV-positive adults (ACT HIV).

**Ref:** WHO consolidated TB guidelines, Module 3: diagnosis, 2024; Donovan et al., ACT HIV, N Engl J Med 2023.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W03-D3 · Necrotising soft-tissue infection and toxic shock

**1.** A 61-year-old man with diabetes presents with severe thigh pain, fever and hypotension 2 days after a minor abrasion. The overlying skin shows only mild erythema. Regarding suspected necrotising fasciitis:

- A. Surgical exploration should not wait for cross-sectional imaging when clinical suspicion is high. — T / F
- B. Crepitus is present in most patients at presentation. — T / F
- C. Clindamycin is added to broad-spectrum therapy mainly for suppression of toxin production. — T / F
- D. Near-normal overlying skin makes necrotising fasciitis unlikely. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – Delay to debridement is the strongest modifiable predictor of death; imaging must not delay surgery in an unstable patient.
- B (False) – Crepitus and gas are **late** signs present in a minority – specific but insensitive.
- C (True) – Clindamycin inhibits ribosomal toxin synthesis and is unaffected by the inoculum (Eagle) effect; it is combined with a broad-spectrum β-lactam (piperacillin–tazobactam or a carbapenem) ± MRSA cover.
- D (False) – Infection spreads along the fascia beneath initially normal-looking skin; **pain out of proportion** to findings is the classic early clue.

**EDIC trap:** Hypotension plus disproportionate pain after minor trauma = NSTI until surgical exploration proves otherwise.

**Ref:** Stevens et al., IDSA SSTI guidelines, Clin Infect Dis 2014; Sartelli et al., WSES/SIS-E consensus, World J Emerg Surg 2022.

</details>

**2.** Regarding toxic shock syndromes (TSS):

- A. Blood cultures are positive more often in streptococcal than in staphylococcal TSS. — T / F
- B. Desquamation, particularly of the palms and soles, typically appears 1–2 weeks after onset. — T / F
- C. TSST-1 acts as a superantigen, cross-linking MHC class II molecules and T-cell receptors outside the antigen-binding groove. — T / F
- D. Positive blood cultures for S. aureus are required for the diagnosis of staphylococcal TSS. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – Streptococcal TSS is bacteraemic in ~60% of cases, staphylococcal TSS in < 5%.
- B (True) – Late desquamation (palms and soles, 1–2 weeks after onset) is a CDC criterion for confirmed staphylococcal TSS.
- C (True) – TSST-1 is a superantigen that cross-links MHC class II and the TCR Vβ chain outside the antigen-binding groove, activating up to 20% of T cells non-specifically and producing a cytokine storm.
- D (False) – Staphylococcal TSS is toxin-mediated; the CDC definition requires fever, rash, hypotension, ≥ 3 organ systems and negative tests for alternatives – **not** positive *S. aureus* blood cultures.

**EDIC trap:** Negative blood cultures in a shocked woman with erythroderma favour staphylococcal TSS.

**Ref:** Lappin & Ferguson, Lancet Infect Dis 2009; CDC TSS (other than streptococcal) case definition 2011.

</details>

**3.** Regarding the classification and assessment of necrotising soft-tissue infection (NSTI):

- A. Type 1 NSTI is polymicrobial with anaerobes and is typical of perineal (Fournier's) infection in diabetic patients. — T / F
- B. Type 2 NSTI is most commonly caused by Clostridium perfringens. — T / F
- C. A LRINEC score below 6 reliably excludes NSTI. — T / F
- D. NSTI due to Vibrio vulnificus is associated with seawater exposure and chronic liver disease. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – T.**

- A (True) – Type 1 (the most common) is polymicrobial – anaerobes, Enterobacterales, streptococci – and typical of Fournier's gangrene in diabetics.
- B (False) – Type 2 is monomicrobial, usually **group A streptococcus** (± *S. aureus*); clostridial myonecrosis is classed separately as gas gangrene (or with type 1).
- C (False) – LRINEC has poor sensitivity (~68% in meta-analysis) and must **not** be used to rule out NSTI.
- D (True) – *Vibrio vulnificus* (type 3, with *Aeromonas*) is linked to marine wounds, raw shellfish and cirrhosis; type 4 is fungal.

**EDIC trap:** Type 2 GAS NSTI affects healthy people of any age, often without a portal of entry.

**Ref:** Fernando et al., Ann Surg 2019; Stevens & Bryant, N Engl J Med 2017.

</details>

**4.** Concerning the diagnosis and treatment of necrotising fasciitis:

- A. Gas in the soft tissues on CT is required to make the diagnosis. — T / F
- B. Antibiotics alone are adequate for early type 1 infection in a haemodynamically stable patient. — T / F
- C. Pain is usually proportionate to the visible skin changes. — T / F
- D. Necrosis of muscle with intact fascia is the defining lesion. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – F, B – F, C – F, D – F.**

- A (False) – Gas is absent in many cases; CT findings (fascial thickening, fluid) support but cannot exclude the diagnosis. Surgical exploration ("finger test": easy dissection, dishwater fluid, no bleeding) is diagnostic.
- B (False) – Surgical debridement is the cornerstone regardless of type or stability; antibiotics are adjunctive.
- C (False) – Pain is classically **out of proportion** to the visible skin changes.
- D (False) – Necrotising fasciitis is defined by necrosis of the superficial/deep **fascia**; myonecrosis may coexist but does not define it.

**EDIC trap:** Plan a second-look operation within 24 h.

**Ref:** Stevens & Bryant, N Engl J Med 2017; Sartelli et al., WSES/SIS-E consensus, World J Emerg Surg 2022.

</details>

**5.** A 24-year-old woman presents 3 days after septorhinoplasty with nasal packing in situ. She has a temperature of 39.6 °C, diffuse macular erythroderma, BP 78/40 mmHg, diarrhoea, confusion, creatine kinase 2400 U/L, creatinine 190 µmol/L and platelets 85 × 10⁹/L. Her conjunctivae are injected. Alongside fluid resuscitation, which is the most appropriate immediate step?

- A. IV immunoglobulin as the sole specific treatment
- B. Ceftriaxone, leaving the packing in place to protect the surgical result
- C. CT of the sinuses before any antibiotic is given
- D. Remove the nasal packing and give an anti-staphylococcal β-lactam plus clindamycin
- E. Meropenem monotherapy

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.**

**Why D:** Fever, erythroderma, shock and multi-organ involvement (GI, CNS, muscle, platelets, kidney) after nasal packing is staphylococcal toxic shock syndrome. Priorities: remove the toxin source and give a bactericidal anti-staphylococcal agent (flucloxacillin/cefazolin; vancomycin or linezolid if MRSA risk) plus a protein-synthesis inhibitor (clindamycin or linezolid) to suppress toxin production.

**Why not the others:**

- A – IVIG is an adjunct in refractory cases, not a substitute for source control and antibiotics.
- B – Leaving the packing leaves the toxin source in place; ceftriaxone also lacks an antitoxin effect.
- C – Delaying antibiotics for imaging is wrong in shock.
- E – Meropenem alone lacks an antitoxin effect and is unnecessarily broad.

**EDIC trap:** Non-menstrual TSS follows nasal packing, surgical wounds, burns.

**Ref:** Lappin & Ferguson, Lancet Infect Dis 2009; Stevens et al., IDSA SSTI guidelines, Clin Infect Dis 2014.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W03-D4 · Immunocompromised patient: neutropenia and HIV

**1.** A 49-year-old woman on chemotherapy for breast cancer presents with a temperature of 38.6 °C, BP 84/50 mmHg and a neutrophil count of 0.2 × 10⁹/L. She has a tunnelled central venous catheter without signs of exit-site infection. Regarding her initial management:

- A. An empirical antipseudomonal β-lactam should be given within 1 hour. — T / F
- B. Febrile neutropenia is defined using a neutrophil count < 0.5 × 10⁹/L, or one expected to fall below this. — T / F
- C. Vancomycin should be added routinely because she has a central venous catheter. — T / F
- D. Given her septic shock, adding an aminoglycoside (or escalating to a carbapenem, depending on local resistance) is reasonable. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – T.**

- A (True) – An antipseudomonal β-lactam (piperacillin–tazobactam, cefepime or meropenem) within 1 h; delays increase mortality.
- B (True) – Standard ESMO/IDSA definition: ANC < 0.5 × 10⁹/L or expected to fall below this within 48 h, with fever ≥ 38.3 °C (or ≥ 38.0 °C for 1 h).
- C (False) – Glycopeptides are added only for specific indications (suspected serious catheter-related infection, skin/soft-tissue infection, pneumonia, MRSA colonisation, haemodynamic instability) – not for the mere presence of a CVC.
- D (True) – Septic shock is a complicated presentation: ECIL-4 recommends a **de-escalation** approach (carbapenem, or β-lactam + aminoglycoside, ± anti-MRSA cover), narrowing at 72–96 h.

**EDIC trap:** The line need not be removed immediately unless tunnel/pocket infection or *S. aureus*, *Pseudomonas* or *Candida* bacteraemia.

**Ref:** Averbuch et al., ECIL-4, Haematologica 2013; Klastersky et al., ESMO guideline, Ann Oncol 2016.

</details>

**2.** A 41-year-old woman is newly diagnosed with HIV infection after presenting with weight loss and painful swallowing; her CD4 count is 260 cells/µL and HIV viral load 85 000 copies/mL. She has not yet started antiretroviral therapy, and further investigations are under way. Which one of the following conditions, if present, would by itself establish a diagnosis of AIDS?

- A. Multidermatomal herpes zoster
- B. Oral hairy leukoplakia
- C. Cervical intraepithelial neoplasia grade 2
- D. Oesophageal candidiasis
- E. Idiopathic thrombocytopenic purpura

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.**

**Why D:** With a CD4 count > 200 cells/µL, AIDS (CDC stage 3) is defined by an AIDS-defining illness. Candidiasis of the oesophagus, trachea, bronchi or lungs is AIDS-defining; oral candidiasis is not. Odynophagia and weight loss fit oesophageal candidiasis.

**Why not the others:**

- A – Multidermatomal herpes zoster is a "category B" indicator condition, not AIDS-defining.
- B – Oral hairy leukoplakia is a category B condition.
- C – Cervical dysplasia/carcinoma in situ is category B; only invasive cervical carcinoma is AIDS-defining.
- E – ITP is an HIV-associated condition but not AIDS-defining.

**EDIC trap:** AIDS-defining conditions relevant to ICU include *Pneumocystis* pneumonia, cerebral toxoplasmosis, cryptococcal meningitis, tuberculosis, recurrent bacterial pneumonia and CMV retinitis.

**Ref:** Selik et al., CDC revised surveillance case definition, MMWR Recomm Rep 2014.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W03-D5 · Infection prevention and control

**1.** Regarding selective digestive decontamination (SDD) in ventilated ICU patients:

- A. It aims to eradicate carriage of aerobic Gram-negative bacilli, S. aureus and yeasts while preserving the anaerobic flora. — T / F
- B. It uses non-absorbable antimicrobials applied as an oropharyngeal paste and an enteral suspension. — T / F
- C. SuDDICU (JAMA 2022) showed a significant reduction in hospital mortality. — T / F
- D. Enteral vancomycin is a routine component in all ICUs. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – F.**

- A (True) – SDD targets aerobic Gram-negative bacilli, *S. aureus* and yeasts while sparing anaerobes, which maintain colonisation resistance.
- B (True) – Non-absorbable topical agents (classically colistin, tobramycin and amphotericin B/nystatin) are given as oropharyngeal paste and enteral suspension throughout ventilation, plus a short IV cephalosporin course.
- C (False) – SuDDICU (5982 patients, 19 Australian ICUs) found hospital mortality 27.0% vs 29.1%, OR 0.91 (0.82–1.02) – **not significant**.
- D (False) – Enteral vancomycin is added only in units with endemic MRSA, not routinely.

**EDIC trap:** The pooled evidence (Hammond 2022) suggests a small benefit that depends on the IV component.

**Ref:** Myburgh et al., SuDDICU, JAMA 2022; Hammond et al., JAMA 2022.

</details>

**2.** Regarding hand hygiene, decontamination of equipment and central-line infection programmes:

- A. "Before an aseptic procedure" and "after body fluid exposure risk" are two of the WHO five moments for hand hygiene. — T / F
- B. In the Spaulding classification, items that enter sterile tissue or the vascular system require sterilisation. — T / F
- C. An alcohol-based hand rub is preferred to soap and water when hands are visibly soiled. — T / F
- D. The Matching Michigan programme in English ICUs targeted central venous catheter–associated bloodstream infection. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – T.**

- A (True) – The WHO five moments: before touching a patient, before a clean/aseptic procedure, after body fluid exposure risk, after touching a patient, after touching patient surroundings.
- B (True) – Spaulding: critical items (sterile tissue, vascular system) → sterilisation; semi-critical (mucous membranes, e.g. laryngoscope blades, bronchoscopes) → high-level disinfection; non-critical (intact skin) → low-level disinfection/cleaning.
- C (False) – Visibly soiled hands (and *C. difficile*/norovirus exposure) require **soap and water**; alcohol rub is preferred otherwise.
- D (True) – Matching Michigan adapted the Keystone CVC bundle to 215 English ICUs; CVC-BSI rates fell, partly in line with a pre-existing downward trend.

**EDIC trap:** Alcohol does not kill *C. difficile* spores.

**Ref:** WHO guidelines on hand hygiene in health care, 2009; Bion et al., Matching Michigan, BMJ Qual Saf 2013.

</details>

**3.** Under the Health Protection (Notification) Regulations 2010 in England, which of the following must be notified by the attending clinician on clinical suspicion?

- A. Legionnaires' disease — T / F
- B. Botulism — T / F
- C. Uncomplicated seasonal influenza A in an adult — T / F
- D. Invasive group A streptococcal disease — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – T.**

- A (True) – Legionnaires' disease is listed in Schedule 1.
- B (True) – Botulism is listed in Schedule 1.
- C (False) – Seasonal influenza is monitored through laboratory surveillance and is **not** clinician-notifiable.
- D (True) – Invasive group A streptococcal disease (and scarlet fever) is notifiable.

Schedule 1 also includes acute meningitis, acute encephalitis, meningococcal septicaemia, tuberculosis, tetanus, HUS, measles, diphtheria and viral haemorrhagic fevers. Notification is on **clinical suspicion**, without waiting for laboratory confirmation. Other European countries have analogous national lists under the EU decision on communicable disease surveillance.

**Ref:** Health Protection (Notification) Regulations 2010, SI 2010/659 (as amended); UKHSA, Notifiable diseases and causative organisms: how to report, 2024.

</details>

**4.** Regarding prevention of device-associated infection in the ICU:

- A. 2 % chlorhexidine in 70 % alcohol is preferred to povidone-iodine for skin antisepsis before CVC insertion. — T / F
- B. The subclavian site is associated with a lower risk of catheter-related bloodstream infection than the femoral site. — T / F
- C. Routine replacement of CVCs every 7 days reduces catheter-related bloodstream infection. — T / F
- D. The risk of bacteriuria with an indwelling urinary catheter is roughly 3–7 % per day of catheterisation. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – T.**

- A (True) – In CLEAN, chlorhexidine–alcohol reduced CRBSI by about 80% vs povidone iodine–alcohol (0.28 vs 1.32/1000 catheter-days, HR 0.21).
- B (True) – In 3SITES the subclavian site had the lowest rate of CRBSI + symptomatic DVT (1.5 vs 4.6/1000 catheter-days for femoral), at the cost of more pneumothorax.
- C (False) – Scheduled replacement (over a guidewire or at a new site) does **not** reduce infection and adds mechanical risk; remove catheters when no longer needed.
- D (True) – Bacteriuria risk is roughly 3–7% per catheter-day, which is why daily review and early removal are the key CAUTI-prevention measures.

**EDIC trap:** Keystone's fifth bundle element is removal of unnecessary lines, not routine replacement.

**Ref:** Mimoz et al., CLEAN, Lancet 2015; Parienti et al., 3SITES, N Engl J Med 2015; Gould et al., CDC CAUTI guideline 2009 (updated 2019).

</details>

**5.** Regarding the evidence on selective decontamination strategies:

- A. SDD is primarily intended for patients expected to require mechanical ventilation for more than 48 hours. — T / F
- B. Selective oropharyngeal decontamination (SOD) omits the enteral component of SDD. — T / F
- C. A 2022 systematic review and meta-analysis found SDD to be associated with lower hospital mortality in ventilated patients. — T / F
- D. SDD markedly increases the incidence of Clostridioides difficile infection. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – Trials enrolled patients expected to be ventilated beyond the next day or two (SuDDICU: > 48 h).
- B (True) – SOD uses only the oropharyngeal paste, without the enteral suspension or IV component; de Smet (NEJM 2009) compared SDD, SOD and standard care.
- C (True) – Hammond et al. (32 RCTs, 24,389 patients) estimated hospital mortality RR 0.91 (95% CrI 0.82–0.99), moderate certainty.
- D (False) – *C. difficile* infection is not increased; SuDDICU's ecological analysis showed non-inferiority for *C. difficile*.

**EDIC trap:** SuDDICU itself was neutral, unit-level non-inferiority for resistant organisms was not confirmed, and data from high-resistance settings (R-GNOSIS) are not reassuring.

**Ref:** Hammond et al., JAMA 2022; Myburgh et al., SuDDICU, JAMA 2022; de Smet et al., N Engl J Med 2009.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W04-D1 · Physiology of shock

**1.** A patient has a cardiac output of 5 L/min, haemoglobin 100 g/L (10 g/dL) and SaO₂ 90 %. Ignoring dissolved oxygen, what is his oxygen delivery (DO₂)?

- A. ≈ 300 mL/min
- B. ≈ 450 mL/min
- C. ≈ 600 mL/min
- D. ≈ 800 mL/min
- E. ≈ 1,000 mL/min

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.**

CaO₂ = 1.34 × 10 × 0.90 ≈ 12.1 mL/dL = 121 mL/L. DO₂ = CO × CaO₂ = 5 × 121 ≈ 600 mL/min.

**EDIC trap:** Remember × 10 to convert mL/dL to mL/L.

**Ref:** Vincent JL, De Backer D. Circulatory shock. *N Engl J Med* 2013;369:1726–1734.

</details>

**2.** Regarding venous oxygen saturation and oxygen delivery:

- A. A low SvO₂ is typical of cardiogenic and hypovolaemic shock. — T / F
- B. A normal or high SvO₂ in septic shock confirms adequate tissue oxygenation. — T / F
- C. Below the critical DO₂, VO₂ becomes delivery-dependent and lactate rises. — T / F
- D. At constant VO₂ and cardiac output, anaemia lowers SvO₂. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – T, D – T.**

- A (True) – Low flow → high extraction → low SvO₂.
- B (False) – Normal or high SvO₂ in distributive shock can coexist with tissue hypoxia (impaired extraction, shunting).
- C (True) – Supply dependency below DO₂crit.
- D (True) – Lower CaO₂ → more extraction → lower SvO₂.

**EDIC trap:** A "good" SvO₂ in sepsis is not reassurance.

**Ref:** Cecconi M, et al. ESICM shock consensus. *Intensive Care Med* 2014;40:1795–1815.

</details>

**3.** According to the ESICM 2014 consensus on circulatory shock:

- A. Hypotension is required for the diagnosis of shock. — T / F
- B. Routine use of a pulmonary artery catheter is recommended. — T / F
- C. Echocardiography is the preferred modality to evaluate the type of shock. — T / F
- D. An initial MAP target of ≥ 65 mmHg is recommended, individualised thereafter. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – F, B – F, C – T, D – T.**

- A (False) – Shock = inadequate cellular O₂ utilisation; hypotension is not required.
- B (False) – Recommended **against** routine PAC (1A).
- C (True) – Echo preferred (2B).
- D (True) – Initial MAP ≥ 65, then individualise.

**EDIC trap:** Shock can exist with a normal blood pressure.

**Ref:** Cecconi M, et al. *Intensive Care Med* 2014.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W04-D2 · Fluid responsiveness

**1.** A 71-year-old man on day 2 after laparotomy for faecal peritonitis is ventilated on pressure support 10 cmH2O and PEEP 6 cmH2O, triggering every breath at 24/min. He is in sinus rhythm with MAP 61 mmHg on low-dose noradrenaline. Which finding best indicates that he will increase his cardiac output with a fluid bolus?

- A. Pulse pressure variation of 16 % on the radial arterial line
- B. Central venous pressure of 4 mmHg
- C. Inferior vena cava collapsibility of 50 % on ultrasound
- D. A small left-ventricular end-diastolic area on transthoracic echocardiography
- E. A 15 % rise in left-ventricular outflow tract VTI during passive leg raising

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**E.**

**Why E:** Passive leg raising transfers ~300 mL of venous blood from the legs and splanchnic bed and, when measured with real-time CO or LVOT VTI (threshold ≈ 10 %), predicts fluid responsiveness regardless of spontaneous breathing, arrhythmia or tidal volume (pooled AUROC 0.95). A 15 % rise in VTI is well above the threshold and above the ~12 % precision limit of echo VTI.

**Why not the others:**

- A – PPV is valid only with fully controlled ventilation, VT ≥ 8 mL/kg, sinus rhythm and a closed chest; triggering every breath invalidates it.
- B – CVP is a static measure and predicts responsiveness poorly, even when low.
- C – IVC collapsibility during spontaneous or assisted breathing has only modest accuracy and is confounded by inspiratory effort.
- D – LV end-diastolic area is a static preload index with poor predictive value.

**Ref:** Monnet X, et al. Passive leg raising for predicting fluid responsiveness: a systematic review and meta-analysis. *Intensive Care Med* 2016;42:1935–1947. Monnet X, Shi R, Teboul JL. Prediction of fluid responsiveness. What's new? *Ann Intensive Care* 2022;12:46.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W04-D3 · Haemodynamic monitoring

**1.** Regarding interpretation of the arterial pressure waveform in an ICU patient:

- A. The maximum slope of the systolic upstroke (dP/dt max) from a peripheral line is a rough index of LV contractility but is influenced by arterial compliance. — T / F
- B. A pulse pressure variation of 18 % in a patient ventilated with a tidal volume of 6 mL/kg who is triggering breaths reliably indicates preload responsiveness. — T / F
- C. Systolic pressure measured in the radial artery is normally lower than in the ascending aorta. — T / F
- D. A dicrotic notch positioned low on the descending limb suggests a high systemic vascular resistance. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – F.**

- A (True) – Peripheral dP/dt max tracks LV contractility directionally but is affected by vascular tone, compliance and pulse amplification.
- B (False) – With triggering and VT 6 mL/kg PPV is uninterpretable – irregular efforts can create false positives and low VT false negatives; use PLR or EEO.
- C (False) – Distal pulse amplification by reflected waves makes radial systolic pressure **higher** than aortic; MAP is similar.
- D (False) – A low dicrotic notch suggests vasodilatation and low SVR; a high notch suggests vasoconstriction.

**Ref:** Saugel B, et al. Cardiac output estimation using pulse wave analysis. *Br J Anaesth* 2021;126:67–76. Michard F, et al. Applicability of pulse pressure variation. *Crit Care* 2015;19:144.

</details>

**2.** Concerning variables obtained with a pulmonary artery catheter:

- A. Thermodilution cardiac output is a measured variable, calculated from the area under the temperature–time curve (Stewart–Hamilton equation). — T / F
- B. Pulmonary vascular resistance is calculated from mean pulmonary artery pressure, PAOP and cardiac output. — T / F
- C. Mixed venous oxygen saturation is measured from blood sampled at the catheter tip in the pulmonary artery. — T / F
- D. Oxygen consumption can be derived (reverse Fick) from cardiac output and the arterial and mixed venous oxygen contents. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – T.**

- A (True) – Thermodilution CO is measured with the Stewart–Hamilton equation (CO inversely proportional to the area under the curve); accuracy falls with severe TR, shunts and very low output.
- B (True) – PVR = 80 × (mPAP − PAOP) / CO in dyn·s·cm⁻⁵, or without the factor 80 in Wood units.
- C (True) – SvO2 comes from blood sampled at the PA tip or from fibreoptic oximetry – true mixed venous blood.
- D (True) – Reverse Fick: VO2 = CO × (CaO2 − CvO2) × 10; likewise DO2 = CO × CaO2 × 10.

**EDIC trap:** Measured: pressures, PAOP, thermodilution CO, SvO2. Derived: CI, SV, SVR, PVR, DO2, VO2.

**Ref:** Rajaram SS, et al. Pulmonary artery catheters for adult patients in intensive care. *Cochrane Database Syst Rev* 2013;(2):CD003408. Cecconi M, et al. ESICM consensus on circulatory shock and hemodynamic monitoring. *Intensive Care Med* 2014;40:1795–1815.

</details>

**3.** Regarding oesophageal Doppler monitoring:

- A. It measures blood flow velocity in the descending thoracic aorta and estimates stroke volume using a nomogram-derived aortic cross-sectional area. — T / F
- B. A corrected flow time (FTc) below 330 ms is specific for hypovolaemia. — T / F
- C. Its velocity measurement is accurate regardless of the angle between the beam and the direction of flow. — T / F
- D. It is poorly tolerated by awake patients and is used mainly in sedated, intubated patients. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – T.**

- A (True) – Only descending aortic flow (~70 % of CO) is measured and scaled by a nomogram; altered flow distribution (cross-clamp, pregnancy) causes error.
- B (False) – A short FTc reflects low preload but also high afterload (vasoconstriction, mitral stenosis); interpret it with the SV response to fluid.
- C (False) – The Doppler equation contains cos θ; angles > 20° underestimate velocity, so probe position matters.
- D (True) – It is poorly tolerated awake and suits theatre and sedated, intubated ICU patients.

**Ref:** Singer M. Oesophageal Doppler. *Curr Opin Crit Care* 2009;15:244–248. Cecconi M, et al. ESICM consensus on circulatory shock and hemodynamic monitoring. *Intensive Care Med* 2014;40:1795–1815.

</details>

**4.** A 74-year-old woman with septic shock is ventilated and triggering the ventilator. She is in permanent atrial fibrillation (ventricular rate 110–140/min) and echocardiography shows severe tricuspid regurgitation with a dilated right ventricle. She has received 2 L of crystalloid and remains on noradrenaline 0.3 µg/kg/min. You want to track her response to fluid and dobutamine. Which method is most appropriate?

- A. Serial transthoracic echocardiographic LVOT VTI averaged over several beats
- B. Pulse pressure variation from the radial arterial line
- C. Bolus thermodilution via a pulmonary artery catheter
- D. Trend of central venous pressure
- E. Uncalibrated pulse-contour analysis of beat-to-beat stroke volume variation

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.**

**Why A:** Echocardiographic LVOT VTI (× LVOT area = SV), **averaged over 5–10 cardiac cycles**, copes with the irregular rhythm and can be repeated after each fluid bolus or dobutamine change. Because LVOT area is constant, changes in VTI alone track changes in stroke volume.

**Why not the others:**

- B – PPV requires sinus rhythm and controlled ventilation; both conditions fail here.
- C – Severe TR recirculates the cold indicator and makes PAC thermodilution unreliable.
- D – CVP is not a measure of flow and is raised by TR.
- E – SVV has the same limitations as PPV, and uncalibrated pulse contour is unreliable with high-dose vasopressors.

**Ref:** Cecconi M, et al. ESICM consensus on circulatory shock and hemodynamic monitoring. *Intensive Care Med* 2014;40:1795–1815. Vieillard-Baron A, et al. A decade of progress in critical care echocardiography. *Intensive Care Med* 2019;45:770–788.

</details>

**5.** Regarding the physics of invasive arterial pressure measurement:

- A. An underdamped system overestimates systolic and underestimates diastolic pressure. — T / F
- B. Long, compliant tubing lowers the natural frequency of the system. — T / F
- C. If the transducer is raised 10 cm above the phlebostatic axis, the displayed pressure will be about 7 mmHg lower than the true value. — T / F
- D. A fast-flush test producing one or two oscillations before returning to baseline indicates appropriate damping. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – T.**

- A (True) – Resonance (ringing) exaggerates systolic peaks and lowers diastolic pressure; MAP is relatively preserved.
- B (True) – Natural frequency falls with long, compliant tubing, air bubbles and extra stopcocks, and rises with short, stiff, wide-bore tubing.
- C (True) – 10 cmH2O ≈ 7.4 mmHg; levelling to the phlebostatic axis is distinct from zeroing to atmosphere.
- D (True) – 1.5–2 oscillations indicate optimal damping; none suggests overdamping, many suggest underdamping.

**Ref:** Saugel B, et al. How to measure blood pressure using an arterial catheter: a systematic 5-step approach. *Crit Care* 2020;24:172.

</details>

**6.** Which of the following typically raise the pulmonary artery occlusion pressure (PAOP)?

- A. Severe idiopathic pulmonary arterial hypertension with a normal left heart — T / F
- B. Acute massive pulmonary embolism — T / F
- C. Acute severe mitral regurgitation — T / F
- D. Aggressive diuresis in a patient with normal cardiac function — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – F, B – F, C – T, D – F.**

- A (False) – Pulmonary arterial hypertension is pre-capillary: high mPAP and PVR with PAOP ≤ 15 mmHg.
- B (False) – PE is a pre-capillary obstruction; PAOP is normal or low because LV filling falls.
- C (True) – Acute severe MR produces large v waves and a raised PAOP.
- D (False) – Diuresis lowers PAOP.

**EDIC trap:** High PEEP or a catheter tip outside West zone 3 can falsely elevate PAOP.

**Ref:** Humbert M, et al. 2022 ESC/ERS pulmonary hypertension guidelines. *Eur Heart J* 2022;43:3618–3731. Magder S. *Chest* 2017;151:908–916.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W04-D4 · Lactate

**1.** A 66-year-old man with type 2 diabetes on metformin presents with vomiting, AKI (creatinine 520 µmol/L), pH 6.98 and lactate 18 mmol/L, with normal blood pressure. Regarding lactate and lactic acidosis:

- A. Hyperlactataemia in sepsis always indicates tissue hypoxia. — T / F
- B. Metformin accumulation can cause type B lactic acidosis. — T / F
- C. The kidney is the main organ of lactate clearance. — T / F
- D. D-lactate (e.g. in short-bowel syndrome) is detected by standard blood gas lactate analysers. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – F, B – T, C – F, D – F.**

- A (False) – Much septic hyperlactataemia comes from β2-stimulated aerobic glycolysis and reduced hepatic clearance; lactate marks severity, not proof of anaerobiosis.
- B (True) – Metformin inhibits mitochondrial respiration and hepatic gluconeogenesis from lactate; accumulation in AKI causes MALA, and EXTRIP recommends dialysis in severe cases (e.g. lactate > 20 mmol/L, pH ≤ 7.0).
- C (False) – The liver clears ~60–70 % (Cori cycle); the kidneys ~20–30 %.
- D (False) – Analysers use L-lactate oxidase; D-lactic acidosis causes a raised anion gap and encephalopathy with a normal measured lactate.

**Ref:** Garcia-Alvarez M, Marik P, Bellomo R. Sepsis-associated hyperlactatemia. *Crit Care* 2014;18:503. Calello DP, et al. EXTRIP metformin. *Crit Care Med* 2015;43:1716–1730. Kraut JA, Madias NE. Lactic acidosis. *N Engl J Med* 2014;371:2309–2319.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W04-D5 · Echocardiography and POCUS in shock

**1.** A ventilated patient in shock has an LVOT VTI of 16 cm. During passive leg raising it rises to 18.5 cm. How should this be interpreted?

- A. Not fluid responsive – the change is < 25 %
- B. Fluid responsive – VTI rose by ≥ 10 %
- C. Uninterpretable without an IVC measurement
- D. Uninterpretable because the patient is ventilated
- E. Fluid responsive only if CVP also rises by ≥ 2 mmHg

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

(18.5 − 16)/16 ≈ +16 % ≥ 10 % threshold for PLR → fluid responsive. PLR works in ventilated patients; the least significant change of VTI is ~12 %.

**EDIC trap:** PLR threshold 10 %; EEO + EIO 13 %.

**Ref:** Robba C, et al. ESICM POCUS consensus. *Intensive Care Med* 2021;47:1347–1367.

</details>

**2.** A patient with moderate ARDS on PEEP 14 becomes hypotensive. TTE shows RV/LV end-diastolic area ratio 1.1 and paradoxical septal motion.

- A. The RV is severely dilated. — T / F
- B. A fluid bolus is the first step. — T / F
- C. Noradrenaline to maintain RV perfusion pressure is appropriate. — T / F
- D. Prone positioning can unload the RV. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – T, D – T.**

- A (True) – RV/LV > 0.6 = dilated, ≥ 1 = severe; with septal dyskinesia = acute cor pulmonale (~22 % in ARDS).
- B (False) – Fluids worsen RV dilatation and septal shift.
- C (True) – Restore RV coronary perfusion.
- D (True) – Proning lowers pulmonary vascular resistance and is part of RV-protective ventilation.

**EDIC trap:** In ACP, fluids harm.

**Ref:** Mekontso Dessap A, et al. *Intensive Care Med* 2016;42:862–870.

</details>

**3.** Regarding the inferior vena cava (IVC) in shock:

- A. A small IVC that collapses on inspiration in a spontaneously breathing hypotensive patient suggests hypovolaemia. — T / F
- B. A plethoric IVC in a patient on high PEEP reliably excludes fluid responsiveness. — T / F
- C. A non-dilated IVC makes cardiac tamponade very unlikely. — T / F
- D. ESICM 2021 recommends using ultrasound to predict fluid responsiveness in all patients with shock. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – Small collapsing IVC = hypovolaemia.
- B (False) – PEEP is one of the ~10 situations where the IVC fails.
- C (True) – Tamponade almost always produces a plethoric IVC.
- D (False) – Recommended against for basic ultrasound in shock without hypovolaemia.

**EDIC trap:** IVC size has many confounders – PEEP, RV failure, TR, intra-abdominal hypertension.

**Ref:** Robba C, et al. *Intensive Care Med* 2021.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W05-D1 · Acid–base: anion gap and blood gas analysis

**1.** Which of the following typically cause a normal anion gap (hyperchloraemic) metabolic acidosis?

- A. Ureterosigmoidostomy — T / F
- B. Distal (type 1) renal tubular acidosis — T / F
- C. Toluene inhalation (glue sniffing) — T / F
- D. Moderate chronic kidney disease (GFR 20–50 mL/min) — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – T.**

- A (True) – with urine in the colon, Cl⁻ is absorbed in exchange for HCO₃⁻ (and NH₄⁺ is reabsorbed), causing a hyperchloraemic acidosis.
- B (True) – impaired distal H⁺ secretion gives a urine pH > 5.5 and a positive urinary anion gap, often with hypokalaemia.
- C (True) – toluene is metabolised to hippuric acid; hippurate is rapidly excreted in urine with Na⁺/K⁺, so the AG is often normal and hypokalaemia is prominent.
- D (True) – moderate CKD reduces ammoniagenesis, causing a hyperchloraemic acidosis. The gap rises only when GFR falls further and phosphate and sulphate accumulate.

**EDIC trap:** CKD moves from a normal-AG to a high-AG acidosis as GFR declines.

**Ref:** Kraut JA, Madias NE, Metabolic acidosis: pathophysiology, diagnosis and management, Nat Rev Nephrol 2010;6:274–285; Berend K, de Vries AP, Gans RO, N Engl J Med 2014;371:1434–1445.

</details>

**2.** A patient is cooled to 32 °C. Blood gases are analysed in a standard analyser. Regarding blood gas analysis:

- A. The PaO2 reported at 37 °C is higher than the true in vivo value at 32 °C. — T / F
- B. Alpha-stat management uses temperature-corrected values. — T / F
- C. pH-stat management requires increasing minute ventilation to remove CO2. — T / F
- D. A sample with a very high leucocyte count left at room temperature for an hour will show a falsely high PaO2. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – F.**

- A (True) – when blood is warmed to 37 °C in the analyser, gas solubility falls and partial pressures rise, so uncorrected PO₂ and PCO₂ overestimate in vivo values in hypothermia.
- B (False) – alpha-stat uses values measured at 37 °C (**uncorrected**), preserving the ionisation state of histidine imidazole groups.
- C (False) – pH-stat targets a temperature-corrected PaCO₂ of ~5.3 kPa, i.e. relative hypercapnia, achieved by adding CO₂ or **reducing** ventilation.
- D (False) – metabolising leucocytes (and platelets) consume O₂ in the syringe ("leucocyte larceny"), so PaO₂ is falsely **low**.

**EDIC trap:** alpha-stat = uncorrected (37 °C) values; pH-stat = temperature-corrected values.

**Ref:** Ashwood ER, Kost G, Kenny M, Clin Chem 1983;29:1877–1885; Fox MJ, Brody JS, Weintraub LR, Am J Med 1979;67:742–746.

</details>

**3.** Regarding causes and interpretation of a raised anion gap metabolic acidosis:

- A. Pyroglutamic acidosis (5-oxoprolinuria) may occur with regular paracetamol in malnourished patients, especially when combined with flucloxacillin. — T / F
- B. Propylene glycol, a solvent in high-dose IV lorazepam infusions, can cause a raised anion gap and osmolar gap. — T / F
- C. Hypoalbuminaemia lowers the anion gap and can mask a raised-gap acidosis unless the gap is corrected. — T / F
- D. Large-volume 0.9 % saline administration causes a raised anion gap acidosis. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – glutathione depletion leads to 5-oxoproline accumulation, and flucloxacillin inhibits 5-oxoprolinase; malnutrition, sepsis and CKD increase the risk.
- B (True) – propylene glycol is metabolised to L- and D-lactate, causing a raised anion gap together with a raised osmolar gap.
- C (True) – albumin is the main unmeasured anion; corrected AG ≈ AG + 0.25 × (40 − albumin g/L).
- D (False) – saline lowers the SID through hyperchloraemia, causing a **normal** anion gap acidosis.

**Ref:** Berend K et al., N Engl J Med 2014;371:1434–1445; Emmett M, Acetaminophen toxicity and 5-oxoproline (pyroglutamic acid): a tale of two cycles, Clin J Am Soc Nephrol 2014;9:191–200.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W05-D2 · Stewart approach to acid–base

**1.** A 59-year-old woman with decompensated cirrhosis has: pH 7.40, PaCO2 5.3 kPa, HCO3⁻ 24 mmol/L, Na⁺ 138, K⁺ 4.0, Cl⁻ 110, lactate 1.0 mmol/L, albumin 18 g/L. Regarding her acid–base status:

- A. Her apparent strong ion difference is about 31 mEq/L, which is reduced. — T / F
- B. Her low albumin produces a metabolic alkalosis that offsets an acidifying process. — T / F
- C. The normal pH and bicarbonate exclude a metabolic acid–base disorder. — T / F
- D. Infusing 20 % albumin is the appropriate treatment for this acid–base disturbance. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – F.**

- A (True) – SIDa = (Na⁺ + K⁺) − (Cl⁻ + lactate) = 142 − 111 = 31 mEq/L (normal ~40–42, ignoring Ca²⁺/Mg²⁺): a hyperchloraemic (low-SID) acidosis.
- B (True) – albumin is the main component of ATOT; a low ATOT is alkalinising (~+6–7 mmol/L BE at 18 g/L), which is why her pH is normal.
- C (False) – two opposing metabolic disorders cancel out; Stewart or albumin-corrected anion gap analysis reveals them.
- D (False) – acid–base values are not a target for albumin; treat the cause (chloride load, underlying illness).

**EDIC trap:** a normal pH and HCO₃⁻ ≠ normal acid–base status in hypoalbuminaemia (Fencl: ~1/6 of ICU patients).

**Ref:** Kellum JA, Determinants of blood pH in health and disease, Crit Care 2000;4:6–14; Fencl V et al., Am J Respir Crit Care Med 2000;162:2246–2251.

</details>

**2.** In the Stewart (physicochemical) approach:

- A. Bicarbonate concentration is an independent variable. — T / F
- B. The total concentration of weak acids (ATOT), mainly albumin and phosphate, is an independent variable. — T / F
- C. Lactate is a dependent variable. — T / F
- D. The hydroxide ion concentration is an independent variable. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – F, B – T, C – F, D – F.**

- A (False) – bicarbonate is a **dependent** variable, determined by PaCO₂, SID and ATOT.
- B (True) – ATOT (mainly albumin and phosphate) is one of the three independent variables, with PaCO₂ and SID.
- C (False) – lactate is a strong (fully dissociated) anion and therefore a component of the SID: it changes the independent variables rather than being determined by them.
- D (False) – OH⁻, like H⁺, HCO₃⁻, A⁻ and CO₃²⁻, is a dependent variable.

**Ref:** Stewart PA, Modern quantitative acid–base chemistry, Can J Physiol Pharmacol 1983;61:1444–1461; Kellum JA, Crit Care 2000;4:6–14.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W05-D3 · Metabolic alkalosis, mixed disorders, ketoacidoses and toxic alcohols

**1.** After 5 days of vomiting, a 40-year-old woman has pH 7.52, HCO₃⁻ 38 mmol/L, PaCO₂ 6.4 kPa (48 mmHg), K⁺ 2.9 mmol/L and urine chloride 8 mmol/L. The best treatment is:

- A. Acetazolamide
- B. 0.9 % NaCl with KCl
- C. Spironolactone
- D. Hydrochloric acid infusion as first line
- E. Haemodialysis

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

Urine Cl⁻ < 20 = chloride-responsive alkalosis (vomiting, NG losses, previous diuretics) → replace volume, chloride and potassium.

**EDIC trap:** U-Cl⁻ < 20 → saline-responsive; > 20 → mineralocorticoid excess or current diuretics.

**Ref:** Galla JH. Metabolic alkalosis. *J Am Soc Nephrol* 2000;11:369–375.

</details>

**2.** A confused man has Na⁺ 140 mmol/L, glucose 5 mmol/L, urea 5 mmol/L, ethanol undetectable and measured osmolality 330 mOsm/kg. Which is correct?

- A. Osmolar gap 10 – normal
- B. Osmolar gap 40 – a toxic alcohol should be suspected
- C. Osmolar gap 40 – this is diagnostic of methanol
- D. The osmolar gap cannot be calculated without a lactate
- E. Osmolar gap −10 – laboratory error

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

Calculated = 2 × 140 + 5 + 5 = 290; gap = 330 − 290 = 40 (> 10) → suspect a toxic alcohol (methanol, ethylene glycol…). The gap does not identify which one, and a normal gap does not exclude poisoning.

**EDIC trap:** Late presentation: the gap falls as the alcohol is metabolised while the anion gap rises.

**Ref:** Kraut JA, Mullins ME. Toxic alcohols. *N Engl J Med* 2018;378:270–280.

</details>

**3.** Regarding ketoacidosis and mixed disorders:

- A. β-hydroxybutyrate ≥ 3 mmol/L is part of the 2024 consensus definition of DKA. — T / F
- B. If K⁺ is < 3.5 mmol/L, insulin should be delayed until potassium is replaced. — T / F
- C. The 2024 DKA definition requires glucose ≥ 11.1 mmol/L in all patients. — T / F
- D. Salicylate poisoning typically causes respiratory alkalosis with a high anion gap metabolic acidosis. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – T.**
