- A (True) – Ketones ≥ 3 mmol/L.
- B (True) – Insulin drives K⁺ into cells – risk of arrhythmia.
- C (False) – Glucose ≥ 11.1 **or** a history of diabetes (euglycaemic DKA, e.g. SGLT2 inhibitors).
- D (True) – Central respiratory stimulation + HAGMA.

**EDIC trap:** Euglycaemic DKA – SGLT2 inhibitors.

**Ref:** Umpierrez GE, et al. Hyperglycemic crises consensus. *Diabetes Care* 2024;47:1257–1275.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W06-D1 · Hyponatraemia

**1.** Which of the following typically produce hyponatraemia with a normal or raised (not low) serum osmolality?

- A. Hyperglycaemia of 40 mmol/L — T / F
- B. Repeated doses of IV mannitol — T / F
- C. Severe hypertriglyceridaemia measured by an indirect ion-selective electrode — T / F
- D. Primary (psychogenic) polydipsia — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – without insulin, glucose is an effective osmole that draws water out of cells (translocational, hypertonic hyponatraemia). Na⁺ falls by ~1.6–2.4 mmol/L for each 5.5 mmol/L rise in glucose, i.e. by ~10–15 mmol/L at 40 mmol/L.
- B (True) – mannitol is also an effective osmole. It causes hyponatraemia with normal or high measured osmolality and a raised osmolar gap.
- C (True) – this is pseudohyponatraemia: lipids or paraproteins reduce the plasma water fraction, so an indirect ISE (laboratory analyser) underestimates Na⁺. A direct ISE (blood gas analyser) gives the true value, and osmolality is normal.
- D (False) – primary polydipsia causes **hypotonic** hyponatraemia with maximally dilute urine (U-osm ≤ 100 mOsm/kg).

**Ref:** Spasovski G et al., Clinical practice guideline on diagnosis and treatment of hyponatraemia, Intensive Care Med 2014;40:320–331 (and Eur J Endocrinol 2014;170:G1–G47).

</details>

**2.** A 31-year-old woman collapses with a generalised seizure after a marathon. Serum sodium is 118 mmol/L and she remains drowsy (GCS 11). Regarding her immediate treatment:

- A. 150 mL of 3 % saline over 20 minutes is appropriate, repeated (up to twice) until sodium has risen by about 5 mmol/L. — T / F
- B. Fluid restriction alone is the appropriate first-line treatment. — T / F
- C. Tolvaptan is recommended for severe symptoms. — T / F
- D. 1 L of 0.9 % saline over an hour is the preferred initial therapy. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – F.**

- A (True) – exercise-associated hyponatraemia with a seizure and reduced consciousness is an emergency. Give 150 mL of 3 % saline over 20 min (100 mL boluses in some EAH protocols), recheck Na⁺ and repeat until it has risen by ~5 mmol/L, which usually improves symptoms.
- B (False) – fluid restriction is far too slow with severe symptoms; it suits asymptomatic EAH or chronic SIAD.
- C (False) – vaptans act slowly and unpredictably and are not recommended for severe symptoms.
- D (False) – isotonic saline barely raises Na⁺ and, while non-osmotic ADH release persists (as in EAH), it can even lower it.

**EDIC trap:** SALSA showed that rapid intermittent boluses are as safe as a slow continuous infusion, with less need for re-lowering.

**Ref:** Spasovski G et al., Clinical practice guideline on diagnosis and treatment of hyponatraemia, Intensive Care Med 2014;40:320–331 (and Eur J Endocrinol 2014;170:G1–G47); Baek SH et al. (SALSA), JAMA Intern Med 2021;181:81–92; Hew-Butler T et al., Clin J Sport Med 2015;25:303–320.

</details>

**3.** Regarding definitions and safe correction of hyponatraemia (European guideline):

- A. Profound hyponatraemia is defined as a serum sodium below 130 mmol/L. — T / F
- B. Hyponatraemia is classified as chronic if documented to have lasted ≥ 48 hours or if the duration is unknown. — T / F
- C. Hypokalaemia, alcohol use disorder, malnutrition and liver disease increase the risk of osmotic demyelination. — T / F
- D. If sodium has been overcorrected, re-lowering with desmopressin and 5 % dextrose is contraindicated. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – F, B – T, C – T, D – F.**

- A (False) – mild is 130–135, moderate 125–129 and profound < 125 mmol/L.
- B (True) – hyponatraemia is chronic if it has lasted ≥ 48 h, and when the duration is unknown it is treated as chronic.
- C (True) – hypokalaemia, alcohol use disorder, malnutrition and liver disease (and Na⁺ ≤ 105 mmol/L) lower the threshold for osmotic demyelination. The usual limits are ≤ 10 mmol/L in the first 24 h and ≤ 8 mmol/L per further 24 h; aim for ~8 mmol/L/24 h in high-risk patients.
- D (False) – active re-lowering is recommended if the limit is exceeded: stop active treatment and give 10 mL/kg of electrolyte-free water (5 % glucose) over 1 h ± desmopressin 2 µg IV.

**Ref:** Spasovski G et al., Clinical practice guideline on diagnosis and treatment of hyponatraemia, Intensive Care Med 2014;40:320–331 (and Eur J Endocrinol 2014;170:G1–G47); Sterns RH, Disorders of plasma sodium, N Engl J Med 2015;372:55–65.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W06-D2 · Magnesium disorders

**1.** A 64-year-old woman who has taken omeprazole 40 mg daily for 6 years is admitted with muscle cramps and carpopedal spasm. Potassium is 2.9 mmol/L despite a week of oral supplements, corrected calcium is 1.85 mmol/L, QTc is 560 ms, and the monitor shows self-terminating runs of polymorphic ventricular tachycardia. Which is the most appropriate immediate treatment?

- A. IV calcium gluconate 10 %, repeated until the QTc normalises
- B. IV magnesium sulphate 2 g over 10–15 minutes
- C. IV potassium chloride 40 mmol over 1 hour as the sole treatment
- D. IV amiodarone 300 mg
- E. Oral alfacalcidol

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

**Why B:** Long-term PPI therapy impairs active intestinal magnesium absorption (TRPM6/7) and causes severe hypomagnesaemia. This in turn causes refractory hypokalaemia (ROMK-mediated renal K⁺ wasting) and hypocalcaemia (reduced PTH secretion and end-organ resistance). Torsades-type polymorphic VT with a long QT is treated with MgSO₄ 2 g (≈ 8 mmol) IV, whatever the serum Mg²⁺; potassium and calcium will not correct until magnesium is replaced.

**Why not the others:**
- A – calcium does not treat torsades, and the hypocalcaemia will not correct while Mg²⁺ is low.
- C – KCl alone will not correct the K⁺ while magnesium depletion keeps the kidney wasting K⁺, and it does not stop torsades.
- D – amiodarone prolongs the QT and is contraindicated in torsades de pointes.
- E – alfacalcidol acts over days and does not address the magnesium-driven hypocalcaemia or the arrhythmia.

**Ref:** Soar J, Böttiger BW, Carli P, et al. ERC Guidelines 2025 Adult Advanced Life Support, Resuscitation 2025;215(Suppl 1):110769; Hess MW, et al., Aliment Pharmacol Ther 2012;36:405–413.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W06-D3 · Refeeding, phosphate and calcium

**1.** A 29-year-old woman with anorexia nervosa (BMI 13 kg/m²) was admitted to ICU after a collapse. Nasogastric feeding was started at 25 kcal/kg/day. On day 3 she is tachycardic and weak with new ankle oedema; phosphate is 0.28 mmol/L, potassium 2.9 mmol/L and magnesium 0.50 mmol/L. Which is the most appropriate management?

- A. Reduce the feed to about 10 kcal/kg/day, give IV phosphate, potassium and magnesium with thiamine, then increase feeding slowly
- B. Stop all feeding for 48 hours until the electrolytes have normalised
- C. Switch to parenteral nutrition at the same caloric target
- D. Continue the current feed and give oral phosphate supplements only
- E. Give furosemide for the oedema and continue the current feed

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.**

**Why A:** This is refeeding syndrome: an insulin-driven intracellular shift of phosphate, K⁺ and Mg²⁺ with Na⁺ and water retention (oedema, tachycardia). A BMI of 13 alone puts her at high risk by NICE (which suggests even 5 kcal/kg/day if BMI < 14). Reduce energy delivery rather than stopping it, replace electrolytes IV with cardiac monitoring, give thiamine and B vitamins, then advance feeding gradually over 4–7 days. In the Doig trial, protocolised caloric restriction improved 60-day survival (a secondary outcome).

**Why not the others:**
- B – stopping all feeding prolongs starvation; the evidence supports restricting energy, not stopping it.
- C – parenteral nutrition at the same target causes the same insulin-driven shift.
- D – oral phosphate alone is inadequate for a level < 0.3 mmol/L, and the feed rate perpetuates the problem.
- E – furosemide worsens the hypokalaemia and hypomagnesaemia, and continuing the feed sustains the refeeding.

**Ref:** da Silva JSV, et al. ASPEN consensus recommendations for refeeding syndrome, Nutr Clin Pract 2020;35:178–195; Doig GS, et al., Lancet Respir Med 2015;3:943–952; NICE CG32 (2006, updated 2017).

</details>

**2.** Which of the following can cause hypercalcaemia?

- A. Long-term lithium therapy — T / F
- B. Vitamin A toxicity — T / F
- C. Sarcoidosis — T / F
- D. Milk-alkali syndrome — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – T.**

- A (True) – lithium raises the set-point of the calcium-sensing receptor and causes PTH-dependent hypercalcaemia.
- B (True) – vitamin A and other retinoids stimulate osteoclastic bone resorption.
- C (True) – granulomas express 1α-hydroxylase and produce calcitriol, which causes PTH-suppressed hypercalcaemia that responds to glucocorticoids.
- D (True) – excess calcium with absorbable alkali (e.g. calcium carbonate) causes hypercalcaemia, metabolic alkalosis and AKI.

**EDIC trap:** primary hyperparathyroidism and malignancy cause ~90 % of cases. Thiazides, thyrotoxicosis, immobilisation and adrenal insufficiency are other causes.

**Ref:** Minisola S, et al. The diagnosis and management of hypercalcaemia, BMJ 2015;350:h2723; Walker MD, Shane E. Hypercalcemia: a review, JAMA 2022;328:1624–1636.

</details>

**3.** A 68-year-old man with squamous cell lung carcinoma presents with confusion and polyuria. Corrected calcium is 3.6 mmol/L, PTH suppressed, PTH-related peptide raised, eGFR 45 mL/min/1.73 m². After 24 hours and 4 L of IV 0.9 % saline his calcium is 3.3 mmol/L and he remains drowsy. Which is the most appropriate next step?

- A. Furosemide 80 mg IV 6-hourly
- B. Prednisolone 40 mg daily
- C. Urgent haemodialysis
- D. Subcutaneous calcitonin as the sole long-term therapy
- E. IV zoledronic acid

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**E.**

**Why E:** This is PTHrP-mediated humoral hypercalcaemia of malignancy that remains symptomatic after rehydration, so it needs an IV bisphosphonate. Zoledronic acid 4 mg is the most potent; adjust the dose or avoid it in severe renal impairment. The Endocrine Society 2023 guideline suggests an IV bisphosphonate or denosumab, and denosumab is preferred when the disease is bisphosphonate-refractory or renal impairment is severe.

**Why not the others:**
- A – loop diuretics are used only for volume overload after rehydration, not to lower calcium.
- B – glucocorticoids are for calcitriol-mediated causes (lymphoma, granulomatous disease), not PTHrP.
- C – dialysis is reserved for severe hypercalcaemia with renal failure or neurological compromise that does not respond to treatment.
- D – calcitonin acts within hours but tachyphylaxis develops within 48 h, so it is a bridge, not long-term therapy.

**Ref:** El-Hajj Fuleihan G, et al. Treatment of hypercalcemia of malignancy in adults: an Endocrine Society clinical practice guideline, J Clin Endocrinol Metab 2023;108:507–528; Walker MD, Shane E, JAMA 2022;328:1624–1636.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W07-D1 · Cardiogenic shock

**1.** A 63-year-old man with anterior STEMI has BP 82/50 mmHg, cool mottled skin and lactate 4.2 mmol/L. He is started on noradrenaline and dobutamine and goes for PCI; his lactate is falling. His SCAI SHOCK stage is:

- A. A
- B. B
- C. C
- D. D
- E. E

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.**

C (classic) = hypoperfusion needing an intervention (drugs/MCS) beyond fluids. B = hypotension without hypoperfusion; D = deteriorating despite initial therapy; E = extremis (e.g. lactate ≥ 8, CPR). Cardiac arrest with coma adds an "A" modifier.

**EDIC trap:** Stage D means worsening despite the initial intervention.

**Ref:** Naidu SS, et al. SCAI SHOCK update. *J Am Coll Cardiol* 2022;79:933–946.

</details>

**2.** Regarding the management of cardiogenic shock after myocardial infarction:

- A. Noradrenaline is the preferred vasopressor. — T / F
- B. Dopamine is preferred because it reduced mortality in the cardiogenic shock subgroup of SOAP II. — T / F
- C. Routine intra-aortic balloon pump use is recommended. — T / F
- D. Emergency revascularisation of the culprit lesion reduced 6-month mortality in the SHOCK trial. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – T.**

- A (True) – Norepinephrine is first line.
- B (False) – Dopamine caused more arrhythmias and higher mortality in the CS subgroup.
- C (False) – IABP routine use – class III (IABP-SHOCK II).
- D (True) – SHOCK – no 30-day difference but lower 6-month mortality.

**EDIC trap:** SHOCK: the benefit appeared at 6 months, not 30 days.

**Ref:** Hochman JS, et al. SHOCK. *N Engl J Med* 1999; De Backer D, et al. SOAP II. *N Engl J Med* 2010.

</details>

**3.** In which patient is the evidence for a microaxial flow pump (DanGer Shock, IIa) most applicable?

- A. STEMI with cardiogenic shock, awake, no cardiac arrest
- B. Comatose survivor of out-of-hospital cardiac arrest with STEMI and shock
- C. Isolated right ventricular infarction with shock
- D. Takotsubo syndrome with LVOT obstruction
- E. Septic cardiomyopathy with low cardiac output

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.**

DanGer Shock enrolled STEMI-related CS and excluded comatose OHCA survivors; it reduced 180-day mortality at the cost of more complications. Routine MCS for all CS is not recommended.

**EDIC trap:** Microaxial pump = selected STEMI-CS, not comatose OHCA.

**Ref:** Møller JE, et al. DanGer Shock. *N Engl J Med* 2024;390:1382–1393.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W07-D2 · Mechanical circulatory support

**1.** A 58-year-old man with a continuous-flow left ventricular assist device (LVAD), implanted as destination therapy, is admitted with dizziness. Regarding LVAD patients:

- A. A palpable pulse may be weak or absent, and blood pressure is best measured with a Doppler probe or arterial line. — T / F
- B. Right ventricular failure is a major early complication after LVAD implantation. — T / F
- C. Hypovolaemia can cause "suction events", in which the LV collapses onto the inflow cannula and provokes ventricular arrhythmias. — T / F
- D. Chest compressions are absolutely contraindicated if the patient is unresponsive with no evidence of device flow. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – continuous flow reduces pulsatility, so the pulse may be absent and automated cuffs unreliable. A Doppler opening pressure approximates MAP (target ~70–80 mmHg).
- B (True) – after LV unloading, increased venous return and leftward septal shift can unmask RV failure (up to 20–40%), which needs inotropes, pulmonary vasodilators or a temporary RVAD.
- C (True) – suction events occur with hypovolaemia or RV failure: low flow and ventricular arrhythmias. Treat with fluid and a lower pump speed.
- D (False) – ERC 2025 and AHA advise starting chest compressions if the patient is unresponsive and the device cannot be restarted or shows no flow. The need for perfusion outweighs the risk of cannula dislodgement.

**EDIC trap:** in an LVAD patient, assess perfusion (mentation, skin, capnography), check the device and its alarms, and do not rely on the pulse.

**Ref:** Lott C et al., ERC Guidelines 2025 Special Circumstances, Resuscitation 2025;215(Suppl 1):110753; Peberdy MA et al., Circulation 2017;135:e1115–e1134.

</details>

**2.** Regarding the principles of the intra-aortic balloon pump (IABP):

- A. The balloon should inflate on the R wave of the ECG. — T / F
- B. Significant aortic regurgitation is a contraindication. — T / F
- C. Helium is used because it is more soluble in blood than carbon dioxide. — T / F
- D. Balloon size is selected primarily according to the patient's body weight. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – F, B – T, C – F, D – F.**

- A (False) – the balloon inflates at the dicrotic notch (aortic valve closure, around the middle of the T wave) and deflates just before the next systole (R wave).
- B (True) – diastolic inflation would increase regurgitant volume. Aortic dissection and severe peripheral vascular disease are other contraindications.
- C (False) – helium is used for its **low density** (rapid shuttling). CO₂ is more soluble and was used earlier for safety in case of rupture.
- D (False) – balloon size (e.g. 30–50 mL) is chosen by patient **height**, not weight.

**EDIC trap:** inflation is triggered by the T wave and deflation by the R wave.

**Ref:** Krishna M, Zacharowski K, Contin Educ Anaesth Crit Care Pain 2009;9:24–28; Thiele H et al., IABP-SHOCK II, N Engl J Med 2012;367:1287–1296.

</details>

**3.** A 63-year-old man develops acute severe mitral regurgitation from papillary muscle rupture 3 days after an inferior myocardial infarction. He is in cardiogenic shock and an IABP is inserted via the femoral artery as a bridge to emergency surgery; BP before insertion was 82/50 mmHg with pulmonary oedema. Each of the following is an expected effect of correctly timed counterpulsation EXCEPT:

- A. A reduction in regurgitant fraction
- B. An increase in diastolic aortic pressure
- C. A reduction in left-ventricular afterload
- D. An increase in left-ventricular end-diastolic pressure
- E. A modest increase in forward cardiac output

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.**

**Why D:** Correctly timed counterpulsation **lowers** LV end-diastolic pressure. Deflation just before systole lowers aortic end-diastolic pressure and afterload, so the LV empties more completely, and LV wall stress, LVEDP and myocardial O₂ demand all fall.

**Why not the others:**
- A – lower afterload favours forward ejection and reduces the regurgitant fraction in MR (and the shunt in post-infarct VSD).
- B – inflation in diastole augments diastolic aortic (coronary) perfusion pressure.
- C – presystolic deflation creates a "sink" that reduces LV afterload.
- E – forward cardiac output rises modestly (~0.5–1 L/min).

**EDIC trap:** IABP remains a bridge in mechanical complications of MI, but IABP-SHOCK II showed no mortality benefit in AMI-CS without them, and routine use there is class III (ESC 2023).

**Ref:** Byrne RA et al., 2023 ESC ACS Guidelines, Eur Heart J 2023;44:3720–3826; Thiele H et al., N Engl J Med 2012;367:1287–1296.

</details>

**4.** Regarding IABP timing, position and evidence:

- A. On chest X-ray, the radio-opaque tip should lie about 2–3 cm below the origin of the left subclavian artery, roughly at the level of the carina. — T / F
- B. Late deflation increases left-ventricular afterload and workload. — T / F
- C. Early inflation, before aortic valve closure, improves stroke volume. — T / F
- D. ESC 2023 guidelines recommend routine IABP insertion in acute myocardial infarction complicated by cardiogenic shock. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – F.**

- A (True) – the tip should sit about 2–3 cm below the origin of the left subclavian artery (around the carina). Too high risks occluding the left subclavian or carotid artery; too low risks occluding the renal and mesenteric arteries.
- B (True) – a balloon still inflated in early systole forces the LV to eject against it, increasing afterload and workload.
- C (False) – early inflation, before the aortic valve closes, causes premature valve closure, a smaller stroke volume and a higher LVEDV and afterload.
- D (False) – routine IABP in AMI-CS without mechanical complications is not recommended (class III), based on IABP-SHOCK II (no difference in 30-day, 1-year or 6-year mortality).

**Ref:** Byrne RA et al., Eur Heart J 2023;44:3720–3826; Thiele H et al., IABP-SHOCK II 6-year outcome, Circulation 2019;139:395–403.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W07-D3 · Acute coronary syndromes in ICU

**1.** A 66-year-old man has just undergone primary PCI to an occluded proximal right coronary artery. In the coronary care unit his BP is 78/45 mmHg and HR 38/min with complete heart block. JVP is raised, lungs are clear, and echocardiography shows a dilated, hypokinetic right ventricle with good LV function. He has already received 500 mL of crystalloid. What is the most appropriate next step?

- A. Start a glyceryl trinitrate infusion
- B. Insert a temporary pacing wire, ideally achieving AV-synchronous pacing
- C. Give IV furosemide 40 mg
- D. Give a further 2 L of crystalloid rapidly
- E. Insert an intra-aortic balloon pump

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

**Why B:** In RV infarction, cardiac output depends heavily on heart rate, preload and the atrial contribution. Complete heart block, common with proximal RCA occlusion, should be treated with temporary pacing, ideally dual-chamber to restore AV synchrony. If shock persists, add inotropes (dobutamine) or mechanical RV support.

**Why not the others:**
- A – a GTN infusion reduces preload and can cause profound hypotension in a preload-dependent RV.
- C – furosemide also reduces preload and is harmful here.
- D – a modest fluid challenge is reasonable, but 2 L overdistends the RV, shifts the septum and reduces LV filling.
- E – an IABP mainly supports the LV and does not fix bradycardia or RV failure.

**Ref:** Byrne RA et al., 2023 ESC ACS Guidelines, Eur Heart J 2023;44:3720–3826; Harjola VP et al., Eur J Heart Fail 2016;18:226–241.

</details>

**2.** A 72-year-old woman with known stable coronary disease is on day 3 after hip fracture fixation. She develops melaena; haemoglobin falls to 62 g/L, HR is 125/min and BP 92/55 mmHg. She complains of central chest tightness, the ECG shows new 1.5 mm horizontal ST depression in V4–V6, and high-sensitivity troponin rises from 30 to 900 ng/L. According to the Fourth Universal Definition, this is best classified as:

- A. Type 1 myocardial infarction
- B. Type 4a myocardial infarction
- C. Non-ischaemic (acute) myocardial injury
- D. Type 2 myocardial infarction
- E. Type 5 myocardial infarction

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.**

**Why D:** A troponin rise and fall above the 99th centile with evidence of ischaemia (chest tightness, new ischaemic ST depression) caused by an O₂ supply–demand imbalance (anaemia, tachycardia, hypotension), not acute atherothrombosis, is type 2 MI. Treat the cause (transfusion, endoscopic haemostasis) rather than escalating antithrombotic therapy, which would worsen the bleeding.

**Why not the others:**
- A – type 1 MI is plaque rupture or erosion with thrombus, and there is no such trigger here.
- B – type 4a MI is related to PCI (within 48 h of the procedure).
- C – non-ischaemic myocardial injury is a troponin rise **without** clinical evidence of ischaemia (e.g. sepsis, myocarditis, PE).
- E – type 5 MI is related to CABG.

**Ref:** Thygesen K et al., Fourth Universal Definition of Myocardial Infarction, Eur Heart J 2019;40:237–269.

</details>

**3.** A 29-year-old man presents 1 hour after insufflating cocaine with central chest pain, agitation, HR 130/min and BP 195/115 mmHg. He is sweating and tremulous with dilated pupils. The ECG shows sinus tachycardia with 1 mm ST depression in the lateral leads, and the first troponin is pending. Which initial treatment is LEAST appropriate?

- A. Aspirin 300 mg orally
- B. IV benzodiazepine titrated to sedation
- C. IV propranolol
- D. Sublingual or IV glyceryl trinitrate
- E. IV phentolamine if hypertension persists despite benzodiazepines and nitrates

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.**

**Why C:** In acute cocaine toxicity, non-selective β-blockade can leave α-adrenergic vasoconstriction unopposed, which worsens coronary spasm and hypertension. Guidelines advise avoiding β-blockers in the acute phase.

**Why not the others:**
- A – aspirin is appropriate for suspected ACS (no contraindication here).
- B – benzodiazepines are first-line: they reduce central sympathetic drive, agitation, tachycardia and hypertension.
- D – nitrates relieve coronary vasospasm and lower BP and are part of first-line treatment.
- E – an α-antagonist (phentolamine) or a calcium channel blocker is the appropriate next step for persistent hypertension.

**EDIC trap:** uncontrolled hypertension is a relative contraindication to thrombolysis; if STEMI develops, primary PCI is preferred.

**Ref:** McCord J et al., AHA scientific statement, Circulation 2008;117:1897–1907; Byrne RA et al., 2023 ESC ACS Guidelines, Eur Heart J 2023;44:3720–3826.

</details>

**4.** Regarding reperfusion and antithrombotic therapy in STEMI (ESC 2023):

- A. Primary PCI is preferred if the anticipated time from STEMI diagnosis to wire crossing is ≤ 120 minutes; otherwise fibrinolysis should be given within 10 minutes of diagnosis. — T / F
- B. After successful fibrinolysis, routine angiography within 2–24 hours is recommended. — T / F
- C. A resuscitated out-of-hospital cardiac arrest patient with persistent ST elevation should undergo emergency angiography, even if comatose. — T / F
- D. Ticagrelor is preferred over prasugrel in patients proceeding to PCI. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – a 120-minute threshold (diagnosis to wire crossing) separates primary PCI from the pharmaco-invasive strategy; fibrinolysis should be given within 10 min of diagnosis.
- B (True) – after successful lysis, angiography at 2–24 h is recommended; immediate rescue PCI if lysis fails.
- C (True) – primary PCI is recommended for resuscitated patients with persistent ST elevation, whether or not they are comatose. Without ST elevation, routine immediate angiography is not recommended (COACT, TOMAHAWK).
- D (False) – prasugrel should be considered **in preference to** ticagrelor in patients proceeding to PCI (IIa), based on ISAR-REACT 5.

**Ref:** Byrne RA et al., 2023 ESC ACS Guidelines, Eur Heart J 2023;44:3720–3826; Schüpke S et al., ISAR-REACT 5, N Engl J Med 2019;381:1524–1534.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W07-D4 · Inotropes, acute heart failure and stress cardiomyopathy

**1.** Regarding levosimendan:

- A. It sensitises the myofilaments to calcium by binding cardiac troponin C in a calcium-dependent manner. — T / F
- B. In LeoPARDS, adding levosimendan reduced organ dysfunction in septic shock. — T / F
- C. Its active metabolite OR-1896 has a half-life of about 80 hours, so effects persist for days after a 24-hour infusion. — T / F
- D. Its principal adverse haemodynamic effect is hypertension. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – levosimendan binds cardiac troponin C in a calcium-dependent way, and opens K-ATP channels to cause vasodilation. Intracellular Ca²⁺ does not rise, so myocardial O₂ consumption increases little and diastolic relaxation is preserved.
- B (False) – LeoPARDS found no reduction in mean SOFA score, more supraventricular tachyarrhythmias and a lower chance of weaning from ventilation. SSC 2021 suggests against levosimendan in septic shock.
- C (True) – the active metabolite OR-1896 (t½ ~80 h) explains the prolonged action; the loading dose is often omitted to avoid hypotension.
- D (False) – the main adverse effects are vasodilation and **hypotension**, tachyarrhythmias and hypokalaemia.

**Ref:** Gordon AC et al., LeoPARDS, N Engl J Med 2016;375:1638–1648; Papp Z et al., Int J Cardiol 2012;159:82–87.

</details>

**2.** A 67-year-old woman is 3 hours out of theatre after mitral valve replacement and tricuspid annuloplasty for longstanding mitral regurgitation. HR 118/min (sinus), MAP 68 mmHg on noradrenaline 0.08 µg/kg/min, CVP 18 mmHg, mean PAP 45 mmHg, PAOP 14 mmHg, cardiac index 1.8 L/min/m², SvO2 52 %, lactate 3.4 mmol/L. Echocardiography shows a dilated, poorly contracting right ventricle and a well-seated prosthesis. Which is the most appropriate addition?

- A. Dobutamine 10 µg/kg/min
- B. Milrinone infusion (without a bolus), maintaining MAP with noradrenaline
- C. Levosimendan 12 µg/kg loading bolus followed by infusion
- D. Intra-aortic balloon pump
- E. A further increase in noradrenaline alone to a MAP of 85 mmHg

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

**Why B:** This is low cardiac output from RV failure with raised PVR after mitral surgery (high CVP, mPAP 45 mmHg with normal PAOP, CI 1.8, SvO₂ 52%). A PDE-3 inhibitor (an inodilator) increases RV contractility and lowers PVR with less chronotropy than dobutamine. Omit the bolus to avoid hypotension and support MAP with noradrenaline. Inhaled pulmonary vasodilators (NO, epoprostenol) are reasonable adjuncts.

**Why not the others:**
- A – high-dose dobutamine would raise an already high heart rate (118/min) and myocardial O₂ demand.
- C – a levosimendan loading bolus risks significant hypotension in a patient already on noradrenaline.
- D – an IABP supports the LV and does little for RV failure.
- E – raising noradrenaline alone does not improve RV output.

**EDIC trap:** DOREMI showed similar outcomes with milrinone and dobutamine in CS; choose by haemodynamic profile.

**Ref:** Mathew R et al., DOREMI, N Engl J Med 2021;385:516–525; Harjola VP et al., Eur J Heart Fail 2016;18:226–241.

</details>

**3.** A 78-year-old woman is brought in with acute breathlessness. BP 205/120 mmHg, HR 115/min, RR 36/min, SpO2 82 % on a 15 L/min non-rebreathe mask, widespread crackles. She is alert and able to follow commands. ABG: pH 7.28, PaCO2 6.5 kPa. ECG shows LV hypertrophy without ST elevation. What is the most appropriate immediate management?

- A. Immediate rapid sequence induction and intubation
- B. IV morphine 10 mg and furosemide
- C. CPAP or NIV together with a titrated IV nitrate infusion
- D. High-flow nasal oxygen and IV furosemide only
- E. Fibrinolysis

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.**

**Why C:** This is hypertensive acute HF with pulmonary oedema. CPAP or NIV rapidly improves oxygenation, work of breathing and acidosis and lowers LV afterload, while a titrated IV nitrate reduces preload and afterload and is the key drug when SBP is high. ESC 2026 suggests early NIV in respiratory distress (RR > 25/min, SpO₂ < 90%; IIa, B2) and IV vasodilators when SBP > 110 mmHg (IIb, C). In 3CPO, NIV gave faster physiological improvement without a mortality difference.

**Why not the others:**
- A – intubation is for NIV failure, coma or exhaustion, and this patient is alert and cooperative.
- B – routine morphine is not recommended (III, C) because of respiratory depression and its association with harm.
- D – high-flow oxygen with furosemide alone omits the vasodilator and positive pressure that hypertensive APO needs.
- E – fibrinolysis has no role without STEMI.

**Ref:** Køber L et al., 2026 ESC HF Guidelines, Eur Heart J 2026;47:5346–5457; Gray A et al., 3CPO, N Engl J Med 2008;359:142–151.

</details>

**4.** Regarding Takotsubo (stress) syndrome:

- A. Mid-ventricular and basal ("inverted") patterns of wall-motion abnormality occur, as well as apical ballooning. — T / F
- B. In shock with dynamic LV outflow tract obstruction, inotropes such as dobutamine are the first-line treatment. — T / F
- C. Recurrence does not occur. — T / F
- D. In-hospital mortality is negligible, similar to that of the general population. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – F.**

- A (True) – apical ballooning is the commonest pattern, but mid-ventricular, basal ("inverted") and focal types are recognised.
- B (False) – catecholamines worsen dynamic LVOT obstruction and may perpetuate the syndrome. Treat with fluids and cautious β-blockade; in CS without obstruction, consider levosimendan or mechanical support.
- C (False) – recurrence occurs in about 2% per year.
- D (False) – in-hospital mortality (~4–5%) and complications (CS, LV thrombus, arrhythmia) are comparable to ACS. Physical triggers such as critical illness carry a worse prognosis than emotional ones.

**EDIC trap:** a takotsubo patient in shock with a systolic murmur needs echo for LVOT obstruction before any inotrope.

**Ref:** Ghadri JR et al., International Expert Consensus on Takotsubo Syndrome, Eur Heart J 2018;39:2032–2046 and 2047–2062; Templin C et al., N Engl J Med 2015;373:929–938.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W07-D5 · Pulmonary hypertension

**1.** Regarding pulmonary hypertension (PH), according to the 2022 ESC/ERS guidelines:

- A. PH is defined as a mean pulmonary artery pressure > 20 mmHg at rest, measured by right-heart catheterisation. — T / F
- B. Pre-capillary PH requires a PAWP ≤ 15 mmHg and a pulmonary vascular resistance > 2 Wood units. — T / F
- C. Left heart disease (group 2) is the most common cause of PH. — T / F
- D. Pulmonary arterial vasodilators (e.g. PDE-5 inhibitors, endothelin antagonists) are recommended for PH due to left heart disease. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – the 2022 definition lowered the threshold from mPAP ≥ 25 to > 20 mmHg at rest, and it must be measured by right heart catheterisation.
- B (True) – pre-capillary PH requires PAWP ≤ 15 mmHg and PVR > 2 WU (previously > 3 WU). Post-capillary PH has PAWP > 15 mmHg.
- C (True) – group 2 (left heart disease) is the commonest cause, followed by group 3 (lung disease/hypoxia).
- D (False) – PAH-specific drugs are not recommended in group 2 PH: there is no benefit, and they may cause harm by worsening pulmonary oedema. Treat the underlying left heart disease.

**EDIC trap:** a pulmonary vasodilator given to a patient with high PAWP can precipitate pulmonary oedema.

**Ref:** Humbert M et al., 2022 ESC/ERS Guidelines for the diagnosis and treatment of pulmonary hypertension, Eur Heart J 2022;43:3618–3731.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W08-D1 · Atrial fibrillation in the critically ill

**1.** A 24-year-old man presents with an irregular broad-complex tachycardia at 240/min with varying QRS morphology; a previous ECG showed a short PR interval and delta waves. Regarding his management:

- A. If he is haemodynamically stable, IV procainamide or ibutilide may be used for pharmacological cardioversion. — T / F
- B. Synchronised DC cardioversion is indicated if he becomes haemodynamically unstable. — T / F
- C. IV amiodarone is the drug of choice. — T / F
- D. Catheter ablation of the accessory pathway is recommended after this episode. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – T.**

- A (True) – in stable pre-excited AF, IV procainamide or ibutilide (or flecainide/propafenone) slow conduction over the accessory pathway and can restore sinus rhythm.
- B (True) – electrical cardioversion is the treatment if he becomes unstable, or if in doubt, because pre-excited AF at 240/min can degenerate to VF.
- C (False) – the ESC advises against IV amiodarone in pre-excited AF; like other AV-nodal blockers (adenosine, verapamil/diltiazem, beta-blockers, digoxin) it can increase conduction over the accessory pathway.
- D (True) – catheter ablation of the accessory pathway is recommended after pre-excited AF, as this is a high-risk pathway.

**EDIC trap:** An irregular, very fast broad-complex tachycardia with changing QRS shape is pre-excited AF until proven otherwise – do not block the AV node.

**Ref:** Brugada J et al., 2019 ESC SVT Guidelines, Eur Heart J 2020;41:655–720; Van Gelder IC et al., 2024 ESC AF Guidelines, Eur Heart J 2024;45:3314–3414.

</details>

**2.** An 81-year-old woman with permanent atrial fibrillation (5 years, left atrium 58 mm, on apixaban) and heart failure with LVEF 30 % is admitted with pneumonia. Ventricular rate is 150/min, BP 112/70 mmHg, and she has bibasal crackles with SpO2 92 % on 4 L/min oxygen. Which is the most appropriate rate-control strategy?

- A. Synchronised DC cardioversion
- B. IV verapamil
- C. IV flecainide
- D. Repeated IV metoprolol boluses targeting a heart rate below 80/min
- E. IV digoxin loading, treating the pneumonia and optimising volume status

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**E.**

**Why E:** Her AF is permanent with a 58-mm left atrium, so the goal is rate control, not rhythm. In HFrEF with acute congestion, IV digoxin (or IV amiodarone) controls the rate without depressing contractility or BP. Treating the trigger (pneumonia, hypoxaemia, volume status) is essential, and lenient rate control (< 110/min) is acceptable at first (ESC 2024).

**Why not the others:**
- A – she is not in shock, and cardioversion of long-standing permanent AF with a large atrium is unlikely to last.
- B – verapamil is negatively inotropic and contraindicated with LVEF ≤ 40%.
- C – flecainide is contraindicated in structural heart disease and HFrEF.
- D – repeated beta-blocker boluses during acute pulmonary congestion can cause decompensation, and < 80/min is a strict, not initial, target.

**Ref:** Van Gelder IC et al., 2024 ESC AF Guidelines, Eur Heart J 2024;45:3314–3414.

</details>

**3.** A 59-year-old man presents with 2 hours of palpitations and chest pain. ECG shows an irregular narrow-complex tachycardia at 180/min. BP is 72/40 mmHg, he is clammy and confused. He has no previous cardiac history. A defibrillator and an anaesthetist for sedation are available, and his potassium is 4.2 mmol/L. Which is the most appropriate immediate treatment?

- A. Synchronised cardioversion, starting at the maximum biphasic energy
- B. Unsynchronised shock at 150 J
- C. Adenosine 6 mg rapid IV bolus
- D. Amiodarone 300 mg IV over 20 minutes before any shock
- E. Anticoagulation with heparin and transoesophageal echocardiography before cardioversion

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.**

**Why A:** This tachyarrhythmia has adverse features (shock, myocardial ischaemia, reduced consciousness), so it needs immediate synchronised cardioversion under sedation or anaesthesia. ERC 2025 recommends that for AF the first synchronised shock is delivered at maximum defibrillator output rather than escalating. If it fails, give amiodarone 300 mg IV over 10–20 min and repeat the shock.

**Why not the others:**
- B – an unsynchronised shock may fall on the T wave (R-on-T) and cause VF.
- C – adenosine is for regular narrow-complex tachycardia; this rhythm is irregular.
- D – amiodarone is given after a failed shock or in stable patients; it would delay treatment of shock.
- E – instability overrides the usual anticoagulation/TOE requirements, although heparin should be started as soon as possible.

**Ref:** Soar J et al., ERC Guidelines 2025 Adult ALS, Resuscitation 2025;215(Suppl 1):110769; Van Gelder IC et al., 2024 ESC AF Guidelines, Eur Heart J 2024;45:3314–3414.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W08-D2 · Pulmonary embolism

**1.** Regarding risk stratification of acute pulmonary embolism (ESC 2019):

- A. A normotensive patient with sPESI ≥ 1, RV dysfunction on CT and an elevated troponin is classified as intermediate-high risk. — T / F
- B. A patient with systolic BP 85 mmHg for 20 minutes and RV dilatation is classified as intermediate-high risk. — T / F
- C. Full-dose systemic thrombolysis is recommended routinely for intermediate-high-risk PE. — T / F
- D. An RV/LV diameter ratio ≥ 1.0 on CT pulmonary angiography indicates RV dysfunction. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – T.**

- A (True) – evidence of RV strain on both imaging and biomarkers in a normotensive patient with sPESI ≥ 1 means intermediate-high risk; one or none is intermediate-low.
- B (False) – persistent hypotension (SBP < 90 mmHg for > 15 min, not explained by another cause) is haemodynamic instability, which makes this **high risk**.
- C (False) – routine primary thrombolysis is not recommended; in PEITHO it reduced decompensation but increased major and intracranial bleeding. Rescue thrombolysis (or catheter-directed therapy) is used if the patient deteriorates.
- D (True) – an RV/LV diameter ratio ≥ 1.0 on CTPA (or > 1.0 on echo) indicates RV dysfunction.

**Ref:** Konstantinides SV et al., 2019 ESC PE Guidelines, Eur Heart J 2020;41:543–603.

</details>

**2.** A 54-year-old woman develops dyspnoea and pleuritic pain 10 days after a knee replacement; CT confirms bilateral pulmonary emboli with an RV/LV ratio of 1.2. She is normotensive with HR 112/min and SpO2 91 % on air, and a 12-lead ECG is recorded. Each of the following ECG findings is recognised in acute pulmonary embolism EXCEPT:

- A. T-wave inversion in leads V1–V4
- B. New right bundle branch block
- C. An S1Q3T3 pattern
- D. New-onset atrial fibrillation
- E. Left ventricular hypertrophy with lateral strain pattern

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**E.**

**Why E:** LVH with a lateral strain pattern reflects chronic LV pressure overload (e.g. hypertension, aortic stenosis) and is not a sign of acute PE. Acute PE loads the right ventricle, so the ECG may show sinus tachycardia (most common), anterior T-wave inversion, QR in V1, S1Q3T3, RBBB, right axis deviation or atrial arrhythmias. Overall, the ECG is neither sensitive nor specific for PE.

**Why not the others:**
- A – T-wave inversion in V1–V4 reflects RV strain and is linked to RV dysfunction and worse outcome.
- B – new RBBB (complete or incomplete) is a sign of acute RV dilatation.
- C – the S1Q3T3 pattern is a recognised, though uncommon, sign of acute RV strain.
- D – atrial arrhythmias, including new AF, occur in acute PE.

**Ref:** Konstantinides SV et al., 2019 ESC PE Guidelines, Eur Heart J 2020;41:543–603; Shopp JD et al., Acad Emerg Med 2015;22:1127–1137.

</details>

**3.** Which of the following increase the risk of venous thromboembolism?

- A. Heterozygous factor V Leiden — T / F
- B. Haemophilia A — T / F
- C. Paroxysmal nocturnal haemoglobinuria — T / F
- D. Vitamin K deficiency — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – factor V Leiden causes resistance to activated protein C; it is the most common inherited thrombophilia in Europeans (~5-fold VTE risk when heterozygous).
- B (False) – haemophilia A is factor VIII deficiency and causes bleeding, not thrombosis.
- C (True) – in PNH, complement-mediated haemolysis and platelet activation cause thrombosis at unusual sites (hepatic, cerebral, mesenteric veins); thrombosis is the main cause of death.
- D (False) – vitamin K deficiency lowers factors II, VII, IX and X (and proteins C/S), and the net effect is a bleeding tendency.

**EDIC trap:** Low protein C and S in vitamin K deficiency does not make it prothrombotic overall – the procoagulant factors fall too.

**Ref:** Konstantinides SV et al., 2019 ESC PE Guidelines, Eur Heart J 2020;41:543–603; Hill A et al., Nat Rev Dis Primers 2017;3:17028.

</details>

**4.** Regarding reperfusion therapy in acute pulmonary embolism:

- A. The standard systemic regimen of alteplase is 100 mg over 2 hours (or an accelerated 0.6 mg/kg over 15 minutes). — T / F
- B. In PEITHO, tenecteplase in intermediate-high-risk PE reduced death or haemodynamic decompensation but increased major bleeding, including haemorrhagic stroke. — T / F
- C. When thrombolysis is given during cardiac arrest from suspected PE, resuscitation should be continued for 60–90 minutes before stopping. — T / F
- D. Surgical embolectomy or catheter-directed treatment is an alternative in high-risk PE when thrombolysis is contraindicated or has failed. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – T.**

- A (True) – ESC 2019: alteplase 100 mg over 2 h is standard; the accelerated regimen of 0.6 mg/kg over 15 min (max 50 mg) is used mainly in cardiac arrest or extreme instability.
- B (True) – in PEITHO (1006 patients) the primary outcome fell from 5.6% to 2.6%, but major extracranial bleeding (6.3% vs 1.2%) and stroke (2.4% vs 0.2%, mostly haemorrhagic) rose; 30-day mortality did not differ.
- C (True) – ERC guidance: consider continuing CPR for 60–90 min after giving a thrombolytic for PE.
- D (True) – surgical embolectomy (I C) or catheter-directed treatment (IIa C) is an alternative when thrombolysis is contraindicated or has failed.

**EDIC trap:** In intermediate-high-risk PE, HI-PEITHO and PRAGUE-26 (2026) showed that catheter-directed thrombolysis reduced early decompensation, but not mortality.

**Ref:** Meyer G et al. (PEITHO), N Engl J Med 2014;370:1402–1411; Konstantinides SV et al., 2019 ESC PE Guidelines, Eur Heart J 2020;41:543–603; Lott C et al., ERC Guidelines 2025 Special Circumstances, Resuscitation 2025;215(Suppl 1):110753.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W08-D3 · Aortic dissection and tamponade

**1.** Regarding the chest X-ray in acute aortic dissection:

- A. A normal chest X-ray excludes aortic dissection. — T / F
- B. Mediastinal widening is present in nearly all cases. — T / F
- C. Pleural effusions associated with dissection are typically right-sided. — T / F
- D. The "calcium sign" requires a separation of less than 2 mm between intimal calcification and the outer aortic wall. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – F, B – F, C – F, D – F.**

- A (False) – the chest X-ray is normal in about 10–20% of dissections, so it cannot exclude the diagnosis.
- B (False) – mediastinal widening is seen in only about 60% of cases.
- C (False) – effusions from dissection (haemothorax or reactive) are usually **left-sided**.
- D (False) – the calcium sign is displacement of intimal calcification by **> 6–10 mm** from the outer aortic contour, not < 2 mm.

**EDIC trap:** Other CXR clues are an abnormal aortic contour, apical cap, tracheal deviation to the right and a depressed left main bronchus – but CT angiography (or TOE if unstable) is the diagnostic test.

**Ref:** Erbel R et al., 2014 ESC Aortic Diseases Guidelines, Eur Heart J 2014;35:2873–2926; Mazzolai L et al., 2024 ESC Guidelines on peripheral arterial and aortic diseases, Eur Heart J 2024;45:3538–3700; Hagan PG et al. (IRAD), JAMA 2000;283:897–903.

</details>

**2.** A 70-year-old man is 4 hours out of theatre after aortic valve replacement. His chest drains, which had drained 150 mL/h, have abruptly stopped. Cardiac index has fallen from 2.4 to 1.5 L/min/m², CVP risen from 8 to 17 mmHg and PAOP from 10 to 18 mmHg, HR is 125/min and noradrenaline requirements are rising. Transthoracic windows are poor, and transoesophageal echocardiography shows a posterior clot compressing the right atrium. Which is the most appropriate management?

- A. Subxiphoid needle pericardiocentesis
- B. Emergency re-exploration (resternotomy)
- C. Increase inotropic support and observe
- D. IV furosemide to reduce filling pressures
- E. Milk and strip the drains and repeat imaging in 1 hour

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

**Why B:** Drains that suddenly stop after heavy output, with rising CVP and PAOP, falling cardiac index and rising vasopressor need, mean postoperative tamponade. TOE confirms a localised posterior clot compressing the right atrium. After cardiac surgery, tamponade is often due to localised clot, so classic signs (equal pressures, pulsus paradoxus) may be missing. The treatment is surgical re-exploration; emergency resternotomy on the ICU is part of the CALS protocol if arrest occurs.

**Why not the others:**
- A – a needle cannot remove clot, and pericardiocentesis is dangerous after sternotomy.
- C – more inotropes do not relieve the compression and only delay treatment.
- D – the patient is preload-dependent, so furosemide would worsen output.
- E – waiting an hour in worsening tamponade risks cardiac arrest.

**Ref:** STS Task Force on Resuscitation After Cardiac Surgery, Ann Thorac Surg 2017;103:1005–1020; Adler Y et al., 2015 ESC Pericardial Diseases Guidelines, Eur Heart J 2015;36:2921–2964.

</details>

**3.** A 62-year-old man with poorly controlled hypertension develops sudden severe chest pain followed 20 minutes later by left-sided weakness. On arrival (onset 90 min ago), BP is 170/90 mmHg in the right arm and 115/70 mmHg in the left arm, and there is a new early diastolic murmur. CT head shows no haemorrhage. What is the most appropriate next step?

- A. IV alteplase for acute ischaemic stroke
- B. Aspirin and ticagrelor loading
- C. CT angiography of the thoracic and abdominal aorta
- D. Coronary angiography
- E. IV unfractionated heparin

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.**

**Why C:** Chest pain followed by a neurological deficit, a 55-mmHg inter-arm BP difference and a new early diastolic (AR) murmur strongly suggest type A dissection extending into the supra-aortic vessels. About 5–10% of dissections present with stroke. The aorta must be imaged before any antithrombotic treatment – CT angiography, or bedside TOE/TTE if unstable. If confirmed: IV beta-blocker (HR < 60/min, SBP 100–120 mmHg) and emergency surgery.

**Why not the others:**
- A – alteplase in dissection can cause fatal bleeding or tamponade.
- B – dual antiplatelet loading for presumed ACS increases bleeding at surgery.
- D – coronary angiography delays diagnosis and catheter manipulation in a dissected aorta is hazardous.
- E – heparin may extend the dissection or haemopericardium.

**EDIC trap:** Always look for dissection "red flags" before thrombolysis for stroke or STEMI.

**Ref:** Mazzolai L et al., 2024 ESC Guidelines on peripheral arterial and aortic diseases, Eur Heart J 2024;45:3538–3700; Isselbacher EM et al., 2022 ACC/AHA Aortic Disease Guideline, Circulation 2022;146:e334–e482.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W08-D4 · Hypertensive emergencies

**1.** Regarding hypertensive emergencies and urgencies:

- A. A hypertensive emergency is defined by severe BP elevation associated with acute hypertension-mediated organ damage. — T / F
- B. In a hypertensive emergency without a compelling condition, BP should be normalised within the first hour. — T / F
- C. In acute aortic dissection, systolic BP should be lowered rapidly to ≤ 120 mmHg (with heart rate ≤ 60/min). — T / F
- D. Severe asymptomatic hypertension without organ damage should be treated with IV labetalol in the emergency department. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – acute organ damage (encephalopathy, retinopathy, AKI, acute HF, ACS, dissection, eclampsia, TMA) defines an emergency, not the absolute BP value.
- B (False) – without a compelling condition, MAP is lowered by no more than ~20–25% over several hours, to avoid cerebral, renal and coronary hypoperfusion (autoregulation shifted right).
- C (True) – aortic dissection is the exception: SBP ≤ 120 mmHg and HR ≤ 60/min immediately, with a beta-blocker first and then a vasodilator.
- D (False) – severe hypertension without acute organ damage is an urgency, managed by starting or intensifying oral therapy and lowering BP gradually; IV treatment is unnecessary and risky.

**EDIC trap:** Pulmonary oedema and ACS also need an immediate reduction (SBP < 140 mmHg).

**Ref:** van den Born BJH et al., ESC Council on Hypertension position document, Eur Heart J Cardiovasc Pharmacother 2019;5:37–46; McEvoy JW et al., 2024 ESC Hypertension Guidelines, Eur Heart J 2024;45:3912–4018.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W08-D5 · Valvular disease, endocarditis and cardiomyopathy

**1.** A 32-year-old woman who injects heroin presents with fever, cough and haemoptysis. CT shows multiple peripheral cavitating nodules, and echocardiography shows a 15 mm vegetation on the tricuspid valve with moderate regurgitation. Blood cultures have been taken and she is haemodynamically stable on the ward. Which statement about her condition is correct?

- A. Her prognosis is worse than that of left-sided native-valve endocarditis.
- B. Most patients with this condition require valve surgery.
- C. Candida species are the most likely cause.
- D. Staphylococcus aureus is the most likely cause.
- E. Septic pulmonary emboli are an uncommon complication.

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.**

**Why D:** Tricuspid endocarditis in people who inject drugs is caused by *S. aureus* in 60–90% of cases (MRSA in some regions). The clinical picture is typical: fever, cough and haemoptysis from septic pulmonary emboli, with cavitating nodules on CT. Other causes are streptococci, *Pseudomonas* and *Candida*.

**Why not the others:**
- A – the prognosis is relatively good (in-hospital mortality < 10%), better than left-sided NVE.
- B – most patients are treated medically; ESC 2023 surgery is for right HF from severe TR not responding to diuretics, persistent bacteraemia or difficult organisms, or vegetations > 20 mm with recurrent PE.
- C – *Candida* is an uncommon cause.
- E – septic pulmonary emboli are common, as here.

**EDIC trap:** Her 15-mm vegetation does not meet the > 20 mm threshold for right-sided surgery.

**Ref:** Delgado V et al., 2023 ESC Endocarditis Guidelines, Eur Heart J 2023;44:3948–4042.

</details>

**2.** An 84-year-old man with known severe aortic stenosis (valve area 0.6 cm², mean gradient 52 mmHg, preserved LV function) undergoes hip fracture fixation under spinal anaesthesia. Shortly after the block his BP falls to 70/40 mmHg with HR 105/min (sinus) and he becomes nauseated and pale. The surgeons are waiting to start. Which drug is most appropriate?

- A. Ephedrine 6 mg boluses
- B. Phenylephrine (or metaraminol) boluses followed by an infusion
- C. Glyceryl trinitrate infusion
- D. Dobutamine infusion
- E. Adrenaline 1 mg IV bolus

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

**Why B:** In severe AS the hypertrophied LV has a fixed stroke volume and depends on diastolic pressure for coronary perfusion. Spinal anaesthesia causes vasodilation, and hypotension then leads to ischaemia and a downward spiral; tachycardia shortens diastole and makes it worse. A pure α-agonist (phenylephrine, metaraminol) or noradrenaline restores coronary perfusion pressure without raising heart rate. Keep sinus rhythm and treat AF promptly.

**Why not the others:**
- A – ephedrine raises heart rate (β effect), which is unwanted at HR 105/min.
- C – glyceryl trinitrate is a vasodilator and can cause cardiovascular collapse.
- D – dobutamine causes tachycardia and vasodilation.
- E – 1 mg of adrenaline is an arrest dose and would cause severe tachycardia and hypertension.

**Ref:** Praz F et al., 2025 ESC/EACTS Valvular Heart Disease Guidelines, Eur Heart J 2025; Halvorsen S et al., 2022 ESC Guidelines on non-cardiac surgery, Eur Heart J 2022;43:3826–3924.

</details>

**3.** Regarding hypertrophic cardiomyopathy (HCM):

- A. Dynamic LV outflow tract obstruction is worsened by hypovolaemia, vasodilators and positive inotropes. — T / F
- B. Digoxin is useful to relieve symptoms of obstructive HCM. — T / F
- C. Mavacamten, a cardiac myosin inhibitor, may be used for symptomatic obstructive HCM. — T / F
- D. In shock caused by dynamic LV outflow tract obstruction, dobutamine is the first-line drug. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – a smaller LV cavity (hypovolaemia, vasodilators) or stronger contraction (inotropes) increases systolic anterior motion of the mitral valve and the LVOT gradient.
- B (False) – digoxin is a positive inotrope and is avoided in obstructive HCM, as are vasodilators.
- C (True) – ESC 2023: beta-blockers first-line, then (or added) the myosin inhibitor mavacamten or disopyramide; septal reduction therapy for refractory symptoms.
- D (False) – shock from dynamic LVOT obstruction is treated with fluids, a pure vasoconstrictor (phenylephrine/noradrenaline), beta-blockade and stopping inotropes; dobutamine worsens the obstruction.

**EDIC trap:** Dynamic LVOT obstruction can also occur without HCM in hypovolaemic, catecholamine-treated septic patients – look for SAM on echo.

**Ref:** Arbelo E et al., 2023 ESC Cardiomyopathy Guidelines, Eur Heart J 2023;44:3503–3626.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W09-D1 · Advanced life support: drugs and access

**1.** Regarding drug therapy during adult advanced life support (ERC 2025):

- A. In a shockable rhythm, adrenaline 1 mg is given after the third shock and then every 3–5 minutes. — T / F
- B. Amiodarone 300 mg is given after three shocks, with a further 150 mg after the fifth shock. — T / F
- C. Lidocaine 100 mg is an acceptable alternative if amiodarone is unavailable. — T / F
- D. In a non-shockable rhythm, adrenaline should be withheld until two cycles of CPR have been completed. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – in VF/pVT, adrenaline 1 mg is given after the 3rd shock and then every 3–5 min. This is unchanged since ERC 2015/2021.
- B (True) – amiodarone 300 mg after 3 shocks, with a further 150 mg after the 5th shock.
- C (True) – lidocaine 1 mg/kg (100 mg), with a further 50 mg after the 5th shock, is the alternative if amiodarone is unavailable. ALPS showed similar outcomes.
- D (False) – in PEA/asystole, give adrenaline 1 mg **as soon as IV/IO access is obtained**. Earlier adrenaline is associated with better outcomes in non-shockable rhythms.

**EDIC trap:** the timing of adrenaline depends on the rhythm: immediately in non-shockable, after the 3rd shock in shockable.

**Ref:** Soar J, Böttiger BW, Carli P, et al. ERC Guidelines 2025 Adult ALS, Resuscitation 2025;215(Suppl 1):110769; Kudenchuk PJ, et al. ALPS, N Engl J Med 2016;374:1711–1722.

</details>

**2.** A 68-year-old man is in refractory VF after an out-of-hospital arrest. Paramedics have failed two attempts at peripheral IV cannulation over 2 minutes; three shocks have been delivered and high-quality CPR with mechanical compressions is ongoing; an i-gel is in place with a capnography trace. Which is the most appropriate action?

- A. Give adrenaline 3 mg via the tracheal tube
- B. Give adrenaline 1 mg intramuscularly
- C. Interrupt CPR to insert a femoral central venous catheter
- D. Insert an intraosseous needle and give double the IV dose of adrenaline
- E. Insert an intraosseous needle (proximal humerus or tibia) and give the standard IV doses

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**E.**

**Why E:** ERC 2025 recommends trying IV access first and using the **intraosseous route** (proximal humerus or tibia) if IV access is not rapidly achievable. IO drug doses are the **same as IV doses**. PARAMEDIC-3 and IVIO found no survival benefit from an IO-first strategy, but IO remains the standard fallback.

**Why not the others:**
- A – tracheal drug administration is no longer recommended because absorption is unpredictable.
- B – IM adrenaline is not used in cardiac arrest, since there is no muscle perfusion.
- C – a femoral central line interrupts CPR, takes too long and is not faster than IO.
- D – IO doses are not doubled.

**Ref:** Soar J, et al. ERC 2025 Adult ALS, Resuscitation 2025;215(Suppl 1):110769; Couper K, et al. PARAMEDIC-3, N Engl J Med 2025;392:336–348.

</details>

**3.** Regarding vascular access during cardiac arrest:

- A. In PARAMEDIC-3, an intraosseous-first strategy improved 30-day survival compared with an IV-first strategy. — T / F
- B. Drugs given via a peripheral vein should be followed by a flush of about 20 mL. — T / F
- C. If neither IV nor IO access is available, the tracheal route is recommended for adrenaline. — T / F
- D. Proximal humeral IO sites generally achieve higher flow rates than proximal tibial sites. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – F, B – T, C – F, D – T.**

- A (False) – PARAMEDIC-3 (n = 6082) found no difference in 30-day survival (4.5% IO vs 5.1% IV). IVIO (Denmark) also found no benefit.
- B (True) – a flush of about 20 mL, with limb elevation, speeds delivery to the central circulation.
- C (False) – the tracheal route is no longer recommended for adrenaline.
- D (True) – the humerus is closer to the central circulation and allows higher flows than the tibia, although no outcome benefit by site has been shown.

**Ref:** Couper K, et al. PARAMEDIC-3, N Engl J Med 2025;392:336–348; Vallentin MF, et al. IVIO, N Engl J Med 2025;392:349–360; Soar J, et al. ERC 2025 ALS, Resuscitation 2025;215(Suppl 1):110769.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W09-D2 · Post-resuscitation care

**1.** Regarding the evidence base for post-cardiac arrest care:

- A. In TTM2, targeted hypothermia at 33 °C improved 6-month survival compared with normothermia and early treatment of fever. — T / F
- B. In BOX, a restrictive oxygen target reduced death or severe disability compared with a liberal target. — T / F
- C. In TOMAHAWK, immediate coronary angiography in resuscitated patients without ST elevation did not reduce 30-day mortality. — T / F
- D. ERC–ESICM 2025 recommends routine prophylactic antibiotics after ROSC. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – F, B – F, C – T, D – F.**

- A (False) – TTM2 (n = 1900) found no difference in mortality or functional outcome, and hypothermia caused more arrhythmias. ERC–ESICM 2025 recommends active fever prevention (≤ 37.5 °C) for 36–72 h.
- B (False) – BOX compared PaO₂ targets of 9–10 vs 13–14 kPa (and MAP 63 vs 77 mmHg) and found no difference in death or severe disability.
- C (True) – TOMAHAWK, like COACT, was neutral and showed a trend towards harm. Delayed or selective angiography is appropriate without ST elevation.
- D (False) – prophylactic antibiotics are not routinely recommended, even though ANTHARTIC showed a lower early pneumonia rate.

**Ref:** Nolan JP, Sandroni C, Cariou A, et al. ERC–ESICM Guidelines 2025: Post-Resuscitation Care, Resuscitation 2025;215(Suppl 1):110809; Desch S, et al. TOMAHAWK, N Engl J Med 2021;385:2544–2553; Schmidt H, et al. BOX (oxygen), N Engl J Med 2022;387:1467–1476.

</details>

**2.** A 61-year-old woman has ROSC after 12 minutes of in-hospital VF arrest due to hyperkalaemia, which has been corrected. She is intubated, ventilated with FiO2 0.6 and remains comatose. SpO2 100 %, PaO2 35 kPa, PaCO2 3.8 kPa, MAP 72 mmHg, temperature 36.9 °C. The ECG shows no ST elevation. What is the most appropriate next step?

- A. Reduce FiO2 to target SpO2 94–98 % and adjust ventilation to normocapnia
- B. Maintain FiO2 0.6 for 24 hours to protect the brain from hypoxia
- C. Increase minute ventilation to a PaCO2 of 3.5 kPa to reduce intracranial pressure
- D. Actively cool to 33 °C for 24 hours
- E. Arrange immediate coronary angiography

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.**

**Why A:** ERC–ESICM 2025 recommends avoiding both hypoxaemia and hyperoxaemia after ROSC. Once SpO₂ can be measured reliably, titrate FiO₂ to **SpO₂ 94–98% (PaO₂ 10–13 kPa)**. Target **normocapnia (PaCO₂ 4.7–6.0 kPa)**, because hypocapnia (here 3.8 kPa) causes cerebral vasoconstriction. Correcting the PaO₂ of 35 kPa and the PaCO₂ is the immediate priority.

**Why not the others:**
- B – keeping FiO₂ 0.6 maintains harmful hyperoxaemia.
- C – hyperventilation to 3.5 kPa worsens cerebral ischaemia.
- D – temperature control means preventing fever (≤ 37.5 °C), not cooling to 33 °C (TTM2).
- E – immediate angiography is for ST elevation or strongly suspected occlusion, and this arrest had a non-coronary cause (hyperkalaemia).

**Ref:** Nolan JP, et al. ERC–ESICM Guidelines 2025: Post-Resuscitation Care, Resuscitation 2025;215(Suppl 1):110809; Dankiewicz J, et al. TTM2, N Engl J Med 2021;384:2283–2294.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W09-D3 · Temperature control after cardiac arrest

**1.** A 55-year-old man is being cooled to 33 °C (for example in a trial or local protocol). Regarding the physiological effects of mild induced hypothermia:

- A. Potassium shifts into cells during cooling and may rebound to hyperkalaemia during rewarming. — T / F
- B. Reduced hepatic clearance increases plasma concentrations of sedatives and opioids, which can delay awakening. — T / F
- C. Shivering increases metabolic rate and oxygen consumption and should be controlled. — T / F
- D. Hypothermia typically causes sinus tachycardia and QT shortening. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – cooling shifts K⁺ into cells and causes cold diuresis, leading to hypokalaemia; rewarming can cause rebound hyperkalaemia. Rewarm slowly (0.25–0.5 °C/h).
- B (True) – cytochrome P450 activity falls by about 7–20% per °C below 37 °C. Propofol, fentanyl and midazolam accumulate, which delays awakening and confounds neuroprognostication.
- C (True) – shivering increases metabolic rate and O₂ consumption. Control it with sedation, opioids, magnesium, surface counter-warming and, if needed, NMB.
- D (False) – hypothermia causes **bradycardia**, PR/QT **prolongation** and J (Osborn) waves.

Other complications are infection (pneumonia), coagulopathy, hyperglycaemia and diuresis. TTM2 showed no benefit of 33 °C over fever prevention.

**Ref:** Polderman KH. Mechanisms of action, physiological effects, and complications of hypothermia, Crit Care Med 2009;37(7 Suppl):S186–S202; Dankiewicz J, et al. TTM2, N Engl J Med 2021;384:2283–2294.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W09-D4 · Neuroprognostication after cardiac arrest

**1.** A 66-year-old woman remains comatose after an out-of-hospital cardiac arrest. Regarding prognostication of poor neurological outcome (ERC–ESICM 2025):

- A. Bilaterally absent N20 waves on somatosensory evoked potentials (≥ 24 h after ROSC) predict poor outcome. — T / F
- B. A neuron-specific enolase above 60 µg/L at 48 and/or 72 hours supports a poor outcome. — T / F
- C. Multimodal prognostication may be performed as early as 12 hours after ROSC. — T / F
- D. An absent motor response to pain at 24 hours is sufficient on its own to predict poor outcome. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – F.**

- A (True) – bilaterally absent N20 SSEP waves at ≥ 24 h after ROSC are one of the six predictors of poor outcome.
- B (True) – NSE > 60 µg/L at 48 and/or 72 h supports a poor outcome, provided haemolysis has been excluded.
- C (False) – prognostication starts **≥ 72 h** after ROSC in patients who are still unconscious (GCS motor ≤ 5 in 2025; ≤ 3 in 2021), after excluding confounders such as sedation, hypothermia and metabolic causes. Assessing at 12 h risks a self-fulfilling prophecy.
- D (False) – motor response alone is unreliable and is used only to select patients. Poor outcome requires ≥ 2 of the six predictors: absent pupillary and corneal reflexes, absent N20, highly malignant EEG, NSE > 60 µg/L, status myoclonus ≤ 72 h, and diffuse anoxic injury on CT/MRI.

**Ref:** Nolan JP, et al. ERC–ESICM Guidelines 2025: Post-Resuscitation Care, Resuscitation 2025;215(Suppl 1):110809; Sandroni C, et al. Prognostication after cardiac arrest, Crit Care 2018;22:150.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W09-D5 · ECPR and special circumstances

**1.** A 35-year-old man is found in a snowdrift in VF with a core temperature of 27 °C.

- A. Adrenaline is withheld until the core temperature is ≥ 30 °C. — T / F
- B. A maximum of 3 shocks should be given until the temperature is > 30 °C. — T / F
- C. Rewarming with VA-ECMO is preferred while in cardiac arrest. — T / F
- D. Once the temperature is 30–35 °C, adrenaline is given every 3–5 minutes. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – < 30 °C: no adrenaline.
- B (True) – Max 3 shocks below 30 °C.
- C (True) – Extracorporeal rewarming (VA-ECMO) in arrest.
- D (False) – 30–35 °C: double the interval – every 6–10 min.

**EDIC trap:** Hypothermia: adrenaline intervals are doubled, not standard.

**Ref:** ERC Guidelines 2025 – special circumstances.

</details>

**2.** A woman at 34 weeks of gestation has a cardiac arrest on the labour ward. CPR is under way with manual left uterine displacement. What else should be prioritised?

- A. Wait for 20 minutes of CPR before considering delivery
- B. Prepare for resuscitative hysterotomy as early as possible if there is no rapid ROSC
- C. Withhold adrenaline because of uterine perfusion
- D. Move her to the operating theatre before any delivery is considered
- E. Tilt her 15° to the right

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

Resuscitative hysterotomy should be done as early as possible (decision early, aim within ~5 min) when there is no rapid ROSC; it helps maternal resuscitation and the fetus. Do it where she is; manual left uterine displacement relieves aortocaval compression.

**EDIC trap:** The hysterotomy is for the mother's resuscitation as much as for the baby.

**Ref:** ERC Guidelines 2025 – cardiac arrest in pregnancy.

</details>

**3.** Regarding extracorporeal CPR (ECPR) for refractory out-of-hospital cardiac arrest:

- A. The ARREST trial was stopped early because of better survival with ECPR in refractory VF. — T / F
- B. The Prague OHCA trial showed a significant improvement in its primary endpoint. — T / F
- C. INCEPTION showed no significant difference in 30-day survival with a favourable neurological outcome. — T / F
- D. ERC 2025 suggests ECPR for all refractory arrests irrespective of the system. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – ARREST (n = 30): 43 vs 7 % survival.
- B (False) – Prague OHCA: 31.5 vs 22.0 % – not significant (better 30-day neurological outcome).
- C (True) – INCEPTION: 20 vs 16 % (NS), long low-flow times.
- D (False) – Only in selected patients in systems able to deliver it.

**EDIC trap:** ECPR works only in a system – selection and short low-flow time.

**Ref:** Yannopoulos D, et al. ARREST. *Lancet* 2020; Belohlavek J, et al. *JAMA* 2022; Suverein MM, et al. INCEPTION. *N Engl J Med* 2023.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W10-D1 · ARDS definition and transfusion-related lung injury

**1.** Regarding the 2023 Global definition of ARDS:

- A. Non-intubated patients receiving high-flow nasal oxygen at ≥ 30 L/min can be diagnosed with ARDS. — T / F
- B. An SpO2/FiO2 ratio ≤ 315 (with SpO2 ≤ 97 %) can be used instead of PaO2/FiO2. — T / F
- C. Bilateral opacities may be identified with lung ultrasound by a trained operator. — T / F
- D. A pulmonary artery occlusion pressure < 18 mmHg is required to exclude cardiogenic oedema. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – non-intubated patients on HFNO ≥ 30 L/min (or NIV/CPAP with PEEP ≥ 5 cmH2O) with P/F ≤ 300 or S/F ≤ 315 qualify; they are not graded by severity.
- B (True) – SpO2/FiO2 ≤ 315 may replace PaO2/FiO2, but only when SpO2 ≤ 97 % (flat part of the dissociation curve above that).
- C (True) – bilateral B-lines and/or consolidations on lung ultrasound by a trained operator are accepted, alongside chest X-ray or CT.
- D (False) – the PAOP criterion belonged to AECC 1994 and was abandoned in Berlin 2012; oedema must only be not primarily explained by cardiac failure or fluid overload, and ARDS may coexist with raised hydrostatic pressure.

**EDIC trap:** the Global definition keeps the Berlin timing (≤ 1 week) and adds a resource-limited variant without PEEP or flow requirements.

**Ref:** Matthay MA et al., Am J Respir Crit Care Med 2024;209:37–47; ARDS Definition Task Force, JAMA 2012;307:2526–2533.

</details>

**2.** A 45-year-old woman with a postpartum haemorrhage receives 4 units of red cells and 4 units of fresh frozen plasma over 2 hours with a neutral fluid balance. Ninety minutes after the last unit she develops fever, hypotension and hypoxaemia (PaO2/FiO2 120 mmHg) with bilateral infiltrates. Echocardiography shows normal LV function and a small, collapsing IVC; BNP is normal. What is the most likely diagnosis?

- A. Transfusion-related acute lung injury (TRALI)
- B. Transfusion-associated circulatory overload (TACO)
- C. Anaphylactic transfusion reaction
- D. Aspiration pneumonitis
- E. Acute haemolytic transfusion reaction

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.**

**Why A:** TRALI is new acute hypoxaemia with bilateral infiltrates within 6 h of transfusion without evidence of left atrial hypertension as the main cause. Fever and hypotension, normal LV function, a collapsing IVC and a normal BNP after plasma-rich products (donor anti-HLA/HNA antibodies) fit TRALI.

**Why not the others:**
- B – TACO typically causes hypertension, raised BNP, a plethoric IVC and a positive fluid balance.
- C – anaphylaxis presents with bronchospasm, urticaria and angio-oedema, usually within minutes.
- D – aspiration has no link to transfusion timing and is often dependent or unilateral.
- E – acute haemolysis causes haemoglobinuria, DIC and AKI rather than isolated lung injury.

**EDIC trap:** in the 2019 redefinition, TRALI type II has an ARDS risk factor but stable respiratory status in the 12 h before transfusion; if ARDS was already worsening, it is classified as ARDS.

**Ref:** Vlaar APJ et al., Transfusion 2019;59:2465–2476; Vlaar APJ et al., Intensive Care Med 2020;46:673–696.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W10-D2 · Protective ventilation

**1.** A 165-cm-tall woman (actual weight 95 kg) with ARDS is intubated. Approximately what tidal volume corresponds to 6 mL/kg predicted body weight?

- A. 300 mL
- B. 340 mL
- C. 400 mL
- D. 450 mL
- E. 570 mL

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

PBW (women) = 45.5 + 0.91 × (165 − 152.4) ≈ 57 kg → 6 mL/kg ≈ 340 mL. Actual weight is irrelevant.

**EDIC trap:** Using actual weight in obesity gives dangerously large volumes.

**Ref:** ARDS Network. *N Engl J Med* 2000;342:1301–1308.

</details>

**2.** A passive patient in volume control has VT 420 mL, PEEP 10 cmH₂O and a plateau pressure of 26 cmH₂O. Which pair is correct?

- A. Driving pressure 16 cmH₂O; compliance ≈ 26 mL/cmH₂O
- B. Driving pressure 26 cmH₂O; compliance ≈ 16 mL/cmH₂O
- C. Driving pressure 16 cmH₂O; compliance ≈ 42 mL/cmH₂O
- D. Driving pressure 10 cmH₂O; compliance ≈ 42 mL/cmH₂O
- E. Driving pressure cannot be calculated without peak pressure

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.**

ΔP = Pplat − PEEP = 16; Crs = VT/ΔP = 420/16 ≈ 26 mL/cmH₂O.

**EDIC trap:** Peak − plateau reflects resistance, not compliance.

**Ref:** Amato MBP, et al. *N Engl J Med* 2015;372:747–755.

</details>

**3.** Regarding lung-protective ventilation:

- A. In ARMA, 6 mL/kg PBW reduced mortality compared with 12 mL/kg (31.0 vs 39.8 %). — T / F
- B. ESICM 2023 strongly recommends VT 4–8 mL/kg PBW in ARDS. — T / F
- C. Targeting driving pressure has been validated in a randomised trial. — T / F
- D. Permissive hypercapnia is safe in patients with raised intracranial pressure. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – F.**

- A (True) – ARMA – stopped early for benefit.
- B (True) – Strong recommendation.
- C (False) – ΔP comes from observational analysis (Amato 2015) – not RCT-validated.
- D (False) – Caution in ICH and RV failure.

**EDIC trap:** ΔP is a strong predictor, not a proven target.

**Ref:** ARDS Network 2000; Grasselli G, et al. ESICM ARDS guidelines. *Intensive Care Med* 2023;49:727–759.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W10-D3 · PEEP and recruitment

**1.** Regarding PEEP and recruitment trials in ARDS:

- A. In ART, maximal recruitment with PEEP titration increased mortality. — T / F
- B. In the Briel meta-analysis, higher PEEP was associated with benefit only at P/F ≤ 200 mmHg. — T / F
- C. ESICM 2023 recommends a specific method of PEEP titration. — T / F
- D. In EPVent-2, oesophageal-pressure-guided PEEP improved outcome compared with empirical high PEEP. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – F.**

- A (True) – ART – 55.3 vs 49.3 %, more barotrauma.
- B (True) – Mild ARDS – trend towards harm.
- C (False) – No recommendation on PEEP level or titration method.
- D (False) – No difference.

**EDIC trap:** Recruitment manoeuvres: prolonged RM strongly against; brief RM conditionally against routine use.

**Ref:** ART investigators. *JAMA* 2017;318:1335–1345; Briel M, et al. *JAMA* 2010;303:865–873.

</details>

**2.** A 60-year-old man has moderate–severe ARDS with P/F 130 mmHg on VT 6 mL/kg PBW. Which approach is most consistent with ESICM 2023 and ATS 2024?

- A. A prolonged recruitment manoeuvre at 40 cmH₂O for 40 s, then decremental PEEP titration
- B. A higher-PEEP strategy without prolonged recruitment manoeuvres
- C. Routine recruitment manoeuvres every 4 hours
- D. PEEP 5 cmH₂O to minimise haemodynamic effects
- E. PEEP guided only by peak airway pressure

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

ATS 2024 conditionally suggests higher PEEP without RM in moderate–severe ARDS; ESICM 2023 strongly recommends against prolonged high-pressure RM and makes no recommendation on PEEP titration.

**EDIC trap:** ESICM and ATS agree on avoiding prolonged RM.

**Ref:** Grasselli G, et al. *Intensive Care Med* 2023; Qadir N, et al. ATS ARDS guideline. *Am J Respir Crit Care Med* 2024.

</details>

**3.** Regarding the classic higher-PEEP trials:

- A. ALVEOLI found no reduction in mortality with higher PEEP. — T / F
- B. LOVS reduced refractory hypoxaemia and the need for rescue therapies. — T / F
- C. EXPRESS increased ventilator-free days. — T / F
- D. All three trials showed a significant reduction in mortality. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – ALVEOLI – no mortality benefit.
- B (True) – LOVS – less refractory hypoxaemia.
- C (True) – EXPRESS – more ventilator- and organ-failure-free days.
- D (False) – None showed lower mortality on its own.

**EDIC trap:** Higher PEEP improves oxygenation and secondary endpoints, not mortality.

**Ref:** Brower RG, et al. *N Engl J Med* 2004; Meade MO, et al. *JAMA* 2008; Mercat A, et al. *JAMA* 2008.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W10-D4 · Rescue therapies in severe ARDS

**1.** A 52-year-old man has influenza A pneumonia complicated by ARDS, intubated 30 hours ago. He is ventilated with VT 6 mL/kg predicted body weight, PEEP 14 cmH2O, FiO2 0.8; plateau pressure 29 cmH2O, PaO2/FiO2 85 mmHg, pH 7.27, PaCO2 7.9 kPa. He is deeply sedated, haemodynamically stable on low-dose noradrenaline, and has not yet been turned prone. What is the most appropriate next step?

- A. High-frequency oscillatory ventilation
- B. Inhaled nitric oxide as a definitive therapy
- C. Immediate referral for VV-ECMO without other changes
- D. Prone positioning for at least 16 hours per day
- E. A staircase recruitment manoeuvre to PEEP 35 cmH2O followed by decremental PEEP titration

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.**

**Why D:** After intubation and stabilisation he has P/F 85 mmHg on FiO2 0.8 and PEEP 14 – the PROSEVA population, in which proning ≥ 16 h/day reduced 28-day mortality (16.0 vs 32.8 %). ESICM 2023 strongly recommends it (high certainty). He does not yet meet EOLIA criteria (e.g. P/F < 80 for > 6 h); ECMO is considered if he fails proning.

**Why not the others:**
- A – HFOV increased mortality in OSCILLATE and had no effect in OSCAR.
- B – inhaled NO improves oxygenation transiently without survival benefit and may cause AKI.
- C – ECMO referral is premature before proning and before EOLIA criteria are met.
- E – prolonged high-pressure RMs with decremental PEEP increased mortality in ART and are strongly recommended against.

**Ref:** Guérin C et al. (PROSEVA), N Engl J Med 2013;368:2159–2168; Grasselli G et al., Intensive Care Med 2023;49:727–759; Combes A et al. (EOLIA), N Engl J Med 2018;378:1965–1975.

</details>

**2.** According to the ESICM 2023 ARDS guidelines:

- A. Prone positioning is recommended for patients with moderate–severe ARDS. — T / F
