- B. Prolonged high-pressure recruitment manoeuvres are recommended against. — T / F
- C. Inhaled nitric oxide is recommended because it improves survival. — T / F
- D. Routine continuous infusion of neuromuscular blocking agents is recommended in moderate–severe ARDS. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – F.**

- A (True) – prone positioning for moderate–severe ARDS (P/F < 150, PEEP ≥ 5), early and ≥ 16 h, is a strong recommendation with high certainty (PROSEVA, meta-analyses).
- B (True) – prolonged high-pressure RMs (≥ 35 cmH2O for ≥ 1 min) are strongly recommended against after ART showed higher 28-day mortality; brief RMs are conditionally suggested against for routine use.
- C (False) – iNO is not recommended; it improves oxygenation transiently but does not reduce mortality and may cause AKI.
- D (False) – after ROSE (no benefit of cisatracurium versus light sedation) ESICM recommends against routine continuous NMBA infusion (strong, moderate); NMBA may still be used for specific indications such as refractory asynchrony.

**EDIC trap:** ATS 2024 differs on NMBA (conditional for in early severe ARDS).

**Ref:** Grasselli G et al., Intensive Care Med 2023;49:727–759; Moss M et al. (ROSE), N Engl J Med 2019;380:1997–2008; Cavalcanti AB et al. (ART), JAMA 2017;318:1335–1345.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W10-D5 · ESICM ARDS guidelines 2023

**1.** According to the ESICM 2023 ARDS guidelines:

- A. Prone positioning for ≥ 16 h/day is recommended in ARDS with P/F < 150 mmHg. — T / F
- B. Routine continuous infusion of neuromuscular blockers is recommended in moderate–severe ARDS. — T / F
- C. VV-ECMO is recommended for selected patients with severe ARDS according to EOLIA criteria, in an ECMO centre. — T / F
- D. ECCO₂R is recommended to allow ultra-protective ventilation. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – Strong recommendation, early.
- B (False) – Strongly **against** routine NMBA infusion.
- C (True) – Strong recommendation (EOLIA criteria, experienced centre).
- D (False) – Against ECCO₂R outside RCTs.

**EDIC trap:** Prone and ECMO – for; routine NMBA and ECCO₂R – against.

**Ref:** Grasselli G, et al. *Intensive Care Med* 2023;49:727–759.

</details>

**2.** A 50-year-old woman with non-COVID pneumonia has acute hypoxaemic respiratory failure (SpO₂ 88 % on 15 L/min via a reservoir mask, RR 32/min) and no indication for immediate intubation. ESICM 2023 recommends:

- A. Conventional oxygen therapy
- B. High-flow nasal oxygen
- C. Immediate intubation
- D. NIV rather than HFNO (strong recommendation)
- E. Helmet CPAP as first line (strong recommendation)

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

Strong recommendation: HFNO over conventional O₂ to reduce intubation. No recommendation on NIV vs HFNO in non-COVID AHRF (CPAP/NIV are conditionally suggested in COVID-19).

**EDIC trap:** ESICM gives no recommendation on NIV vs HFNO outside COVID-19.

**Ref:** Grasselli G, et al. *Intensive Care Med* 2023.

</details>

**3.** Which treatment does ATS 2024 conditionally recommend in ARDS, whereas ESICM 2023 makes no such recommendation?

- A. Prone positioning
- B. Low tidal volume
- C. Corticosteroids
- D. VV-ECMO
- E. Routine prolonged recruitment manoeuvres

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.**

ATS 2024 conditionally recommends corticosteroids, NMBA in early severe ARDS and higher PEEP without RM. ESICM 2023 does not recommend corticosteroids for ARDS in general.

**EDIC trap:** Know where the two guidelines differ.

**Ref:** Qadir N, et al. *Am J Respir Crit Care Med* 2024;209:24–36.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W11-D1 · ECMO for respiratory failure

**1.** A 52-year-old man (BMI 38 kg/m²) with severe ARDS after influenza pneumonia has been mechanically ventilated for 12 days, with plateau pressures of 32–34 cmH2O for most of that time. Despite prone positioning, his PaO2/FiO2 is 55 mmHg and pH 7.22 with PaCO2 9 kPa. Echocardiography shows normal biventricular function, and he has no other organ failure. Which feature most argues against VV-ECMO in this patient?

- A. His age
- B. A PaO2/FiO2 of 55 mmHg despite prone positioning
- C. Twelve days of high-pressure mechanical ventilation before referral
- D. Hypercapnic acidosis with pH 7.22
- E. Obesity with a BMI of 38 kg/m²

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.**

**Why C:** More than 7 days of high-pressure/high-FiO₂ ventilation before ECMO is associated with poor survival (a component of the RESP score) because established fibroproliferative injury is less reversible. ELSO lists mechanical ventilation at high settings (Pplat > 30 cmH₂O, FiO₂ > 0.9) for > 7–10 days as a relative contraindication, and EOLIA excluded patients ventilated > 7 days.

**Why not the others:**

- A – Age 52 is not a contraindication; age only lowers the RESP score at older ages.
- B – P/F 55 mmHg despite proning is an indication, close to the EOLIA thresholds.
- D – Hypercapnic acidosis despite optimisation is itself an EOLIA-type indication (pH < 7.25, PaCO₂ ≥ 60 mmHg).
- E – Obesity is not a contraindication and is not associated with worse ECMO outcome.

**EDIC trap:** the "7-day rule" – time on injurious ventilation matters more than the severity of hypoxaemia.

**Ref:** Tonna JE, et al. ELSO VV-ECMO guideline. *ASAIO J* 2021;67:601–610. Schmidt M, et al. RESP score. *Am J Respir Crit Care Med* 2014;189:1374–1382. Combes A, et al. EOLIA. *N Engl J Med* 2018;378:1965–1975.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W11-D2 · Pulse oximetry

**1.** Regarding sources of error in pulse oximetry:

- A. In patients with darker skin pigmentation, pulse oximetry more often overestimates arterial saturation, missing occult hypoxaemia. — T / F
- B. IV methylene blue causes a transient falsely low SpO2. — T / F
- C. Severe anaemia (haemoglobin 70 g/L) alone causes large SpO2 errors at normal saturations. — T / F
- D. Fetal haemoglobin causes major errors in SpO2. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – F.**

- A (True) – Occult hypoxaemia (SaO₂ < 88 % with SpO₂ 92–96 %) was about three times more frequent in Black than in White patients (Sjoding 2020); the FDA 2025 draft guidance requires more diverse validation.
- B (True) – Methylene blue absorbs strongly at 660 nm and causes a transient falsely low SpO₂.
- C (False) – Oximetry measures the ratio of oxy- to total haemoglobin, so moderate anaemia causes little error at normal saturations; accuracy worsens only with severe anaemia combined with hypoxaemia.
- D (False) – HbF has absorption spectra similar to HbA and causes negligible error.

**EDIC trap:** dyes and dyshaemoglobins distort SpO₂; anaemia and HbF largely do not.

**Ref:** Sjoding MW, et al. Racial bias in pulse oximetry measurement. *N Engl J Med* 2020;383:2477–2478. Jubran A. Pulse oximetry. *Crit Care* 2015;19:272.

</details>

**2.** Regarding the principles of pulse oximetry:

- A. It uses light at about 660 nm (red) and 940 nm (infrared). — T / F
- B. Deoxyhaemoglobin absorbs more red light (660 nm) than oxyhaemoglobin. — T / F
- C. Its calibration is empirical, derived from healthy volunteers desaturated to about 70–80 %, so accuracy declines below this range. — T / F
- D. Venous pulsations (e.g. severe tricuspid regurgitation) can produce falsely low readings. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – T.**

- A (True) – Two wavelengths are used: ~660 nm (red) and ~940 nm (infrared); the ratio of pulsatile (AC) to non-pulsatile (DC) absorbance ("ratio of ratios") is converted to SpO₂.
- B (True) – Deoxyhaemoglobin absorbs more red light, oxyhaemoglobin more infrared light.
- C (True) – Calibration curves are empirical, from healthy volunteers desaturated to ~70–80 %; values below this are extrapolated and less accurate.
- D (True) – The device assumes all pulsatile absorbance is arterial; venous pulsation (severe TR, congestion, tourniquet) lowers the reading.

**EDIC trap:** carboxyhaemoglobin is read as oxyhaemoglobin (falsely high); methaemoglobin drives readings towards ~85 %.

**Ref:** Jubran A. Pulse oximetry. *Crit Care* 2015;19:272. Chan ED, et al. Pulse oximetry: basic principles and limitations. *Respir Med* 2013;107:789–799.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W11-D3 · Non-invasive ventilation

**1.** Regarding non-invasive ventilation (NIV) in acute respiratory failure (ERS/ATS 2017):

- A. In acute hypercapnic exacerbations of COPD (pH ≤ 7.35), NIV reduces the need for intubation and mortality. — T / F
- B. NIV is contraindicated in acute cardiogenic pulmonary oedema. — T / F
- C. NIV is recommended to treat established respiratory failure after extubation. — T / F
- D. NIV is recommended as first-line therapy for de novo hypoxaemic respiratory failure. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – F.**

- A (True) – In hypercapnic COPD exacerbation with pH ≤ 7.35, NIV reduces intubation and mortality (strong recommendation, high certainty).
- B (False) – Bilevel NIV or CPAP is recommended in cardiogenic pulmonary oedema (strong, moderate certainty), not contraindicated.
- C (False) – NIV for established post-extubation failure may delay reintubation and increased ICU mortality (Esteban 2004); it is suggested against, and used only to prevent failure in high-risk patients.
- D (False) – No recommendation was made for de novo hypoxaemic failure; the ERS 2022 guideline suggests HFNC over NIV in this setting (conditional).

**EDIC trap:** strong NIV recommendations exist only for hypercapnic COPD and cardiogenic oedema.

**Ref:** Rochwerg B, et al. ERS/ATS NIV guideline. *Eur Respir J* 2017;50:1602426. Oczkowski S, et al. ERS HFNC guideline. *Eur Respir J* 2022;59:2101574. Esteban A, et al. *N Engl J Med* 2004;350:2452–2460.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W11-D4 · Weaning from mechanical ventilation

**1.** Regarding predictors of successful weaning from mechanical ventilation:

- A. The rapid shallow breathing index (respiratory rate divided by tidal volume in litres) is measured during unassisted breathing, with a threshold of about 105 breaths/min/L. — T / F
- B. A maximal inspiratory pressure more negative than −20 to −30 cmH2O is associated with weaning success. — T / F
- C. A spontaneous breathing trial is a better predictor of successful extubation than indices measured beforehand. — T / F
- D. A low RSBI has high specificity, so it guarantees successful extubation. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – RSBI = RR / V_T (L) during unassisted breathing; Yang & Tobin found < 105 breaths/min/L predicted success (sensitivity 0.97).
- B (True) – A MIP more negative than −20 to −30 cmH₂O reflects adequate inspiratory muscle strength and is associated with success, but alone has poor specificity.
- C (True) – The SBT itself is the best test; guidelines advise proceeding directly to an SBT (PS 5–8 cmH₂O or T-piece, 30–120 min) once readiness criteria are met.
- D (False) – RSBI is sensitive but not specific (0.64); extubation can still fail from upper-airway, secretion or cardiac causes.

**EDIC trap:** predictors are aids – the decision is made on the SBT.

**Ref:** Yang KL, Tobin MJ. *N Engl J Med* 1991;324:1445–1450. Boles JM, et al. *Eur Respir J* 2007;29:1033–1056. Schmidt GA, et al. ATS/ACCP. *Am J Respir Crit Care Med* 2017;195:115–119.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W11-D5 · Extubation failure and post-extubation support

**1.** A 45-year-old man ventilated for 2 days after abdominal surgery passes a spontaneous breathing trial. He has no risk factors for extubation failure. The best post-extubation support is:

- A. Preventive NIV
- B. High-flow nasal oxygen
- C. No oxygen support
- D. CPAP 10 cmH₂O
- E. Heliox

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

In low-risk patients HFNO reduced reintubation compared with conventional oxygen (4.9 vs 12.2 %). Preventive NIV is for high-risk patients.

**EDIC trap:** Low risk → HFNO; high risk → NIV (± HFNO between sessions).

**Ref:** Hernández G, et al. *JAMA* 2016;315:1354–1361.

</details>

**2.** A 72-year-old man with COPD and hypercapnia during the SBT is extubated.

- A. Preventive NIV immediately after extubation is recommended. — T / F
- B. In HIGH-WEAN, HFNO alternating with NIV reduced reintubation compared with HFNO alone. — T / F
- C. If respiratory failure develops 18 h later, NIV should be used to avoid reintubation. — T / F
- D. HFNO alone was inferior to NIV in high-risk patients. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – F.**

- A (True) – ATS/ACCP 2017 – preventive NIV in high-risk patients.
- B (True) – 11.8 vs 18.2 %.
- C (False) – NIV for established post-extubation failure delayed reintubation and increased mortality (25 vs 14 %).
- D (False) – HFNO was non-inferior to NIV (22.8 vs 19.1 %).

**EDIC trap:** Preventive NIV – yes; rescue NIV – no.

**Ref:** Thille AW, et al. HIGH-WEAN. *JAMA* 2019; Esteban A, et al. *N Engl J Med* 2004.

</details>

**3.** A 68-year-old woman intubated for 8 days after a difficult, traumatic intubation fails a cuff-leak test. She is otherwise ready for extubation. The best next step is:

- A. Extubate immediately and give nebulised adrenaline
- B. Give systemic corticosteroids at least 4 h before extubation, without repeating the test
- C. Perform a tracheostomy
- D. Delay extubation by 7 days
- E. Repeat the cuff-leak test every 6 hours until it is positive

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

In high-risk patients with a failed cuff-leak test, corticosteroids ≥ 4 h before extubation reduce post-extubation stridor and reintubation; the test need not be repeated (e.g. methylprednisolone 20 mg every 4 h from 12 h before: laryngeal oedema 3 vs 22 %).

**EDIC trap:** Don't make extubation wait for a positive retest.

**Ref:** François B, et al. *Lancet* 2007;369:1083–1089; Girard TD, et al. ATS/ACCP. *Am J Respir Crit Care Med* 2017.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W12-D1 · Acute asthma and COPD exacerbation

**1.** A 22-year-old man presents with acute asthma. Which of the following are features of life-threatening asthma (BTS/NICE/SIGN)?

- A. SpO2 < 92 % — T / F
- B. Cardiac arrhythmia — T / F
- C. Heart rate > 110/min — T / F
- D. Respiratory rate ≥ 25/min — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – F.**

- A (True) – SpO₂ < 92 % (or PaO₂ < 8 kPa) is a life-threatening feature.
- B (True) – Arrhythmia is a life-threatening feature, as are hypotension, cyanosis, silent chest, poor respiratory effort, exhaustion, altered consciousness, PEF < 33 % and a "normal" PaCO₂ (4.6–6.0 kPa).
- C (False) – Heart rate ≥ 110/min defines **acute severe** asthma, not life-threatening asthma.
- D (False) – Respiratory rate ≥ 25/min is also a feature of **acute severe** asthma (with PEF 33–50 % and inability to complete sentences).

**EDIC trap:** a raised PaCO₂ or the need for ventilation with raised inflation pressures defines **near-fatal** asthma.

**Ref:** BTS/SIGN. British guideline on the management of asthma (SIGN 158), 2019 – acute section still current alongside BTS/NICE/SIGN NG245, 2024; GINA 2025.

</details>

**2.** Regarding the treatment of acute severe asthma in adults:

- A. Nebulised salbutamol should be driven by air rather than oxygen in hypoxaemic patients. — T / F
- B. Oral prednisolone is less effective than IV hydrocortisone. — T / F
- C. Antibiotics should be given routinely. — T / F
- D. Benzodiazepines are useful on the ward to relieve anxiety and dyspnoea. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – F, B – F, C – F, D – F.**

- A (False) – In acute severe asthma, nebulisers should be **oxygen-driven** to avoid hypoxaemia (target SpO₂ 94–98 % in BTS/SIGN; 93–95 % in GINA).
- B (False) – Oral prednisolone 40–50 mg is as effective as IV hydrocortisone if it can be swallowed and retained.
- C (False) – Most exacerbations are viral; antibiotics are given only with evidence of bacterial infection.
- D (False) – Sedatives are contraindicated outside a critical care setting because they depress respiratory drive.

**EDIC trap:** an all-false K′ item is unusual but possible – judge each statement on its own.

**Ref:** BTS/SIGN. SIGN 158 British guideline on the management of asthma, 2019; GINA 2025.

</details>

**3.** A 26-year-old woman with asthma has received three back-to-back nebulised salbutamol doses, nebulised ipratropium and oral prednisolone 50 mg over the last hour. She can speak in short sentences, HR 120/min, RR 28/min, SpO2 95 % on oxygen, PEF 45 % of best, PaCO2 4.4 kPa. Which is the most appropriate next step?

- A. Intubation and mechanical ventilation
- B. IV aminophylline loading dose
- C. Heliox-driven nebulisers as the next routine step
- D. Chest X-ray before any further treatment
- E. IV magnesium sulphate 2 g over 20 minutes

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**E.**

**Why E:** She has acute severe asthma (PEF 45 %, HR 120, RR 28) that has not responded adequately to repeated bronchodilators and steroids. A single dose of IV magnesium sulphate (1.2–2 g over 20 min) is recommended in this situation (SIGN 158, GINA 2025); it reduces admissions in some patients, whereas nebulised magnesium showed no benefit in 3MG.

**Why not the others:**

- A – There is no exhaustion, rising PaCO₂ or altered consciousness, so intubation is not indicated.
- B – IV aminophylline adds little bronchodilation and causes arrhythmias and vomiting; it is reserved for life-threatening asthma after senior review.
- C – Heliox is not routinely recommended because evidence of benefit is lacking.
- D – A chest X-ray is indicated for suspected pneumothorax/consolidation or failure to respond, but it must not delay treatment.

**EDIC trap:** PaCO₂ 4.4 kPa is appropriately low here – it would be "normal" (≥ 4.6 kPa) that signals danger.

**Ref:** SIGN 158, 2019; Kew KM, et al. *Cochrane Database Syst Rev* 2014;(5):CD010909; Goodacre S, et al. 3MG. *Lancet Respir Med* 2013;1:293–300.

</details>

**4.** A 33-year-old man with a history of two previous ICU admissions is assessed in the emergency department during an asthma attack after running out of his inhalers. He is receiving oxygen and nebulised salbutamol while arterial blood gases are being obtained. Each of the following findings indicates life-threatening asthma EXCEPT:

- A. PaCO2 5.4 kPa
- B. SpO2 90 % on air
- C. New confusion
- D. Heart rate 125/min
- E. PEF 30 % of predicted

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.**

**Why D:** Tachycardia ≥ 110/min is a feature of acute severe asthma, not of life-threatening asthma. Bradycardia or hypotension, by contrast, are pre-terminal signs.

**Why not the others:**

- A – A "normal" PaCO₂ (4.6–6.0 kPa) in a tachypnoeic asthmatic indicates fatigue and is life-threatening.
- B – SpO₂ < 92 % on air is life-threatening.
- C – Altered consciousness (confusion, drowsiness) is life-threatening.
- E – PEF < 33 % of best or predicted is life-threatening.

**EDIC trap:** previous ICU admission and running out of medication are risk factors for fatal asthma – keep a low threshold for ICU referral.

**Ref:** BTS/SIGN. SIGN 158, 2019; GINA 2025.

</details>

**5.** A 69-year-old man with COPD (FEV1 35 % predicted) is admitted with an infective exacerbation. After 1 hour of nebulised bronchodilators, prednisolone, antibiotics and controlled oxygen: RR 28/min, alert, SpO2 91 % on 28 % Venturi, pH 7.31, PaCO2 8.4 kPa, HCO3⁻ 31 mmol/L. Which is the most appropriate next step?

- A. Start bilevel NIV in a monitored setting, targeting SpO2 88–92 %
- B. High-flow nasal oxygen at 60 L/min and FiO2 0.6
- C. Immediate intubation and invasive ventilation
- D. Doxapram infusion
- E. Increase to 60 % Venturi mask oxygen

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.**

**Why A:** Persistent respiratory acidosis (pH < 7.35, PaCO₂ > 6.5 kPa) after optimal medical therapy for 1 hour is the indication for bilevel NIV in a monitored setting (strong recommendation, ERS/ATS 2017; BTS/ICS 2016). Oxygen should target SpO₂ 88–92 %.

**Why not the others:**

- B – ERS 2022 suggests trying NIV before HFNO in hypercapnic COPD, and an FiO₂ of 0.6 risks worsening hypercapnia.
- C – He is alert without contraindications to NIV; intubation is for NIV failure or contraindications.
- D – Doxapram is used only when NIV is unavailable or inappropriate.
- E – Increasing oxygen to 60 % would worsen hypercapnia and acidosis.

**EDIC trap:** a raised HCO₃⁻ (31 mmol/L) shows chronic compensation – the acute component is the pH, which decides NIV.

**Ref:** Davidson AC, et al. BTS/ICS guideline. *Thorax* 2016;71(Suppl 2):ii1–ii35; Rochwerg B, et al. *Eur Respir J* 2017;50:1602426; Oczkowski S, et al. *Eur Respir J* 2022;59:2101574.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W12-D2 · Severe pneumonia and influenza

**1.** Regarding severe community-acquired pneumonia (CAP):

- A. In CAPE COD, hydrocortisone reduced 28-day mortality in severe CAP. — T / F
- B. Need for vasopressors is a major criterion of the ATS/IDSA definition of severe CAP. — T / F
- C. A parapneumonic effusion with a pleural fluid pH below 7.2 requires chest tube drainage. — T / F
- D. Combination therapy with a macrolide is not recommended in severe CAP. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – CAPE COD (n = 800): hydrocortisone 200 mg/day reduced 28-day mortality (6.2 % vs 11.9 %) in severe CAP without shock or influenza; ATS 2025 and SCCM 2024 now support corticosteroids in severe CAP, ERS/ESICM 2023 suggested them if shock is present.
- B (True) – The two major criteria are septic shock requiring vasopressors and respiratory failure requiring invasive ventilation.
- C (True) – A pleural pH < 7.2 (or frank pus, or a positive Gram stain/culture) indicates a complicated effusion or empyema needing chest tube drainage.
- D (False) – β-lactam plus macrolide **is** recommended in severe CAP (ERS/ESICM 2023; ATS/IDSA 2019).

**EDIC trap:** corticosteroids should be avoided in influenza pneumonia and uncontrolled fungal infection.

**Ref:** Dequin PF, et al. CAPE COD. *N Engl J Med* 2023;388:1931–1941; Martin-Loeches I, et al. *Intensive Care Med* 2023;49:615–632; Metlay JP, et al. *Am J Respir Crit Care Med* 2019;200:e45–e67; Jones BE, et al. ATS CAP guideline. *Am J Respir Crit Care Med* 2025.

</details>

**2.** A previously well 19-year-old man has had an influenza-like illness for 4 days. He now presents with haemoptysis, hypotension, a white cell count of 1.8 × 10⁹/L and rapidly progressive multilobar consolidation with early cavitation. Sputum Gram stain shows abundant Gram-positive cocci in clusters, and Panton–Valentine leukocidin (PVL)–producing Staphylococcus aureus is suspected; susceptibilities are not yet available. Which is the most appropriate empirical antimicrobial regimen?

- A. Flucloxacillin alone
- B. Vancomycin alone
- C. Co-amoxiclav plus clarithromycin
- D. Ceftriaxone plus doxycycline
- E. Linezolid plus clindamycin plus rifampicin, with IV immunoglobulin considered

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**E.**

**Why E:** A young patient with a post-influenza illness, haemoptysis, leucopenia and cavitating multilobar consolidation has PVL-associated necrotising pneumonia, which carries high mortality. UK guidance recommends toxin-suppressing therapy – linezolid plus clindamycin and rifampicin, covering MRSA until susceptibilities are known – and considering IV immunoglobulin to neutralise toxin.

**Why not the others:**

- A – Flucloxacillin alone does not suppress toxin production and misses MRSA.
- B – Vancomycin has poor lung penetration and does not suppress toxin production.
- C – Co-amoxiclav plus clarithromycin does not reliably cover PVL-MRSA.
- D – Ceftriaxone plus doxycycline is a standard CAP regimen that does not reliably cover PVL-MRSA.

**EDIC trap:** leucopenia is a marker of poor prognosis in PVL pneumonia.

**Ref:** Health Protection Agency (UKHSA). Guidance on the diagnosis and management of PVL-associated *S. aureus* infections in England, 2nd ed., 2008; Gillet Y, et al. *Clin Infect Dis* 2007;45:315–321.

</details>

**3.** A 31-year-old woman at 33 weeks' gestation presents with 4 days of fever, myalgia and cough, now breathless with SpO2 90 % on air. She is not vaccinated this season. RR is 30/min and chest X-ray shows bilateral patchy infiltrates. Nasopharyngeal PCR is positive for influenza A. Which is the most appropriate management?

- A. Withhold oseltamivir because symptoms began more than 48 hours ago
- B. Start oseltamivir immediately and arrange early critical care review
- C. Give high-dose methylprednisolone
- D. Avoid chest imaging because of the pregnancy
- E. Defer ICU review until PaO2/FiO2 falls below 100 mmHg

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

**Why B:** Pregnancy, especially the third trimester, is a major risk factor for severe influenza, critical illness and death, as seen in the 2009 H1N1 pandemic. Oseltamivir should be started immediately in any hospitalised or high-risk patient regardless of symptom duration (benefit persists beyond 48 h), with early critical care review given her hypoxaemia and tachypnoea.

**Why not the others:**

- A – The 48-hour window does not apply to hospitalised or high-risk patients.
- C – Corticosteroids are associated with higher mortality and superinfection in influenza pneumonia.
- D – Imaging must not be withheld; with shielding, the fetal dose from a chest X-ray or CT is very low.
- E – Pregnant patients deteriorate rapidly; waiting for P/F < 100 mmHg is far too late.

**EDIC trap:** oseltamivir is safe in pregnancy – do not withhold it.

**Ref:** Uyeki TM, et al. IDSA influenza guideline 2018 update. *Clin Infect Dis* 2019;68:e1–e47; Siston AM, et al. *JAMA* 2010;303:1517–1525.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W12-D3 · Airway management in the ICU

**1.** During ICU intubation, two laryngoscopy attempts and two attempts with a second-generation supraglottic device have failed. Facemask ventilation is impossible and SpO₂ is 70 % and falling. The next step is:

- A. A third supraglottic device
- B. Fibreoptic intubation via the nose
- C. Scalpel–bougie–tube front-of-neck access
- D. Wake the patient up
- E. Needle cricothyroidotomy with jet ventilation as first choice

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.**

**Why C:** Laryngoscopy (Plan A) and second-generation SAD (Plan B) have failed, facemask ventilation (Plan C) is impossible and SpO₂ is falling: this is a "can't intubate, can't oxygenate" (CICO) situation. DAS 2018 Plan D is immediate front-of-neck access with scalpel–bougie–tube cricothyroidotomy, after ensuring full neuromuscular blockade.

**Why not the others:**

- A – Further SAD attempts delay oxygenation; DAS limits the number of attempts.
- B – Fibreoptic nasal intubation takes too long in a hypoxaemic CICO patient.
- D – Waking up is rarely possible in ICU patients, whose underlying respiratory failure persists.
- E – Needle cricothyroidotomy with jet ventilation has a high failure rate and risks barotrauma; it is not the recommended first choice.

**EDIC trap:** declare "CICO" out loud and use capnography at every step to confirm success.

**Ref:** Higgs A, et al. DAS/ICS/FICM/RCoA guidelines. *Br J Anaesth* 2018;120:323–352.

</details>

**2.** Regarding tracheal intubation of critically ill adults:

- A. A videolaryngoscope increases first-attempt success. — T / F
- B. Routine use of a bougie improved first-attempt success. — T / F
- C. Preoxygenation with NIV reduced hypoxaemia compared with a facemask. — T / F
- D. Bag-mask ventilation between induction and laryngoscopy reduced severe hypoxaemia. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – T, D – T.**

- A (True) – In DEVICE, video laryngoscopy increased first-attempt success (85.1 % vs 70.8 %) compared with direct laryngoscopy.
- B (False) – In BOUGIE, routine bougie use did not improve first-attempt success (80.4 % vs 83.0 % with a stylet).
- C (True) – In PREOXI, NIV preoxygenation reduced hypoxaemia from 18.5 % to 9.1 % compared with a facemask.
- D (True) – In PreVent, bag-mask ventilation between induction and laryngoscopy halved severe hypoxaemia (10.9 % vs 22.8 %) without more aspiration.

**EDIC trap:** the bougie is a rescue tool for a difficult view, not a routine first-pass device.

**Ref:** Prekker ME, et al. DEVICE. *N Engl J Med* 2023;389:418–429; Driver BE, et al. BOUGIE. *JAMA* 2021;326:2488–2497; Gibbs KW, et al. PREOXI. *N Engl J Med* 2024;390:2165–2177; Casey JD, et al. PreVent. *N Engl J Med* 2019;380:811–821.

</details>

**3.** In the RSI trial (2025) comparing ketamine with etomidate for emergency intubation of critically ill adults:

- A. Ketamine reduced 28-day mortality
- B. Etomidate increased 28-day mortality through adrenal suppression
- C. Mortality was similar, but cardiovascular collapse was more frequent with ketamine
- D. Cardiovascular collapse was more frequent with etomidate
- E. Ketamine increased first-attempt success

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.**

**Why C:** In the RSI trial, 28-day in-hospital mortality did not differ significantly between ketamine and etomidate (28.1 % vs 29.1 %). Peri-intubation cardiovascular collapse was more frequent with ketamine (22.1 % vs 17.0 %).

**Why not the others:**

- A – Ketamine did not reduce 28-day mortality.
- B – Despite adrenal suppression, etomidate did not increase mortality.
- D – Cardiovascular collapse was more frequent with ketamine, not etomidate.
- E – The trial was not designed around first-attempt success, and the induction agent was not shown to improve it.

**EDIC trap:** neither induction agent is "haemodynamically safe" in shock – reduce the dose and have a vasopressor ready.

**Ref:** Casey JD, et al. Ketamine or etomidate (RSI trial). *N Engl J Med* 2025. doi:10.1056/NEJMoa2511420.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W12-D4 · Tracheostomy emergencies

**1.** A 58-year-old man is on day 5 after a percutaneous tracheostomy (patent upper airway, not a laryngectomy). He becomes acutely hypoxic and agitated with no capnography trace. Oxygen has been applied to face and stoma, the inner cannula removed, a suction catheter will not pass, and the cuff has been deflated without improvement. The tracheostomy tube has now been removed. He has a pulse but is not breathing adequately. What is the most appropriate next step?

- A. Blindly reinsert a new tracheostomy tube through the stoma
- B. Perform a needle cricothyroidotomy
- C. Oxygenate via the upper airway with bag–mask ventilation while occluding the stoma
- D. Perform fibreoptic bronchoscopy through the stoma
- E. Wait for the ENT team before any further airway intervention

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.**

**Why C:** In the NTSP algorithm, once a non-patent tube has been removed from a patient with a patent upper airway, primary emergency oxygenation is via the mouth and nose (bag–mask, oral airway or supraglottic device) with the stoma covered; the alternative is via the stoma (paediatric face mask or LMA over the stoma). Secondary manoeuvres are oral intubation or, by an experienced operator, stoma intubation, ideally fibreoptic-guided.

**Why not the others:**

- A – Blind reinsertion through an immature (< 7–10 days) percutaneous tract risks a false passage.
- B – Needle cricothyroidotomy is not part of the algorithm when the upper airway and stoma are available.
- D – Fibreoptic stoma intubation is a secondary, expert manoeuvre once oxygenation has been restored.
- E – Help is called early, but waiting before acting risks hypoxic cardiac arrest.

**EDIC trap:** "patent upper airway" vs "laryngectomy" decides whether the mouth can be used at all.

**Ref:** McGrath BA, et al. NTSP guidelines. *Anaesthesia* 2012;67:1025–1041.

</details>

**2.** Regarding tracheostomy airway emergencies (NTSP):

- A. Waveform capnography is the best indicator of a patent tracheostomy airway in a ventilated patient. — T / F
- B. In a patient with a patent upper airway, oxygen should be applied to both the face and the stoma. — T / F
- C. A patient with a laryngectomy can be oxygenated via the mouth. — T / F
- D. A percutaneous tracheostomy tract less than 7 days old allows safe blind reinsertion. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – F.**

- A (True) – Waveform capnography is mandatory in ventilated tracheostomy patients; loss of a trace indicates obstruction or displacement.
- B (True) – Applying oxygen to both the face and the stoma is the first step of the algorithm in a patient with a patent upper airway.
- C (False) – After laryngectomy the trachea is disconnected from the upper airway, so oxygenation is only via the stoma (bedside signage must state this).
- D (False) – An immature tract, especially after percutaneous insertion, can close or allow a false passage; reinsertion needs an experienced operator with a fibreoptic scope or bougie, or oral intubation.

**EDIC trap:** "no trace = wrong place" applies to tracheostomies as much as to endotracheal tubes.

**Ref:** McGrath BA, et al. NTSP guidelines. *Anaesthesia* 2012;67:1025–1041.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W12-D5 · Pleural disease

**1.** A 64-year-old woman with severe COPD presents with sudden breathlessness. SpO2 is 88 % on air, RR 26/min, BP 132/80 mmHg. Chest X-ray shows a left pneumothorax with a 2.5 cm rim at the level of the hilum and no mediastinal shift. She lives alone. Which is the most appropriate management?

- A. Observation and discharge with review in 48 hours
- B. Small-bore (≤ 14 F) Seldinger chest drain and hospital admission
- C. Needle aspiration and discharge if successful
- D. Large-bore (28 F) surgical chest drain
- E. Immediate video-assisted thoracoscopic pleurodesis

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

**Why B:** She has a symptomatic secondary spontaneous pneumothorax (underlying COPD, age > 50) with a rim > 2 cm and poor respiratory reserve. BTS guidance favours intervention with a small-bore (≤ 14 F) drain and hospital admission; small-bore drains are as effective as large-bore drains and better tolerated.

**Why not the others:**

- A – Observation and discharge are unsafe with significant symptoms, hypoxaemia and poor reserve, especially living alone.
- C – Aspiration is reserved for smaller (1–2 cm), minimally symptomatic SSP, and such patients are admitted rather than discharged.
- D – A large-bore surgical drain offers no advantage and is more painful.
- E – Surgery is considered for a persistent air leak (> 3–5 days) or recurrence, not first-line.

**EDIC trap:** the conservative approach for PSP (Brown, NEJM 2020) does **not** apply to SSP.

**Ref:** Roberts ME, et al. BTS Guideline for pleural disease. *Thorax* 2023;78(Suppl 3):s1–s42; MacDuff A, et al. BTS pleural disease guideline 2010. *Thorax* 2010;65(Suppl 2):ii18–ii31.

</details>

**2.** Regarding pleural fluid analysis:

- A. A pleural fluid/serum protein ratio > 0.5 indicates an exudate. — T / F
- B. A pleural fluid LDH greater than two-thirds of the upper limit of normal serum LDH indicates an exudate. — T / F
- C. In patients on diuretics, Light's criteria may misclassify transudates as exudates; a serum–pleural albumin gradient > 12 g/L then favours a transudate. — T / F
- D. Pleural fluid pH should be measured in frankly purulent fluid. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – A pleural/serum protein ratio > 0.5 is one of Light's criteria; any one criterion classifies the fluid as an exudate.
- B (True) – A pleural LDH greater than two-thirds of the upper limit of normal serum LDH is also a Light's criterion (as is an LDH ratio > 0.6).
- C (True) – Diuresis concentrates pleural protein and LDH, so ~25 % of cardiac transudates are misclassified; a serum–pleural albumin gradient > 12 g/L (or raised NT-proBNP) favours a transudate.
- D (False) – Frank pus indicates empyema needing drainage regardless of pH, and pus can damage the blood gas analyser; pH is measured in non-purulent fluid (< 7.2 → drain).

**EDIC trap:** Light's criteria are highly sensitive for exudates but over-call them in patients on diuretics.

**Ref:** Light RW, et al. *Ann Intern Med* 1972;77:507–513; Roberts ME, et al. BTS Guideline for pleural disease. *Thorax* 2023;78(Suppl 3):s1–s42.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W13-D1 · Humidification and aerosol therapy

**1.** Regarding humidification and nebulisation in ventilated patients:

- A. In a normal spontaneously breathing adult, the isothermic saturation boundary lies a few centimetres below the carina. — T / F
- B. Heat and moisture exchangers are preferred in patients with copious, bloody secretions. — T / F
- C. A jet nebuliser driven by an external gas source does not affect the delivered tidal volume or triggering. — T / F
- D. Placing a nebuliser between the heat and moisture exchanger and the endotracheal tube is the recommended arrangement. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – F.**

- A (True) – The isothermic saturation boundary (ISB) is where inspired gas reaches 37 °C and 100 % relative humidity (44 mg/L). In health it lies a few centimetres below the carina. Intubation bypasses the upper airway and shifts it distally.
- B (False) – HMEs are contraindicated with copious, thick or bloody secretions (occlusion risk), large leaks, hypothermia and very high minute ventilation. Use active heated humidification.
- C (False) – Extra gas from an external jet nebuliser adds to delivered VT, changes FiO₂ and can impair triggering. Vibrating-mesh nebulisers add no gas.
- D (False) – An HME between the nebuliser and the patient filters out the aerosol and gets wet. Remove the HME during nebulisation, or use a heated humidifier.

**EDIC trap:** An HME and a nebuliser do not mix. Take out the HME or switch to a mesh nebuliser in the inspiratory limb.

**Ref:** Restrepo RD, Walsh BK. *Respir Care* 2012;57:782–788; Dhand R. *J Aerosol Med Pulm Drug Deliv* 2008;21:45–60.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W13-D2 · Respiratory mechanics and PEEP

**1.** Regarding respiratory mechanics during mechanical ventilation:

- A. During volume control with constant flow, the difference between peak and plateau pressure reflects airway (including tube) resistance. — T / F
- B. Static compliance is calculated as tidal volume divided by (peak pressure − PEEP). — T / F
- C. In a patient with expiratory flow limitation, setting external PEEP well above intrinsic PEEP reduces hyperinflation. — T / F
- D. Pressure-controlled ventilation produces a constant (square-wave) inspiratory flow. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – F.**

- A (True) – With constant flow, Ppeak − Pplat = flow × resistance. That covers the airways and the endotracheal tube.
- B (False) – Static compliance = VT/(Pplat − total PEEP). Using peak pressure gives dynamic compliance, which includes resistance.
- C (False) – External PEEP up to about 80 % of PEEPi lowers the inspiratory threshold load without worsening hyperinflation. PEEP well above PEEPi adds to end-expiratory volume.
- D (False) – PCV gives a decelerating (exponential) flow pattern. Constant square-wave flow is typical of volume control.

**EDIC trap:** In flow limitation, "PEEP below PEEPi" helps triggering. "PEEP above PEEPi" worsens hyperinflation.

**Ref:** Hess DR. *Respir Care* 2014;59:1773–1794; Marini JJ. *Am J Respir Crit Care Med* 2011;184:756–762.

</details>

**2.** Regarding the physiological effects of PEEP:

- A. PEEP reduces left-ventricular afterload by decreasing LV transmural pressure. — T / F
- B. PEEP that overdistends alveoli increases right-ventricular afterload. — T / F
- C. PEEP reduces the pressure gradient for venous return. — T / F
- D. PEEP improves oxygenation partly by recruiting alveoli and increasing functional residual capacity. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – T.**

- A (True) – Higher intrathoracic pressure lowers LV transmural pressure (afterload). That helps in LV failure and cardiogenic pulmonary oedema.
- B (True) – Overdistension compresses alveolar vessels and raises PVR. RV afterload rises, with a risk of acute cor pulmonale.
- C (True) – PEEP raises right atrial pressure, so the gradient for venous return (mean systemic filling pressure − RAP) falls.
- D (True) – PEEP recruits collapsed alveoli and raises FRC, which reduces shunt and improves oxygenation.

**EDIC trap:** The net effect on cardiac output depends on volume status and which ventricle is failing. PEEP can help LV failure and harm RV failure.

**Ref:** Mahmood SS, Pinsky MR. *Ann Transl Med* 2018;6:349; Vieillard-Baron A, et al. *Intensive Care Med* 2016;42:739–749.

</details>

**3.** Regarding gas exchange and respiratory mechanics:

- A. An increase in physiological dead space fraction raises PaCO2 if minute ventilation is unchanged. — T / F
- B. Doubling respiratory rate while halving tidal volume keeps alveolar ventilation constant. — T / F
- C. In the upright, spontaneously breathing lung, ventilation per unit volume is greater at the bases than at the apices. — T / F
- D. The compliance of the respiratory system equals the sum of lung compliance and chest wall compliance. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – PaCO₂ ∝ VCO₂ / [VE × (1 − VD/VT)]. If MV is unchanged, a higher dead-space fraction means less alveolar ventilation and a higher PaCO₂.
- B (False) – Alveolar ventilation = RR × (VT − VD). With a fixed dead space, halving VT lowers alveolar ventilation even though MV is unchanged.
- C (True) – Basal alveoli start smaller, on the steep part of the pressure–volume curve, so they expand more per unit volume.
- D (False) – Elastances add (E_rs = E_L + E_cw), so 1/C_rs = 1/C_L + 1/C_cw. Respiratory system compliance is lower than either component.

**EDIC trap:** Rapid shallow breathing wastes ventilation on dead space. The same MV can mean a higher PaCO₂.

**Ref:** West JB, Luks AM. *West's Respiratory Physiology: The Essentials*, 11th ed. Wolters Kluwer 2021; Hess DR. *Respir Care* 2014;59:1773–1794.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W13-D3 · Patient–ventilator asynchronies

**1.** A 70-year-old man with COPD on pressure support 18 cmH₂O, PEEP 5 cmH₂O has repeated small negative deflections in the airway pressure and brief flow increases during expiration that are not followed by a ventilator breath. The best action is:

- A. Increase pressure support to 24 cmH₂O
- B. Reduce pressure support and/or add external PEEP
- C. Increase trigger sensitivity to the maximum (auto-trigger)
- D. Lower the cycle-off percentage to 5 %
- E. Start neuromuscular blockade

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

**Why B:** The small negative deflections and brief flow rises during expiration are ineffective efforts. The patient cannot lower alveolar pressure below the trigger threshold because of intrinsic PEEP. High PS (18 cmH₂O) gives large VT and short Te, which raises PEEPi. Reducing PS/VT (Thille 2008) and adding external PEEP below PEEPi both help.

**Why not the others:**
- A – More support means larger VT, more PEEPi and more ineffective efforts.
- C – Maximal trigger sensitivity risks auto-triggering and does not remove the PEEPi threshold load.
- D – A 5 % cycle-off prolongs inspiration and worsens hyperinflation.
- E – Paralysis abolishes effort rather than correcting the setting, and it is not appropriate here.

**EDIC trap:** More support usually makes ineffective efforts worse.

**Ref:** Thille AW, et al. *Intensive Care Med* 2006;32:1515–1522; Thille AW, et al. *Intensive Care Med* 2008;34:1477–1486.

</details>

**2.** Regarding patient–ventilator asynchrony:

- A. An asynchrony index > 10 % is considered severe. — T / F
- B. Delayed cycling in COPD is corrected by increasing the cycle-off (expiratory trigger) percentage. — T / F
- C. Auto-triggering may be caused by circuit leak or cardiac oscillations. — T / F
- D. Reverse triggering cannot cause breath stacking. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – AI = asynchronous events / (ventilator cycles + ineffective efforts) × 100. An AI above 10 % is defined as severe (Thille 2006).
- B (True) – A higher cycle-off (expiratory trigger) % ends inspiration earlier. That fixes delayed cycling caused by the long τ in COPD.
- C (True) – Leaks and cardiac oscillations can falsely trigger breaths. In suspected brain death this can mimic respiratory drive.
- D (False) – Reverse triggering can produce double triggering and breath stacking, with a hidden large VT.

**EDIC trap:** Delayed cycling: raise cycle-off %. Premature cycling: lower it.

**Ref:** Thille AW, et al. *Intensive Care Med* 2006;32:1515–1522; Blanch L, et al. *Intensive Care Med* 2015;41:633–641.

</details>

**3.** In Thille's 2006 study of asynchrony during assisted ventilation, the two most common types were:

- A. Auto-triggering and premature cycling
- B. Ineffective efforts and double triggering
- C. Reverse triggering and flow starvation
- D. Delayed cycling and auto-triggering
- E. Trigger delay and premature cycling

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

**Why B:** Thille et al. studied 62 patients on assisted ventilation. Ineffective triggering and double triggering were the two dominant patterns. A quarter of patients (24 %) had an AI above 10 %, which was associated with longer ventilation (median 25 vs 7 days).

**Why not the others:**
- A – Auto-triggering and premature cycling were uncommon.
- C – Reverse triggering was described later (Akoumianaki 2013) and was not a main pattern here. Flow starvation was not quantified.
- D – Delayed cycling ("long cycles") and auto-triggering were minor contributors.
- E – Trigger delay and premature cycling were not the main types.

**EDIC trap:** An AI above 10 % is associated with longer ventilation and, in observational studies, higher mortality.

**Ref:** Thille AW, et al. *Intensive Care Med* 2006;32:1515–1522.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W13-D4 · VILI, P-SILI, mechanical power, P0.1 and Pocc

**1.** A passive patient in volume control has RR 20/min, VT 0.5 L, peak pressure 30 cmH₂O, plateau 25 cmH₂O and PEEP 10 cmH₂O. Using the simplified formula 0.098 × RR × VT × (Ppeak − ½ΔP), mechanical power is approximately:

- A. 11 J/min
- B. 17 J/min
- C. 22 J/min
- D. 30 J/min
- E. 44 J/min

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.**

**Why C:** ΔP = Pplat − PEEP = 25 − 10 = 15 cmH₂O. MP = 0.098 × 20 × 0.5 × (30 − 7.5) = 0.98 × 22.5 ≈ 22 J/min. That is above the ~17 J/min linked to higher mortality in observational data (Serpa Neto 2018).

**Why not the others:**
- A – Half the correct value (RR or VT halved).
- B – Corresponds to a pressure term of about 17 cmH₂O (½ΔP + PEEP). It also matches the mortality threshold, which is the distractor.
- D – Uses Ppeak (30) without subtracting ½ΔP.
- E – Double the correct value (VT taken as 1 L).

**EDIC trap:** The MP threshold comes from observational data, not RCTs.

**Ref:** Gattinoni L, et al. *Intensive Care Med* 2016;42:1567–1575; Serpa Neto A, et al. *Intensive Care Med* 2018;44:1914–1922.

</details>

**2.** A patient on pressure support has P0.1 4.5 cmH₂O and an occlusion pressure swing (ΔPocc) of −20 cmH₂O.

- A. The P0.1 suggests high respiratory drive. — T / F
- B. Estimated inspiratory muscle pressure is about 15 cmH₂O. — T / F
- C. A mechanical power above ~17 J/min has been shown in RCTs to increase mortality. — T / F
- D. Pendelluft and large transpulmonary pressure swings are mechanisms of P-SILI. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – T.**

- A (True) – A P0.1 above 3.5 cmH₂O suggests high drive and potentially excessive effort. A value of 1 or less suggests low drive or over-assistance.
- B (True) – Pmus ≈ −¾ × ΔPocc = 0.75 × 20 = 15 cmH₂O, which is high effort (above 10–15 cmH₂O).
- C (False) – The ~17 J/min threshold comes from observational cohorts. No RCT has tested mechanical power as a target.
- D (True) – P-SILI mechanisms are large transpulmonary pressure swings, pendelluft and higher pulmonary transvascular pressure (oedema).

**EDIC trap:** A high P0.1 on PS suggests under-assistance or high drive and a risk of P-SILI. Consider more support or sedation and treat the cause.

**Ref:** Telias I, et al. *Am J Respir Crit Care Med* 2020;201:1086–1098; Bertoni M, et al. *Crit Care* 2019;23:346; Brochard L, et al. *Am J Respir Crit Care Med* 2017;195:438–442.

</details>

**3.** Which best describes lung strain?

- A. Transpulmonary pressure divided by tidal volume
- B. The ratio of tidal volume (plus PEEP volume) to end-expiratory lung volume
- C. Peak minus plateau pressure
- D. Tidal volume per kg actual body weight
- E. Respiratory rate × driving pressure

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

**Why B:** Strain is lung deformation: the volume change (tidal ± PEEP volume) relative to end-expiratory lung volume (FRC/EELV), the size of the "baby lung". Stress (transpulmonary pressure) ≈ specific lung elastance (about 13.5 cmH₂O) × strain. A strain above about 1.5 was injurious in animals.

**Why not the others:**
- A – Transpulmonary pressure per volume is an elastance, not strain.
- C – Ppeak − Pplat reflects resistance.
- D – VT per kg actual body weight is not strain, and it is not even the correct VT normalisation (that uses PBW).
- E – RR × ΔP is a part of mechanical power, not strain.

**EDIC trap:** VT per kg PBW is only a surrogate for strain. Aerated lung size varies widely in ARDS.

**Ref:** Chiumello D, et al. *Am J Respir Crit Care Med* 2008;178:346–355.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W13-D5 · Ventilation in obstruction

**1.** A 28-year-old woman with status asthmaticus is intubated and ventilated at RR 24/min. She becomes profoundly hypotensive within minutes. The first step is:

- A. Give a 1 L fluid bolus
- B. Increase PEEP to 15 cmH₂O
- C. Briefly disconnect from the ventilator and look for a pneumothorax
- D. Start an adrenaline infusion
- E. Increase the respiratory rate to correct hypercapnia

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.**

**Why C:** Hypotension soon after intubation in status asthmaticus is usually due to dynamic hyperinflation. High PEEPi reduces venous return. A brief disconnection (30–60 s) lets the lungs empty; if BP recovers, the diagnosis is confirmed. If not, look for and treat tension pneumothorax. Then use low RR, low VT, high inspiratory flow and I:E ≥ 1:3, and accept hypercapnia.

**Why not the others:**
- A – Fluids may help venous return but do not remove the cause, which is trapped gas.
- B – Extra PEEP in a passive asthmatic worsens hyperinflation.
- D – Adrenaline treats the symptom and delays fixing the mechanical cause.
- E – A higher RR shortens Te and worsens auto-PEEP.

**EDIC trap:** Hypotension after intubation in asthma means auto-PEEP or pneumothorax until proven otherwise.

**Ref:** Leatherman J. *Chest* 2015;147:1671–1680; Tuxen DV, Lane S. *Am Rev Respir Dis* 1987;136:872–879.

</details>

**2.** Regarding dynamic hyperinflation:

- A. Minute ventilation is the main determinant of dynamic hyperinflation. — T / F
- B. PEEPi is measured with an end-expiratory hold in a passive patient. — T / F
- C. In assisted ventilation of COPD, external PEEP set below PEEPi reduces the work of triggering. — T / F
- D. In severe asthma a plateau pressure up to 35 cmH₂O is acceptable. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – End-inspiratory volume and PEEPi depend mainly on minute ventilation. Lowering MV, mainly through RR, is the most effective step (Tuxen 1987).
- B (True) – An end-expiratory hold in a passive patient gives total PEEP. PEEPi = total PEEP − set PEEP.
- C (True) – In assisted ventilation of COPD with flow limitation, external PEEP below PEEPi offsets the threshold load and cuts triggering work.
- D (False) – Aim for Pplat below 30 (ideally below 25) cmH₂O. An end-inspiratory volume of 1.4 L or more predicts hypotension and barotrauma (Williams 1992).

**EDIC trap:** Lowering RR helps more than increasing inspiratory flow.

**Ref:** Williams TJ, Tuxen DV, et al. *Am Rev Respir Dis* 1992;146:607–615; Tuxen DV, Lane S. *Am Rev Respir Dis* 1987;136:872–879.

</details>

**3.** A 68-year-old man with an acute COPD exacerbation has pH 7.30, PaCO₂ 9 kPa (68 mmHg), RR 28/min and is alert. What is recommended?

- A. HFNO as first line instead of NIV
- B. Bilevel NIV
- C. Immediate intubation
- D. Controlled O₂ only, recheck gas in 6 h
- E. Doxapram

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

**Why B:** pH 7.30 with PaCO₂ 9 kPa is acute hypercapnic respiratory failure in a COPD exacerbation. Bilevel NIV is strongly recommended when pH ≤ 7.35 (ERS/ATS 2017, high certainty). It reduces intubation and mortality, and the patient is alert, so NIV is not contraindicated.

**Why not the others:**
- A – The ERS 2022 HFNC guideline suggests trying NIV before HFNO in hypercapnic COPD. HFNO is an alternative only if NIV is not tolerated.
- C – Intubation is for NIV failure or contraindications (coma, arrest, haemodynamic instability).
- D – Controlled O₂ alone with delayed reassessment misses the window for NIV.
- E – Doxapram is inferior to NIV and is not recommended as first-line.

**EDIC trap:** pH ≤ 7.35 with hypercapnia means NIV. With a normal pH, NIV is suggested against.

**Ref:** Rochwerg B, et al. *Eur Respir J* 2017;50:1602426; Oczkowski S, et al. *Eur Respir J* 2022;59:2101574.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W14-D1 · Review: sepsis and infections

**1.** A 59-year-old man has MSSA bacteraemia from an infected peripheral cannula, which has been removed. Blood cultures are negative after 48 h and TEE shows no vegetations. The minimum appropriate duration of IV therapy is:

- A. 5 days
- B. 7 days
- C. 10 days
- D. 14 days from the first negative culture
- E. 6 weeks

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.**

**Why D:** *S. aureus* bacteraemia carries a high risk of endocarditis and metastatic foci. Even when uncomplicated (catheter removed, cultures negative within 72 h, no vegetations on TEE, no prosthetic material, prompt defervescence), IV therapy should continue for ≥ 14 days from the first negative blood culture.

**Why not the others:**
- A – 5 days is far too short and risks relapse and metastatic infection.
- B – The 7-day course comes from BALANCE, which excluded *S. aureus* bacteraemia.
- C – 10 days is still below the minimum for uncomplicated *S. aureus* bacteraemia.
- E – 4–6 weeks is for complicated bacteraemia or endocarditis.

**EDIC trap:** 7-day courses do not apply to *S. aureus*.

**Ref:** Mermel LA, et al. IDSA. *Clin Infect Dis* 2009;49:1–45; BALANCE Investigators. *N Engl J Med* 2025;392:1065–1078.

</details>

**2.** Revision – sepsis and infections:

- A. *Candida* in endotracheal aspirate is usually colonisation. — T / F
- B. Procalcitonin is recommended to decide whether to start antimicrobials in suspected septic shock. — T / F
- C. Hydrocortisone is indicated in septic shock needing NA ≥ 0.25 µg/kg/min for ≥ 4 h. — T / F
- D. A patient on noradrenaline with lactate 1.5 mmol/L meets the Sepsis-3 criteria for septic shock. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – *Candida* in airway secretions is almost always colonisation, and treatment is rarely indicated; *Candida* pneumonia is extremely rare.
- B (False) – In suspected septic shock, start antimicrobials immediately; procalcitonin helps only to decide when to stop them.
- C (True) – Hydrocortisone 200 mg/day is used in septic shock when noradrenaline ≥ 0.25 µg/kg/min is needed for ≥ 4 h.
- D (False) – Sepsis-3 septic shock requires both a vasopressor to keep MAP ≥ 65 mmHg and lactate > 2 mmol/L despite adequate fluids.

**EDIC trap:** Septic shock = vasopressor **and** lactate > 2 mmol/L.

**Ref:** Prescott HC, et al. Surviving Sepsis Campaign 2026. *Intensive Care Med* 2026; Singer M, et al. Sepsis-3. *JAMA* 2016;315:801–810.

</details>

**3.** Rezafungin was compared with caspofungin in candidaemia and invasive candidiasis (ReSTORE). Its main distinguishing feature is:

- A. Oral administration
- B. Once-weekly IV dosing, non-inferior to caspofungin
- C. Activity against *Aspergillus* only
- D. Superior mortality to caspofungin
- E. It is an azole

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

**Why B:** Rezafungin is a long-acting echinocandin with a long half-life, which allows once-weekly IV dosing. In ReSTORE it was non-inferior to daily caspofungin for day-30 all-cause mortality and for global cure at day 14.

**Why not the others:**
- A – Rezafungin is given IV only.
- C – Its main target is *Candida*; it is not an anti-*Aspergillus*-only drug.
- D – ReSTORE showed non-inferiority, not superior mortality.
- E – Rezafungin is an echinocandin, not an azole.

**EDIC trap:** It is still an echinocandin, with the same spectrum as caspofungin.

**Ref:** Thompson GR, et al. ReSTORE. *Lancet* 2023;401:49–59.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W14-D2 · Review: shock, cardiovascular and cardiac arrest

**1.** A patient with septic shock has ScvO₂ 82 % and lactate 5.0 mmol/L on noradrenaline. Which interpretation is best?

- A. Tissue oxygenation is adequate because ScvO₂ is above 70 %
- B. High ScvO₂ may reflect impaired O₂ extraction; it does not exclude tissue hypoxia
- C. The lactate must be a laboratory error
- D. Transfuse to raise ScvO₂ further
- E. Start dobutamine to increase ScvO₂

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

**Why B:** In distributive shock, microcirculatory shunting and impaired cellular O₂ extraction can keep ScvO₂ normal or high while tissues are hypoxic. A lactate of 5.0 mmol/L shows ongoing hypoperfusion or dysoxia, so a high ScvO₂ is not reassuring.

**Why not the others:**
- A – ScvO₂ > 70 % does not exclude tissue hypoxia when extraction is impaired.
- C – A raised lactate in septic shock is expected and should not be dismissed as error.
- D – Transfusion to raise an already high ScvO₂ has no rationale.
- E – Dobutamine to push ScvO₂ even higher is not indicated.

**EDIC trap:** Normal SvO₂ ≠ adequate tissue oxygenation.

**Ref:** Cecconi M, et al. *Intensive Care Med* 2014;40:1795–1815.

</details>

**2.** Revision – shock and cardiac arrest:

- A. SCAI stage E denotes extremis, e.g. lactate ≥ 8 mmol/L or ongoing CPR. — T / F
- B. In hypothermic cardiac arrest at 28 °C adrenaline is withheld. — T / F
- C. In anaphylaxis without cardiac arrest, a 1 mg IV adrenaline bolus is first-line. — T / F
- D. In local anaesthetic systemic toxicity with cardiac arrest, 20 % lipid emulsion 1.5 mL/kg is given. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – T.**

- A (True) – SCAI stage E (extremis) includes ongoing CPR or ECMO and lactate ≥ 8 mmol/L.
- B (True) – Below 30 °C, adrenaline is withheld; between 30 and 35 °C, the dosing interval is doubled.
- C (False) – In anaphylaxis without arrest, give IM adrenaline 0.5 mg and repeat after 5 min; IV adrenaline is titrated only by experienced specialists.
- D (True) – In LAST cardiac arrest, give 20 % lipid emulsion 1.5 mL/kg and continue CPR for a prolonged period.

**EDIC trap:** Anaphylaxis = IM adrenaline, not an IV bolus.

**Ref:** Naidu SS, et al. SCAI. *J Am Coll Cardiol* 2022;79:933–946; ERC Guidelines 2025. *Resuscitation* 2025.

</details>

**3.** Which echocardiographic finding argues most strongly against cardiac tamponade in a hypotensive patient?

- A. A 2-cm circumferential pericardial effusion
- B. Right atrial systolic collapse
- C. A dilated IVC that does not collapse
- D. A small (non-dilated) IVC
- E. A swinging heart

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.**

**Why D:** Tamponade raises right atrial pressure, so the IVC is almost always dilated and non-collapsing. A small, collapsing IVC therefore makes tamponade very unlikely.

**Why not the others:**
- A – Large effusions can be well tolerated if they accumulate slowly; size alone does not decide.
- B – Right atrial systolic collapse is an early, sensitive sign of tamponade.
- C – A plethoric, non-collapsing IVC is very sensitive for tamponade and supports it.
- E – A swinging heart occurs with large effusions and supports tamponade.

**EDIC trap:** Tamponade is a clinical diagnosis; effusion size alone does not decide.

**Ref:** Robba C, et al. *Intensive Care Med* 2021.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W14-D3 · Review: respiratory

**1.** A 180-cm-tall man with ARDS is to be ventilated at 6 mL/kg predicted body weight. His tidal volume should be approximately:

- A. 360 mL
- B. 410 mL
- C. 450 mL
- D. 540 mL
- E. 600 mL

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.**

**Why C:** PBW (men) = 50 + 0.91 × (180 − 152.4) = 50 + 25.1 ≈ 75 kg. 6 mL/kg × 75 kg ≈ 450 mL.

**Why not the others:**
- A – 360 mL corresponds to ~60 kg, an underestimate of PBW.
- B – 410 mL corresponds to ~68 kg, a PBW calculation error.
- D – 540 mL corresponds to ~90 kg, typical of using actual rather than predicted weight.
- E – 600 mL is 8 mL/kg PBW, above the protective target.

**EDIC trap:** Measure the height; don't estimate it, and never use actual weight.

**Ref:** ARDS Network. *N Engl J Med* 2000;342:1301–1308.

</details>

**2.** Revision – respiratory:

- A. Prone positioning for ≥ 16 h/day is recommended in ARDS with P/F < 150 mmHg. — T / F
- B. ESICM 2023 recommends prolonged high-pressure recruitment manoeuvres. — T / F
- C. NIV used for established post-extubation respiratory failure delayed reintubation and increased mortality. — T / F
- D. In status asthmaticus, an I:E ratio of 1:1 is recommended to improve oxygenation. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – ESICM 2023 strongly recommends prone positioning for ≥ 16 h/day in ARDS with P/F < 150 mmHg (PROSEVA).
- B (False) – ESICM 2023 strongly recommends against prolonged high-pressure recruitment manoeuvres.
- C (True) – In Esteban 2004, NIV for established post-extubation failure did not prevent reintubation, delayed it, and increased ICU mortality (25 vs 14 %).
- D (False) – In status asthmaticus, prolong expiration with an I:E ratio of 1:3 or longer to limit dynamic hyperinflation.

**EDIC trap:** Rescue NIV after extubation is harmful.

**Ref:** Guérin C, et al. PROSEVA. *N Engl J Med* 2013;368:2159–2168; Esteban A, et al. *N Engl J Med* 2004;350:2452–2460; Grasselli G, et al. *Intensive Care Med* 2023;49:727–759.

</details>

**3.** A patient on pressure support 18 cmH₂O, PEEP 6 cmH₂O, breathes at RR 12/min with VT 10 mL/kg PBW. P0.1 is 0.6 cmH₂O. The best change is:

- A. Increase pressure support
- B. Reduce pressure support
- C. Switch to volume control
- D. Add a sedative infusion
- E. Increase PEEP to 12 cmH₂O

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

**Why B:** A P0.1 of 0.6 cmH₂O (below ~1), with a large V_T and low RR, indicates low respiratory drive from over-assistance. Reducing pressure support restores effort, limits ineffective efforts and prevents diaphragm disuse atrophy.

**Why not the others:**
- A – More pressure support worsens over-assistance.
- C – Volume control removes the patient's effort and promotes disuse.
- D – A sedative infusion would suppress an already low drive.
- E – Raising PEEP to 12 cmH₂O does not address over-assistance.

**EDIC trap:** Low P0.1 = over-assistance; high P0.1 (> 3.5 cmH₂O) = under-assistance or high drive.

**Ref:** Telias I, et al. *Am J Respir Crit Care Med* 2020;201:1086–1098.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W14-D4 · Review: acid–base and electrolytes

**1.** A patient has pH 7.18, HCO₃⁻ 12 mmol/L and PaCO₂ 5.3 kPa (40 mmHg). Which is correct?

- A. Compensated metabolic acidosis
- B. Metabolic acidosis with an additional respiratory acidosis
- C. Metabolic acidosis with an additional respiratory alkalosis
- D. Primary respiratory acidosis
- E. Normal compensation

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

**Why B:** Winter's formula: expected PaCO₂ = 1.5 × 12 + 8 = 26 ± 2 mmHg (≈ 3.5 kPa). The measured 40 mmHg is too high, so respiratory compensation is inadequate. That means a coexisting respiratory acidosis, from fatigue, sedation, neuromuscular weakness or airway obstruction.

**Why not the others:**
- A – Compensated metabolic acidosis would have a PaCO₂ of ~24–28 mmHg.
- C – An added respiratory alkalosis would push PaCO₂ below 24 mmHg.
- D – A primary respiratory acidosis would raise HCO₃⁻, not lower it to 12.
- E – PaCO₂ lies far outside the predicted range.

**EDIC trap:** A "normal" PaCO₂ in metabolic acidosis is abnormal.

**Ref:** Berend K, et al. *N Engl J Med* 2014;371:1434–1445.

</details>

**2.** A patient has Na⁺ 140, Cl⁻ 94 and HCO₃⁻ 22 mmol/L (normal anion gap 12). What is the most likely acid–base diagnosis?

- A. Normal acid–base status
- B. Normal anion gap metabolic acidosis
- C. High anion gap metabolic acidosis with concomitant metabolic alkalosis
- D. Pure high anion gap metabolic acidosis
- E. Respiratory alkalosis only

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.**

**Why C:** AG = 140 − (94 + 22) = 24, so ΔAG = 12, but HCO₃⁻ has fallen by only 2. The delta ratio is ≈ 6 (> 2), so something has added HCO₃⁻: a concomitant metabolic alkalosis, e.g. DKA with vomiting. The low Cl⁻ (94) supports chloride loss.

**Why not the others:**
- A – An AG of 24 is abnormal, despite the near-normal HCO₃⁻.
- B – The AG is high, not normal, and Cl⁻ is low rather than high.
- D – A pure HAGMA would lower HCO₃⁻ to ~12 (delta ratio 1–2).
- E – Respiratory alkalosis does not explain an AG of 24.

**EDIC trap:** A near-normal HCO₃⁻ can hide two opposing disorders, so always calculate the anion gap.

**Ref:** Berend K, et al. *N Engl J Med* 2014;371:1434–1445; Rastegar A. *J Am Soc Nephrol* 2007;18:2429–2431.

</details>

**3.** Revision – acid–base:

- A. Metabolic alkalosis with urine Cl⁻ > 20 mmol/L and hypertension suggests mineralocorticoid excess. — T / F
- B. In DIABOLO, acetazolamide shortened the duration of mechanical ventilation. — T / F
- C. A normal osmolar gap excludes toxic-alcohol poisoning. — T / F
- D. In DKA with K⁺ 3.2 mmol/L, insulin should be delayed until potassium is replaced. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – T.**

- A (True) – U-Cl⁻ > 20 with hypertension means chloride-resistant alkalosis from mineralocorticoid excess (hyperaldosteronism, Cushing's, liquorice).
- B (False) – DIABOLO: ventilation lasted 136.5 vs 163 h, NS.
- C (False) – Late presenters have already metabolised the alcohol to acids, so the osmolar gap may be normal while the AG is high.
- D (True) – If K⁺ is < 3.5 mmol/L, delay insulin and replace K⁺ first, because insulin drives K⁺ into cells and risks arrhythmias.

**EDIC trap:** A normal osmolar gap does not exclude toxic alcohols.

**Ref:** Faisy C, et al. DIABOLO. *JAMA* 2016;315:480–488; Umpierrez GE, et al. *Diabetes Care* 2024.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W14-D5 · Mini-mock: mixed topics W01–W13

**1.** A 70-year-old woman has *Klebsiella pneumoniae* bacteraemia from a CVC, which has been removed. She defervesces within 48 h and repeat cultures are negative. The total duration of antibiotics should be about:

- A. 3 days
- B. 7 days
- C. 14 days
- D. 4 weeks
- E. Until the CRP is normal

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

**Why B:** In Gram-negative CRBSI with the catheter removed, rapid defervescence and negative repeat cultures, 7 days in total is enough. BALANCE found 7 days non-inferior to 14 days for 90-day mortality in bloodstream infection, mostly with Gram-negatives.

**Why not the others:**
- A – 3 days is shorter than any trial-supported course for bacteraemia.
- C – 14 days is the minimum for *S. aureus*, or for a source that is not controlled.
- D – 4 weeks is for complicated infection (endocarditis, osteomyelitis, septic thrombosis).
- E – A normal CRP is not a validated rule for stopping antibiotics.

**EDIC trap:** Duration depends on the organism: Gram-negative 7 days, *S. aureus* ≥ 14 days, CoNS 5–7 days.

**Ref:** BALANCE. *N Engl J Med* 2025;392:1065–1078.

</details>

**2.** Mixed revision:

- A. A videolaryngoscope increases first-attempt intubation success in critically ill adults. — T / F
- B. In high-risk patients, HFNO alternating with NIV after extubation reduced reintubation compared with HFNO alone. — T / F
- C. In APROCCHSS, hydrocortisone plus fludrocortisone reduced 90-day mortality. — T / F
- D. Empirical micafungin improved invasive-fungal-infection-free survival in EMPIRICUS. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – DEVICE: videolaryngoscopy increased first-attempt success (85.1 vs 70.8 %).
- B (True) – HIGH-WEAN: NIV alternating with HFNO reduced reintubation in high-risk patients compared with HFNO alone.
- C (True) – APROCCHSS: hydrocortisone plus fludrocortisone reduced 90-day mortality (43.0 vs 49.1 %).
- D (False) – EMPIRICUS: IFI-free survival was 68 vs 60 %, NS. Only new IFIs were fewer.

**EDIC trap:** Empirical antifungals in non-neutropenic ICU patients have no proven benefit.

**Ref:** Prekker ME, et al. DEVICE. *N Engl J Med* 2023;389:418–429; Thille AW, et al. HIGH-WEAN. *JAMA* 2019;322:1465–1475; Timsit JF, et al. EMPIRICUS. *JAMA* 2016;316:1555–1564.

</details>

**3.** A passive ARDS patient has VT 450 mL (7.5 mL/kg PBW), PEEP 12 cmH₂O and plateau pressure 30 cmH₂O. The most appropriate change is:

- A. Increase VT to recruit the lung
- B. Reduce VT towards 6 mL/kg PBW to lower the plateau and driving pressure
- C. Perform a prolonged recruitment manoeuvre
- D. Reduce PEEP to 5 cmH₂O to lower the plateau
- E. No change – the plateau is acceptable

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

**Why B:** ΔP = 30 − 12 = 18 cmH₂O, above the ~15 linked to higher mortality, and Pplat is at the 30 cmH₂O ceiling. ESICM strongly recommends low VT (4–8 mL/kg PBW), so reducing VT towards 6 mL/kg lowers both Pplat and ΔP. Accept permissive hypercapnia and raise RR within limits.

**Why not the others:**
- A – Increasing VT raises Pplat and ΔP (volutrauma).
- C – Prolonged high-pressure recruitment manoeuvres are strongly recommended against.
- D – Dropping PEEP to 5 lowers Pplat but not ΔP, and risks derecruitment.
- E – Pplat 30 is the upper limit, and a ΔP of 18 is too high.

**EDIC trap:** A plateau at 30 with a high ΔP is not "fine".

**Ref:** Grasselli G, et al. *Intensive Care Med* 2023;49:727–759; Amato MBP, et al. *N Engl J Med* 2015;372:747–755.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W15-D1 · Traumatic brain injury

**1.** A 34-year-old motorcyclist is admitted with an isolated severe traumatic brain injury. The team wants to quote a 6-month outcome estimate to the family using the CRASH and IMPACT prognostic models. Regarding the variables in these models:

- A. The GCS motor score (not the total GCS) is one of the three variables of the IMPACT core model. — T / F
- B. Traumatic subarachnoid haemorrhage on the admission CT is a variable of the IMPACT extended model. — T / F
- C. Serum sodium is one of the laboratory variables of the IMPACT lab model. — T / F
- D. Intracranial pressure values recorded during the first 24 h are part of the IMPACT extended model. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – F.**

- A (True) – the IMPACT core model uses age, GCS motor score and pupillary reactivity; the motor score carries most prognostic information.
- B (True) – the extended model adds pre-hospital hypoxia and hypotension, Marshall CT class, traumatic SAH and epidural haematoma.
- C (False) – the lab model adds **glucose and haemoglobin**, not sodium.
- D (False) – IMPACT (and CRASH) are admission models; ICU-phase data such as ICP are not included.

**EDIC trap:** CRASH uses age, GCS, pupils, major extracranial injury and CT findings, with adjustment for country income; neither model should be used alone to limit care in an individual patient.

**Ref:** Steyerberg EW, PLoS Med 2008; MRC CRASH Trial Collaborators, BMJ 2008.

</details>

**2.** A 41-year-old woman with a severe diffuse TBI (GCS 6, Marshall III) is ventilated in a neuro-ICU. Regarding interventions according to the Brain Trauma Foundation 4th edition guideline and later trials:

- A. Early prophylactic hypothermia (33–35 °C) is recommended to improve outcome in diffuse injury. — T / F
- B. Prolonged prophylactic hyperventilation to a PaCO2 ≤ 3.3 kPa (25 mmHg) is not recommended. — T / F
- C. High-dose methylprednisolone is not recommended because it increased mortality. — T / F
- D. Phenytoin prophylaxis in the first week reduces the incidence of late (post-traumatic) epilepsy. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – F, B – T, C – T, D – F.**

- A (False) – BTF 4th edition does not recommend early (≤ 2.5 h), short-term prophylactic hypothermia in diffuse injury; POLAR (2018) confirmed no benefit, and Eurotherm3235 showed harm with hypothermia for ICP control.
- B (True) – prolonged prophylactic hyperventilation to PaCO₂ ≤ 3.3 kPa (25 mmHg) is not recommended (Level IIB).
- C (True) – steroids are contraindicated (Level I) because CRASH showed increased mortality.
- D (False) – phenytoin (or levetiracetam) reduces **early** (< 7 days) seizures only; it does not prevent late post-traumatic epilepsy.

**EDIC trap:** "prophylaxis prevents epilepsy" is a classic false statement – seizure prophylaxis is limited to 1 week.

**Ref:** Carney N, Neurosurgery 2017; Cooper DJ, JAMA 2018 (POLAR).

</details>

**3.** A 27-year-old man with diffuse TBI has an intraparenchymal ICP monitor. He is sedated with propofol and fentanyl, head-up 30°, normothermic, PaCO2 4.9 kPa, Na 141 mmol/L and MAP 90 mmHg. Over 15 min the ICP rises to 28 mmHg and stays there; CPP is 62 mmHg. A repeat CT shows diffuse swelling with no surgical lesion. Which is the most appropriate next step?

- A. Bolus of 20 % human albumin to expand plasma volume
- B. Thiopental infusion titrated to burst suppression
- C. Therapeutic hypothermia to 33 °C
- D. Bolus of hyperosmolar therapy (hypertonic saline or mannitol)
- E. Dexamethasone 10 mg IV followed by 4 mg 6-hourly

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.**

**Why D:** Sustained ICP > 22 mmHg despite tier-0 measures (sedation, head-up, normocapnia, normothermia, normal Na) with no surgical lesion calls for tier-1 SIBICC treatment: an intermittent bolus of hypertonic saline or mannitol. CPP is adequate, so osmotherapy is the logical next step.

**Why not the others:**
- A – albumin was associated with higher mortality in TBI (SAFE-TBI) and does not lower ICP.
- B – barbiturate coma is a tier-3 rescue with hypotension and infection risks.
- C – hypothermia to 33 °C worsened outcome when used for ICP control (Eurotherm3235); only 35–36 °C is a tier-3 option.
- E – corticosteroids increase mortality in TBI (CRASH); dexamethasone is for vasogenic tumour oedema.

**EDIC trap:** move up the SIBICC tiers stepwise and repeat CT before escalating.

**Ref:** Hawryluk GWJ, Intensive Care Med 2019 (SIBICC); Andrews PJD, N Engl J Med 2015.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W15-D2 · Subarachnoid haemorrhage

**1.** A 49-year-old woman with WFNS grade 3 aneurysmal SAH had her anterior communicating artery aneurysm coiled on day 1. Regarding prevention and detection of delayed cerebral ischaemia (DCI):

- A. Oral nimodipine 60 mg every 4 h for 21 days reduces the risk of poor outcome. — T / F
- B. Prophylactic induced hypertension in an asymptomatic patient is recommended once the aneurysm is secured. — T / F
- C. Prophylactic hypervolaemia improves cerebral blood flow and outcome. — T / F
- D. A mean MCA flow velocity > 200 cm/s with a Lindegaard ratio > 6 on transcranial Doppler suggests severe vasospasm. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – T.**

- A (True) – oral nimodipine 60 mg 4-hourly for 21 days is the only drug with proven outcome benefit; it reduces DCI and poor outcome rather than angiographic spasm.
- B (False) – **prophylactic** induced hypertension is not recommended; induced hypertension is a treatment for symptomatic DCI.
- C (False) – prophylactic hypervolaemia ("triple H") does not improve CBF or outcome and causes complications; aim for euvolaemia.
- D (True) – MCA mean velocity > 200 cm/s with a Lindegaard ratio (MCA/extracranial ICA) > 6 indicates severe vasospasm; a ratio < 3 suggests hyperaemia.

**EDIC trap:** angiographic vasospasm and DCI are not synonyms – nimodipine improves outcome without reducing spasm.

**Ref:** Hoh BL, Stroke 2023 (AHA/ASA aSAH guideline); Treggiari MM, Neurocrit Care 2023.

</details>

**2.** A 38-year-old man asks about his own risk of aneurysmal SAH after his sister died from one. Regarding risk factors for aneurysmal SAH:

- A. Autosomal dominant polycystic kidney disease increases the risk of intracranial aneurysm and SAH. — T / F
- B. Moderate regular alcohol intake is protective against aneurysmal SAH. — T / F
- C. Diabetes mellitus roughly doubles the risk of aneurysmal SAH. — T / F
- D. Screening for unruptured aneurysms is routinely recommended for anyone with a single affected first-degree relative. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – F.**

- A (True) – ADPKD is associated with a several-fold higher prevalence of intracranial aneurysms and SAH.
- B (False) – alcohol shows a dose-dependent **increase** in risk; smoking, hypertension and heavy alcohol are the main modifiable factors.
- C (False) – diabetes is not positively associated with aneurysmal SAH (some studies show an inverse association).
- D (False) – screening is usually advised when **≥ 2 first-degree relatives** are affected, or in ADPKD – not for a single affected relative.

**EDIC trap:** risk factors for aneurysmal SAH differ from atherosclerotic ones – diabetes and hypercholesterolaemia do not increase risk.

**Ref:** Feigin VL, Stroke 2005; Claassen J, Park S, Lancet 2022.

</details>

**3.** A 58-year-old woman with WFNS grade 2 SAH had a basilar tip aneurysm coiled on admission. Twenty hours later she becomes progressively drowsy (GCS 15 → 11) with impaired upward gaze. Pupils are equal and reactive. Na 138 mmol/L, glucose 7 mmol/L, temperature 37.2 °C. TCD velocities are normal. CT shows enlarged temporal horns and a rounded third ventricle with periventricular lucency, without new blood. Which is the most appropriate next step?

- A. Switch nimodipine to the intravenous route
- B. Start noradrenaline to raise MAP by 20 %
- C. Give 3 % hypertonic saline 250 ml
- D. Repeat cerebral angiography to exclude rebleeding
- E. Insert an external ventricular drain

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**E.**

**Why E:** Early decline in consciousness with impaired upgaze (dorsal midbrain compression), enlarged temporal horns, a rounded third ventricle and periventricular lucency is acute hydrocephalus. It complicates ~20–30 % of SAH and is treated with CSF diversion by EVD, which often produces rapid neurological improvement.

**Why not the others:**
- A – the route of nimodipine is irrelevant to hydrocephalus; IV nimodipine causes hypotension.
- B – induced hypertension treats DCI, which is unusual before day 3; TCD is normal.
- C – sodium is normal and hydrocephalus needs drainage, not osmotherapy.
- D – CT shows no new blood, arguing against rebleeding.

**EDIC trap:** in early SAH deterioration think rebleeding and hydrocephalus; from day 3–14 think DCI.

**Ref:** Hoh BL, Stroke 2023; Claassen J, Park S, Lancet 2022.

</details>
