**4.** A 46-year-old man with a clipped middle cerebral artery aneurysm develops new expressive aphasia and right arm drift on day 7 after SAH. CT shows no hydrocephalus, rebleeding or established infarct. He is euvolaemic, Na 139 mmol/L, glucose 6.8 mmol/L, and MAP 84 mmHg on oral nimodipine. Which is the most appropriate initial treatment?

- A. 2 L of colloid to achieve a CVP > 12 mmHg
- B. Noradrenaline to raise MAP stepwise, reassessing the neurological deficit
- C. Mannitol 1 g/kg
- D. Simvastatin 80 mg via nasogastric tube
- E. Switch to high-dose intravenous nimodipine

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

**Why B:** New focal deficit on day 7 with the aneurysm secured and CT excluding hydrocephalus, rebleeding and established infarction is symptomatic DCI. First-line treatment is a trial of induced hypertension with noradrenaline, raising MAP stepwise while reassessing the deficit; endovascular rescue (angioplasty/intra-arterial vasodilators) follows if refractory.

**Why not the others:**
- A – hypervolaemia/haemodilution is harmful; he is already euvolaemic.
- C – mannitol does not treat ischaemia and causes hypovolaemia.
- D – high-dose statins showed no benefit (STASH).
- E – IV nimodipine causes hypotension and has no proven advantage over oral.

**EDIC trap:** evidence for induced hypertension is weak (HIMALAIA stopped early), but it remains guideline-endorsed for symptomatic DCI.

**Ref:** Gathier CS, Stroke 2018 (HIMALAIA); Treggiari MM, Neurocrit Care 2023.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W15-D3 · Spontaneous intracerebral haemorrhage

**1.** A 67-year-old man has a 15-mL left putaminal haemorrhage with SBP 210 mmHg, 2 hours after onset. The best blood-pressure strategy is:

- A. Leave BP untreated to preserve cerebral perfusion
- B. Lower SBP to about 140 mmHg within 1 hour, avoiding falls below 130 mmHg
- C. Lower SBP to 110 mmHg as fast as possible
- D. Lower SBP to below 180 mmHg over 24 hours only
- E. Lower MAP by 50 % in the first hour

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

**Why B:** For spontaneous ICH with SBP 150–220 mmHg presenting early, guidelines support lowering SBP to ~140 mmHg (range 130–150) promptly and smoothly, avoiding large variability. INTERACT3, with a care bundle including early intensive BP lowering (< 140 mmHg within 1 h), glycaemic control, fever treatment and anticoagulation reversal, improved functional outcome.

**Why not the others:**
- A – untreated SBP 210 mmHg favours haematoma expansion.
- C – lowering to 110 mmHg is excessive; ATACH-2 (target 110–139) showed no benefit and more renal adverse events.
- D – a 24-hour target < 180 mmHg is too slow and too lenient.
- E – a 50 % MAP reduction risks ischaemia.

**EDIC trap:** lowering SBP below 130 mmHg is potentially harmful and is not recommended.

**Ref:** Greenberg SM, Stroke 2022 (AHA/ASA ICH guideline); Ma L, Lancet 2023 (INTERACT3).

</details>

**2.** A 75-year-old woman taking apixaban has a lobar ICH. Which is an appropriate reversal strategy?

- A. Idarucizumab 5 g IV
- B. Vitamin K 10 mg IV alone
- C. Platelet transfusion
- D. Four-factor PCC 50 IU/kg (or andexanet alfa where available)
- E. Protamine sulfate

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.**

**Why D:** Apixaban is a factor Xa inhibitor. Reversal options are four-factor PCC 50 IU/kg or andexanet alfa where available. ANNEXA-I (n = 530) showed better haemostatic efficacy with andexanet than usual care (mostly PCC) (67.0 vs 53.1 %), but more thrombotic events, including ischaemic stroke, and no difference in mortality or functional outcome.

**Why not the others:**
- A – idarucizumab reverses dabigatran (direct thrombin inhibitor).
- B – vitamin K reverses warfarin and is ineffective for DOACs.
- C – platelet transfusion does not reverse anticoagulants (and was harmful for antiplatelet ICH in PATCH).
- E – protamine reverses unfractionated heparin (partially LMWH).

**EDIC trap:** match the reversal agent to the drug – and check time since last dose.

**Ref:** Connolly SJ, N Engl J Med 2024 (ANNEXA-I); Greenberg SM, Stroke 2022.

</details>

**3.** Regarding the ICU management of spontaneous ICH:

- A. Platelet transfusion is recommended for patients on antiplatelet drugs. — T / F
- B. Cerebellar haemorrhage > 15 mL or with hydrocephalus is an indication for evacuation. — T / F
- C. Prophylactic anti-seizure medication is recommended. — T / F
- D. The ICH score should not be used to limit care in the first 24 hours. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – F, B – T, C – F, D – T.**

- A (False) – PATCH – harm.
- B (True) – Surgical evacuation.
- C (False) – Not recommended without seizures.
- D (True) – Avoid early DNR/limitation – self-fulfilling prophecy.

**EDIC trap:** Platelets for antiplatelet ICH – no (PATCH).

**Ref:** Baharoglu MI, et al. PATCH. *Lancet* 2016;387:2605–2613.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W15-D4 · Acute stroke

**1.** A 66-year-old man is found by his wife at 08:00 with left hemiparesis, left neglect and dysarthria; he was last seen well at 23:00. NIHSS is 14, glucose 6.1 mmol/L, BP 172/94 mmHg. Non-contrast CT shows no haemorrhage (ASPECTS 8); CT angiography shows a right M1 occlusion and CT perfusion a small ischaemic core with a large penumbra. Which is the most appropriate management?

- A. Aspirin 300 mg and admission to the stroke unit
- B. Lower BP to < 140/90 mmHg and repeat imaging in 24 h
- C. Endovascular thrombectomy
- D. Unfractionated heparin infusion
- E. Early decompressive hemicraniectomy

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.**

**Why C:** This is a wake-up stroke (9 h since last seen well) with a right M1 occlusion, ASPECTS 8 and a small core with a large penumbra. DAWN and DEFUSE-3 showed that thrombectomy improves functional outcome up to 24 h from last seen well with favourable imaging, and later trials extended benefit to large cores.

**Why not the others:**
- A – aspirin alone leaves a salvageable penumbra untreated.
- B – BP only needs to be ≤ 185/110 mmHg around reperfusion; aggressive lowering risks penumbral infarction.
- D – heparin has no role in acute ischaemic stroke and increases bleeding.
- E – hemicraniectomy is for malignant oedema, not for a salvageable penumbra.

**EDIC trap:** IV thrombolysis can also be considered with imaging selection (WAKE-UP, EXTEND), but must not delay thrombectomy.

**Ref:** Nogueira RG, N Engl J Med 2018 (DAWN); Albers GW, N Engl J Med 2018 (DEFUSE-3); Turc G, Eur Stroke J 2023.

</details>

**2.** A 52-year-old woman with a left MCA infarct (> 50 % of the territory) was independent before the stroke. She becomes drowsy (GCS 15 → 11) 30 h after onset despite head elevation and normoglycaemia; repeat CT shows space-occupying oedema with 6 mm midline shift. The family asks about decompressive hemicraniectomy. All of the following statements are consistent with current evidence EXCEPT:

- A. Surgery within 48 h reduces mortality in patients aged ≤ 60 years.
- B. In patients > 60 years surgery increases survival, but most survivors have moderately severe or severe disability.
- C. Dominant-hemisphere infarction is not by itself a contraindication to surgery.
- D. Osmotherapy may temporise but has not been shown to improve outcome.
- E. Sustained ICP > 20 mmHg on an invasive monitor should be documented before surgery is offered.

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**E.**

**Why E:** The decision to perform hemicraniectomy for malignant MCA infarction is clinical and radiological (infarct volume > 50 % MCA territory, midline shift, falling consciousness). ICP monitoring is not required, and ICP can be normal despite life-threatening shift because the mass effect is lateral and focal.

**Why not the others:**
- A – pooled DECIMAL/DESTINY/HAMLET data show a large mortality reduction in patients ≤ 60 years operated within 48 h.
- B – DESTINY II: surgery in > 60 years increased survival, but most survivors had mRS 4–5.
- C – aphasia (dominant hemisphere) is not an exclusion; many survivors accept the outcome.
- D – osmotherapy can temporise but has not been shown to improve outcome.

**EDIC trap:** shared decision-making about acceptable disability is essential, particularly in older patients.

**Ref:** Vahedi K, Lancet Neurol 2007; Jüttler E, N Engl J Med 2014 (DESTINY II); van der Worp HB, Eur Stroke J 2021.

</details>

**3.** A 59-year-old man presents with vertigo, vomiting, dysarthria and fluctuating consciousness. Regarding posterior circulation stroke:

- A. Thrombectomy for acute basilar artery occlusion improves functional outcome compared with best medical treatment in selected patients up to 12–24 h. — T / F
- B. In an acute vestibular syndrome, a normal head-impulse test is reassuring for a peripheral cause. — T / F
- C. Early diffusion-weighted MRI can be falsely negative in brainstem infarction. — T / F
- D. For a space-occupying cerebellar infarct with brainstem compression, an EVD alone is preferred to suboccipital decompressive craniectomy. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – ATTENTION (≤ 12 h) and BAOCHE (6–24 h) (NEJM 2022) showed better functional outcome with thrombectomy for basilar artery occlusion.
- B (False) – in an acute continuous vestibular syndrome, a **normal** head-impulse test suggests a **central** (stroke) cause; an abnormal (corrective saccade) test suggests a peripheral lesion (HINTS).
- C (True) – DWI is falsely negative in up to ~20 % of small posterior-fossa infarcts within 24–48 h.
- D (False) – space-occupying cerebellar infarction with brainstem compression needs suboccipital decompressive craniectomy (± EVD); EVD alone risks upward herniation.

**EDIC trap:** HINTS is reassuring only when it fits a peripheral pattern – a normal head impulse is the dangerous finding.

**Ref:** Tao C, N Engl J Med 2022; Jovin TG, N Engl J Med 2022; Wijdicks EFM, Stroke 2014.

</details>

**4.** A 74-year-old woman presents 2 h after onset of a right hemiparesis due to an ischaemic stroke; she has known atrial fibrillation not on anticoagulation. Regarding early management:

- A. Tenecteplase 0.25 mg/kg is an accepted alternative to alteplase for IV thrombolysis. — T / F
- B. BP should be lowered to ≤ 185/110 mmHg before thrombolysis is started. — T / F
- C. Prophylactic-dose LMWH started within 24 h reduces mortality and is preferred to intermittent pneumatic compression. — T / F
- D. For minor-to-moderate stroke, starting a DOAC within 48 h is as safe as delaying it to 6–14 days. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – T.**

- A (True) – ESO 2023 recommends tenecteplase 0.25 mg/kg as an alternative to alteplase 0.9 mg/kg.
- B (True) – BP must be ≤ 185/110 mmHg before lysis and < 180/105 mmHg for 24 h afterwards.
- C (False) – in immobile stroke patients, **intermittent pneumatic compression** reduced DVT (CLOTS 3); early prophylactic LMWH has no proven mortality benefit and increases bleeding, so it is not preferred to IPC.
- D (True) – ELAN (2023) and OPTIMAS (2024) found early DOAC initiation no worse than delayed initiation, without more symptomatic haemorrhage.

**EDIC trap:** early DOAC timing in ELAN depended on stroke size (≤ 48 h minor/moderate, day 6–7 major).

**Ref:** Alamowitch S, Eur Stroke J 2023; Fischer U, N Engl J Med 2023 (ELAN); Werring DJ, Lancet 2024 (OPTIMAS); CLOTS 3 Collaboration, Lancet 2013.

</details>

**5.** A 71-year-old man presents 90 min after the sudden onset of a right homonymous hemianopia, expressive and receptive dysphasia, and weakness of the right face, arm and leg. He is right-handed, takes no anticoagulants and his glucose is 5.8 mmol/L. CT shows no haemorrhage. According to the Oxfordshire (Bamford) classification, which category fits best?

- A. Lacunar syndrome (LACS)
- B. Partial anterior circulation syndrome (PACS)
- C. Posterior circulation syndrome (POCS)
- D. Total anterior circulation syndrome (TACS)
- E. Transient ischaemic attack

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.**

**Why D:** TACS requires all three of: higher cortical dysfunction (dysphasia, neglect, visuospatial disorder), homonymous visual field defect and motor/sensory deficit in ≥ 2 of face, arm and leg. He has dysphasia, right hemianopia and face–arm–leg weakness. TACS carries the worst prognosis (~40 % 30-day mortality) and usually reflects proximal MCA/ICA occlusion – a thrombectomy candidate.

**Why not the others:**
- A – LACS is pure motor/sensory (or sensorimotor/ataxic hemiparesis) without cortical signs.
- B – PACS has two of the three components or isolated cortical dysfunction.
- C – POCS involves brainstem/cerebellar signs or isolated hemianopia.
- E – symptoms are ongoing, so this is not a TIA.

**EDIC trap:** a hemianopia alone is POCS; with dysphasia and hemiparesis it is TACS.

**Ref:** Bamford J, Lancet 1991.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W15-D5 · Status epilepticus

**1.** A 45-year-old man has been having a generalised convulsive seizure for 7 minutes on arrival of the ambulance; no IV access can be obtained. Regarding drug treatment of convulsive status epilepticus:

- A. Intramuscular midazolam 10 mg is an effective first-line alternative to IV lorazepam when venous access is not available. — T / F
- B. After failure of benzodiazepines, levetiracetam, fosphenytoin and valproate have similar efficacy. — T / F
- C. Status epilepticus is termed refractory when seizures continue after a benzodiazepine and one second-line antiseizure drug. — T / F
- D. Rocuronium terminates electrographic seizure activity and facilitates ventilation. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – RAMPART: IM midazolam 10 mg (autoinjector) was at least as effective as IV lorazepam pre-hospital and faster to give.
- B (True) – ESETT: levetiracetam 60 mg/kg, fosphenytoin 20 mg PE/kg and valproate 40 mg/kg each terminated seizures in ~50 %, with similar adverse events.
- C (True) – refractory SE = seizures continuing after an adequate benzodiazepine and one second-line antiseizure drug; anaesthetic agents (midazolam, propofol, thiopental) with continuous EEG are then needed.
- D (False) – neuromuscular blockers abolish only motor signs; electrographic seizures continue, so EEG is mandatory if they are used.

**EDIC trap:** give an adequate first benzodiazepine dose early – under-dosing is the commonest error.

**Ref:** Silbergleit R, N Engl J Med 2012; Kapur J, N Engl J Med 2019; Trinka E, Epilepsia 2015.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W16-D1 · Analgesia and sedation

**1.** A 63-year-old man with Child–Pugh C alcohol-related cirrhosis is intubated for severe community-acquired pneumonia. He has oliguric AKI (creatinine 310 µmol/L) and is not yet on renal replacement therapy. He needs an opioid infusion for analgesia-based sedation to tolerate the ventilator. Which opioid has pharmacokinetics least affected by his hepatic and renal dysfunction?

- A. Remifentanil
- B. Morphine
- C. Oxycodone
- D. Pethidine (meperidine)
- E. Codeine

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.**

**Why A:** Remifentanil is hydrolysed by non-specific blood and tissue esterases, so its context-sensitive half-time (~3–4 min) is unchanged by hepatic or renal failure. Its metabolite, remifentanil acid, accumulates in renal failure but has negligible activity.

**Why not the others:**
- B – morphine-6-glucuronide (active) and morphine-3-glucuronide accumulate in renal failure.
- C – oxycodone depends on hepatic CYP metabolism and renal excretion; both are impaired.
- D – norpethidine accumulates in renal failure and causes seizures.
- E – codeine is a prodrug with unpredictable CYP2D6 activation; its metabolites accumulate in AKI.

**EDIC trap:** "least affected by organ failure" points to esterase metabolism – remifentanil (opioid) and cisatracurium (Hofmann elimination) among NMBAs.

**Ref:** Devlin JW, Crit Care Med 2018 (PADIS); Breen D, Crit Care 2004.

</details>

**2.** A 48-year-old man on long-term oxycodone for chronic back pain is in ICU after an emergency laparotomy. His thoracic epidural fell out on day 1. Despite escalating IV morphine PCA his pain score remains 8/10, limiting coughing. He is on low-dose noradrenaline, creatinine is rising, and he has a prolonged ileus. Which is the most appropriate additional analgesic strategy?

- A. IV ketorolac 30 mg 8-hourly
- B. IV clonidine infusion
- C. Gabapentin via nasogastric tube
- D. Double the morphine PCA bolus dose
- E. Low-dose IV ketamine infusion

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**E.**

**Why E:** Low-dose ketamine (e.g. 0.1–0.3 mg/kg/h) is an effective opioid-sparing adjunct, particularly in opioid-tolerant patients and after major abdominal surgery. PADIS suggests it as an adjunct in post-surgical ICU adults; it does not depress respiration or worsen hypotension or ileus.

**Why not the others:**
- A – NSAIDs are inadvisable with rising creatinine and vasopressor requirement.
- B – clonidine worsens hypotension.
- C – gabapentinoids need enteral absorption (prolonged ileus) and act slowly.
- D – further opioid escalation worsens ileus and does not overcome tolerance (or opioid-induced hyperalgesia).

**EDIC trap:** multimodal analgesia is chosen by excluding what the patient's organ dysfunction forbids.

**Ref:** Devlin JW, Crit Care Med 2018 (PADIS); Brinck ECV, Cochrane Database Syst Rev 2018.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W16-D2 · Delirium

**1.** A 72-year-old woman is on day 5 of mechanical ventilation for pneumonia. Sedation has been stopped for a spontaneous breathing trial, but she is RASS +3, CAM-ICU positive and repeatedly tries to pull out her tube despite reorientation, family presence and pain control. She has no history of alcohol use. QTc is 520 ms. Which drug is the most appropriate choice?

- A. Lorazepam IV bolus
- B. Dexmedetomidine infusion
- C. Haloperidol 5 mg IV 6-hourly
- D. Quetiapine via nasogastric tube
- E. Propofol infusion to RASS −4

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

**Why B:** For agitated delirium that prevents weaning or extubation, after non-pharmacological measures and analgesia, PADIS suggests dexmedetomidine. DahLIA showed more ventilator-free hours at 7 days with dexmedetomidine than placebo. It does not prolong QT, an advantage with QTc 520 ms.

**Why not the others:**
- A – benzodiazepines are a delirium risk factor; there is no alcohol withdrawal to justify them.
- C – haloperidol prolongs QT (already 520 ms) and does not shorten delirium (MIND-USA, AID-ICU).
- D – quetiapine also prolongs QT and its absorption is delayed via NG tube.
- E – deep propofol sedation delays weaning and is not a delirium treatment.

**EDIC trap:** QTc > 500 ms is a common threshold to avoid antipsychotics.

**Ref:** Reade MC, JAMA 2016 (DahLIA); Devlin JW, Crit Care Med 2018 (PADIS); Andersen-Ranberg NC, N Engl J Med 2022.

</details>

**2.** A surgical ICU wants to reduce delirium among its elderly ventilated patients. Regarding delirium prevention:

- A. Higher compliance with the ABCDEF bundle is associated with less delirium and coma. — T / F
- B. Prophylactic low-dose haloperidol in high-risk patients reduced 28-day mortality in the REDUCE trial. — T / F
- C. Non-pharmacological sleep promotion (earplugs, eye masks, noise reduction) is suggested by PADIS. — T / F
- D. Rivastigmine added to haloperidol shortens delirium duration. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – in > 15 000 patients, higher ABCDEF bundle compliance was associated with less delirium, coma, restraint use and mortality (cohort data).
- B (False) – REDUCE (haloperidol 1–2 mg 8-hourly, n = 1789) showed no effect on 28-day survival or delirium incidence.
- C (True) – PADIS suggests sleep-promoting protocols, including noise and light reduction (earplugs, eye masks).
- D (False) – the rivastigmine add-on trial was stopped early because of **higher mortality** and longer delirium duration.

**EDIC trap:** non-pharmacological strategies are the only measures with supportive evidence for delirium prevention.

**Ref:** Pun BT, Crit Care Med 2019; van den Boogaard M, JAMA 2018; van Eijk MM, Lancet 2010.

</details>

**3.** A nurse is assessing a 66-year-old ventilated patient with the CAM-ICU. Regarding this tool:

- A. A patient with a RASS of −4 cannot be assessed and is considered comatose. — T / F
- B. Inattention (feature 2) is tested with the Mini-Mental State Examination. — T / F
- C. A positive result requires all four features to be present. — T / F
- D. A known diagnosis of dementia precludes the use of CAM-ICU. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – F.**

- A (True) – assessment starts with the RASS; at −4/−5 the patient is comatose, cannot be assessed and is reassessed later.
- B (False) – inattention is tested with the letters test ("SAVEAHAART" – squeeze on "A") or picture recognition, not the MMSE.
- C (False) – CAM-ICU is positive with Feature 1 (acute change/fluctuation) + Feature 2 (inattention) + **either** Feature 3 (RASS ≠ 0) **or** Feature 4 (disorganised thinking).
- D (False) – it remains valid in dementia, because it detects acute change from the patient's baseline.

**EDIC trap:** "all four features" is the classic false statement – three are sufficient.

**Ref:** Ely EW, JAMA 2001; Devlin JW, Crit Care Med 2018 (PADIS).

</details>

**4.** A 57-year-old man has a 6-day episode of ICU delirium after septic shock. Regarding the epidemiology and consequences of ICU delirium:

- A. Longer duration of delirium is independently associated with worse long-term cognitive impairment. — T / F
- B. Hyperactive delirium is the most common subtype in ventilated patients. — T / F
- C. An Intensive Care Delirium Screening Checklist (ICDSC) score ≥ 4 indicates delirium. — T / F
- D. Haloperidol or ziprasidone shortened delirium duration compared with placebo in MIND-USA. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – BRAIN-ICU: longer delirium duration independently predicted worse global cognition at 3 and 12 months.
- B (False) – hypoactive and mixed forms predominate; pure hyperactive delirium is uncommon, and hypoactive delirium is often missed.
- C (True) – the ICDSC (8 items) indicates delirium at ≥ 4; 1–3 is subsyndromal delirium.
- D (False) – MIND-USA found no difference in days alive without delirium or coma with haloperidol or ziprasidone versus placebo.

**EDIC trap:** antipsychotics are for distressing symptoms, not for shortening delirium.

**Ref:** Pandharipande PP, N Engl J Med 2013; Girard TD, N Engl J Med 2018; Bergeron N, Intensive Care Med 2001.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W16-D3 · Neuromuscular disorders

**1.** A 26-year-old student develops progressive limb weakness 10 days after a diarrhoeal illness. Regarding the epidemiology and immunology of Guillain–Barré syndrome (GBS):

- A. About two-thirds of patients report an infection in the weeks before onset. — T / F
- B. Anti-GQ1b antibodies are characteristic of the pure motor axonal (AMAN) variant. — T / F
- C. The annual incidence is about 20 per 100 000 population. — T / F
- D. Weakness typically continues to progress for more than 8 weeks before reaching its nadir. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – F.**

- A (True) – about two-thirds report a preceding respiratory or gastrointestinal infection, most often Campylobacter jejuni (also CMV, EBV, Mycoplasma, Zika, SARS-CoV-2).
- B (False) – anti-GQ1b antibodies characterise Miller Fisher syndrome and Bickerstaff encephalitis; AMAN is associated with anti-GM1/GD1a.
- C (False) – annual incidence is ~1–2 per 100 000.
- D (False) – the nadir is reached within 4 weeks (usually ~2 weeks); progression beyond 8 weeks suggests CIDP (acute-onset CIDP if 4–8 weeks or treatment-related fluctuations).

**EDIC trap:** Campylobacter-associated GBS is often axonal (AMAN) with slower recovery.

**Ref:** Shahrizaila N, Lancet 2021; Leonhard SE, Nat Rev Neurol 2019.

</details>

**2.** Several patients with neuromuscular disease need emergency intubation. Regarding the use of neuromuscular blocking drugs:

- A. In Duchenne muscular dystrophy, suxamethonium can cause hyperkalaemic cardiac arrest. — T / F
- B. In myotonic dystrophy, suxamethonium can cause generalised myotonic contracture that makes ventilation difficult. — T / F
- C. Patients with myasthenia gravis are less sensitive than normal to non-depolarising neuromuscular blockers. — T / F
- D. Sugammadex can reverse rocuronium-induced block in myasthenia gravis. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – T.**

- A (True) – dystrophic muscle can release large amounts of potassium (rhabdomyolysis) after suxamethonium, causing hyperkalaemic arrest, especially in undiagnosed boys.
- B (True) – suxamethonium can trigger a generalised myotonic contracture, including masseter rigidity, making ventilation and intubation difficult.
- C (False) – myasthenic patients are resistant to suxamethonium but **very sensitive** to non-depolarising agents; use reduced doses with quantitative monitoring.
- D (True) – sugammadex encapsulates rocuronium independently of acetylcholinesterase and has been used successfully in MG, avoiding neostigmine.

**EDIC trap:** in MG, avoid neostigmine reversal – it may precipitate cholinergic weakness.

**Ref:** Martyn JAJ, Richtsfeld M, Anesthesiology 2006; Blichfeldt-Lauridsen L, Hansen BD, Acta Anaesthesiol Scand 2012.

</details>

**3.** A 61-year-old woman with generalised myasthenia gravis is admitted to ICU with pneumonia and hypomagnesaemia. Which of the following drugs are recognised to precipitate or worsen myasthenic weakness?

- A. Intravenous magnesium sulphate — T / F
- B. Azithromycin — T / F
- C. Ceftriaxone — T / F
- D. Paracetamol — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – F.**

- A (True) – magnesium reduces presynaptic acetylcholine release and can provoke myasthenic crisis; correct hypomagnesaemia cautiously with monitoring.
- B (True) – macrolides impair neuromuscular transmission (FDA warning), as do fluoroquinolones and aminoglycosides.
- C (False) – third-generation cephalosporins such as ceftriaxone are considered safe choices for pneumonia in MG.
- D (False) – paracetamol does not affect neuromuscular transmission.

**EDIC trap:** other precipitants include beta-blockers, immune checkpoint inhibitors, quinine, botulinum toxin and aminoglycosides.

**Ref:** Sheikh S, Muscle Nerve 2021; Narayanaswami P, Neurology 2021.

</details>

**4.** A 38-year-old man with 4 days of ascending weakness and areflexia is in a high-dependency unit receiving IVIg. He now has a weak cough, difficulty swallowing saliva and bifacial weakness. SpO2 is 97 % on air and PaCO2 5.1 kPa. Bedside vital capacity has fallen from 32 to 17 ml/kg over 12 h, and maximal inspiratory pressure is −25 cmH2O. Which is the most appropriate next step?

- A. Elective endotracheal intubation in the ICU
- B. Non-invasive ventilation via a face mask
- C. Continue observation until PaCO2 rises above 6.5 kPa
- D. Switch from IVIg to plasma exchange and repeat vital capacity in 6 h
- E. Start pyridostigmine

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.**

**Why A:** He meets the "20/30/40 rule" (VC 17 ml/kg < 20, MIP −25 cmH₂O weaker than −30), with a rapid fall in VC, bulbar weakness and bifacial palsy. These predict respiratory failure; intubation should be elective and controlled, before hypoxaemia or hypercapnia, which are late signs.

**Why not the others:**
- B – NIV is unsafe with bulbar dysfunction and aspiration risk, and it delays definitive airway control.
- C – waiting for hypercapnia risks a crash intubation with dysautonomic instability.
- D – plasma exchange after IVIg is not beneficial and does not address the imminent airway problem.
- E – pyridostigmine has no role in GBS.

**EDIC trap:** normal SpO₂ and PaCO₂ do not mean respiratory safety in neuromuscular weakness – follow VC and MIP trends.

**Ref:** Lawn ND, Arch Neurol 2001; Shahrizaila N, Lancet 2021.

</details>

**5.** A 31-year-old woman reports 3 weeks of drooping eyelids and double vision that are worse in the evening, and tiredness of her jaw towards the end of meals. Neck flexion weakens after repeated testing. Reflexes, sensation and CK are normal. CT chest shows an anterior mediastinal mass. Which is the most likely diagnosis?

- A. Lambert–Eaton myasthenic syndrome
- B. Polymyositis
- C. Myasthenia gravis
- D. Miller Fisher syndrome
- E. Myotonic dystrophy

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.**

**Why C:** Fatigable ptosis and diplopia worse in the evening, fatigable jaw and neck weakness, normal reflexes, sensation and CK, plus an anterior mediastinal mass (thymoma or thymic hyperplasia), are typical of acetylcholine-receptor antibody myasthenia gravis. Thymectomy is indicated for thymoma.

**Why not the others:**
- A – LEMS causes proximal leg weakness, reduced reflexes that improve after exercise and autonomic features, and is associated with small-cell lung cancer.
- B – polymyositis raises CK and is not fatigable or ocular.
- D – Miller Fisher syndrome gives ophthalmoplegia, ataxia and areflexia after infection.
- E – myotonic dystrophy has myotonia and distal weakness.

**EDIC trap:** fatigability plus ocular involvement plus normal reflexes = MG; areflexia should prompt LEMS or GBS variants.

**Ref:** Gilhus NE, N Engl J Med 2016.

</details>

**6.** A 44-year-old man with suspected GBS has a lumbar puncture on day 3 of symptoms. Regarding CSF findings:

- A. CSF protein may still be normal in the first week of illness. — T / F
- B. A CSF white cell count > 50 cells/µl should prompt a search for an alternative diagnosis. — T / F
- C. A low CSF glucose is typical of GBS. — T / F
- D. Albuminocytological dissociation is specific to GBS. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – F.**

- A (True) – CSF protein is raised in only ~50 % in the first week (~90 % by week 3), so a normal early result does not exclude GBS.
- B (True) – mild pleocytosis (5–50/µl) is compatible, but > 50/µl suggests infection (HIV, Lyme, CMV polyradiculitis), leptomeningeal malignancy or sarcoidosis.
- C (False) – CSF glucose is normal in GBS; a low glucose suggests infection or malignancy.
- D (False) – albuminocytological dissociation is not specific: it also occurs in CIDP, spinal block, diabetes and other conditions.

**EDIC trap:** GBS is a clinical diagnosis supported by CSF and nerve conduction studies – never delay treatment for a normal early LP.

**Ref:** Leonhard SE, Nat Rev Neurol 2019; Fokke C, Brain 2014.

</details>

**7.** A 29-year-old man who injects black-tar heroin into his thigh presents with 2 days of double vision, slurred speech and difficulty swallowing. He is alert and afebrile, has bilateral ptosis, poorly reactive dilated pupils, a dry mouth and symmetrical weakness of the neck and arms; sensation is normal. There is an indurated abscess on his thigh. In addition to supportive care and wound debridement, which treatment is most important?

- A. Intravenous immunoglobulin
- B. Plasma exchange
- C. Pyridostigmine
- D. Benzylpenicillin alone
- E. Botulinum antitoxin

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**E.**

**Why E:** Descending symmetrical paralysis starting with cranial nerves, autonomic signs (dilated poorly reactive pupils, dry mouth), normal sensation and preserved consciousness in a black-tar heroin injector with an abscess = wound botulism. Give botulinum antitoxin as early as possible on clinical suspicion, without waiting for laboratory confirmation, because it neutralises only circulating toxin.

**Why not the others:**
- A – IVIg is a GBS treatment and does not neutralise toxin.
- B – plasma exchange is a GBS/MG treatment, not for botulism.
- C – pyridostigmine is ineffective in botulism.
- D – antibiotics (penicillin or metronidazole) are given after antitoxin with debridement; alone they do not treat toxin already released.

**EDIC trap:** descending paralysis with dilated pupils = botulism; ascending with areflexia = GBS.

**Ref:** Rao AK, MMWR Recomm Rep 2021 (CDC botulism guidelines).

</details>


## Case-style practice · W16-D4 · Brain death and confirmation of death

**1.** A 53-year-old man with a massive intracerebral haemorrhage is being tested for death by neurological criteria according to the UK Code of Practice. Regarding the brainstem reflex tests:

- A. The vestibulo-ocular reflex is tested with at least 50 ml of ice-cold water instilled into each external auditory meatus over 1 minute. — T / F
- B. Absent gag and cough reflexes indicate loss of glossopharyngeal and vagal (medullary) function. — T / F
- C. The pupils need not be dilated, but must be fixed and unresponsive to bright light. — T / F
- D. Preserved deep tendon reflexes in the legs preclude the diagnosis. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – F.**

- A (True) – the UK Code requires at least 50 ml of ice-cold water injected slowly over 1 minute into each external auditory meatus, with tympanic membranes visualised; any eye movement precludes the diagnosis.
- B (True) – absent gag (posterior pharynx stimulation) and cough (suction catheter to the carina) reflexes indicate loss of glossopharyngeal and vagal (medullary) function.
- C (True) – pupils must be fixed and unresponsive to bright light; they need not be dilated, and mid-sized fixed pupils are compatible.
- D (False) – spinal reflexes (tendon reflexes, triple flexion, Lazarus sign) can persist and do not preclude the diagnosis.

**EDIC trap:** the 2025 UK Code requires both eyes and both ears to be examinable; otherwise an ancillary investigation is needed.

**Ref:** Academy of Medical Royal Colleges, Code of Practice for the Diagnosis and Confirmation of Death 2025; Greer DM, JAMA 2020 (World Brain Death Project).

</details>

**2.** A 30-year-old woman with hypoxic brain injury after cardiac arrest is being considered for brainstem death testing in the UK. Which of the following are correct preconditions or apnoea-test requirements?

- A. Before the apnoea test PaCO2 should be ≥ 6.0 kPa with pH < 7.40, and it must rise by > 0.5 kPa with no respiratory effort. — T / F
- B. Blood glucose should be between 3.0 and 20 mmol/L. — T / F
- C. Serum sodium must be between 135 and 145 mmol/L. — T / F
- D. Core temperature must be ≥ 36 °C. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – F, B – T, C – F, D – T.**

- A (False) – these are the old 2008 values. The 2025 UK Code uses an augmented CO₂ apnoea test off the ventilator: starting PaCO₂ ≥ 5.3 kPa, end-point PaCO₂ ≥ 8.0 kPa with pH < 7.30 and a rise ≥ 2.7 kPa, over at least 5 min, with no respiratory effort.
- B (True) – glucose must be 3.0–20.0 mmol/L before testing.
- C (False) – sodium must be **125–160 mmol/L** (lower limit raised from 115 in 2008), not a normal range of 135–145.
- D (True) – core temperature must be ≥ 36 °C at testing (raised from > 34 °C); after hypothermia < 36 °C, wait 24 h after rewarming.

**EDIC trap:** older MCQs quote 34 °C, Na 115 and a 0.5 kPa rise – all superseded since January 2025.

**Ref:** Academy of Medical Royal Colleges, Code of Practice for the Diagnosis and Confirmation of Death 2025; Greer DM, JAMA 2020.

</details>

**3.** An 84-year-old man with metastatic cancer, on a palliative pathway in a monitored ward bed, has a cardiorespiratory arrest. Regarding confirmation of death after circulatory arrest (UK practice):

- A. Death should be confirmed only after a minimum of 5 minutes of observed continuous cardiorespiratory arrest. — T / F
- B. An EEG must be performed before death after circulatory arrest is confirmed. — T / F
- C. If spontaneous cardiac activity returns during the observation period, the 5 minutes continue from where they were interrupted. — T / F
- D. Ancillary imaging (e.g. CT angiography) is mandatory in all UK diagnoses of death by neurological criteria. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – F.**

- A (True) – death is confirmed after a minimum of 5 minutes of observed continuous cardiorespiratory arrest (mechanical asystole and apnoea), supported by ECG, echo or arterial trace; then absent pupillary and motor responses (and corneal reflexes) are checked.
- B (False) – EEG is not part of confirming death after circulatory arrest.
- C (False) – any spontaneous return of cardiac or respiratory activity requires a **fresh** 5-minute observation from the next arrest.
- D (False) – in the UK, ancillary tests are needed only when clinical tests cannot be completed or confounders excluded (e.g. high cervical cord injury, ears/eyes not examinable).

**EDIC trap:** mandatory ancillary testing varies between European countries – the UK and US use them only selectively.

**Ref:** Academy of Medical Royal Colleges, Code of Practice for the Diagnosis and Confirmation of Death 2025.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W16-D5 · Intracranial pressure monitoring

**1.** A 35-year-old man with severe TBI has ICP monitoring. Regarding ICP monitoring devices and derived indices:

- A. Intraparenchymal microtransducers cannot be recalibrated in situ and may drift over time. — T / F
- B. A ventricular catheter allows both ICP measurement and therapeutic CSF drainage. — T / F
- C. A positive pressure-reactivity index (PRx) indicates intact cerebral autoregulation. — T / F
- D. Lundberg B waves are rhythmic oscillations at 0.5–2 per minute linked to vasomotor activity. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – T.**

- A (True) – parenchymal microtransducers are zeroed only before insertion and may drift by a few mmHg over days.
- B (True) – an EVD is the reference standard and also drains CSF therapeutically (though it cannot measure accurately while open to drain).
- C (False) – PRx is a moving correlation between slow waves of MAP and ICP; **positive** values (> 0.2–0.3) indicate pressure-passive vessels and **impaired** autoregulation, associated with worse outcome. Negative/near-zero values indicate intact reactivity.
- D (True) – B waves are 0.5–2/min vasogenic oscillations seen with reduced compensatory reserve.

**EDIC trap:** the CPP with the lowest PRx is "CPPopt" – an individualised target that remains investigational.

**Ref:** Le Roux P, Neurocrit Care 2014; Czosnyka M, Pickard JD, J Neurol Neurosurg Psychiatry 2004.

</details>

**2.** A 19-year-old woman with diffuse brain swelling has a continuous ICP trace displayed. Regarding the ICP waveform:

- A. The percussion wave (P1) reflects arterial pulsation transmitted to the CSF. — T / F
- B. The dicrotic wave (P3) follows the dicrotic notch. — T / F
- C. An increasing ICP pulse amplitude as mean ICP rises (RAP approaching +1) indicates reduced compensatory reserve. — T / F
- D. Lundberg A (plateau) waves are sustained rises to 50–100 mmHg lasting several minutes and are always pathological. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – T.**

- A (True) – P1 (percussion wave) reflects arterial pulsation transmitted through the choroid plexus.
- B (True) – P3, the dicrotic wave, follows the dicrotic notch (aortic valve closure).
- C (True) – RAP (correlation between pulse amplitude and mean ICP) near 0 means good reserve; near +1 means exhausted reserve; falling values at very high ICP signal terminal vasomotor collapse.
- D (True) – plateau waves are rises to 50–100 mmHg lasting 5–20 min, occurring with intact autoregulation but poor compliance; they are always pathological and indicate ischaemic risk.

**EDIC trap:** P2 > P1 (tidal wave dominant) means poor intracranial compliance.

**Ref:** Czosnyka M, Pickard JD, J Neurol Neurosurg Psychiatry 2004; Hawryluk GWJ, Intensive Care Med 2019.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W17-D1 · Spinal cord injury

**1.** A 40-year-old man falls from a ladder and sustains a C6 fracture-dislocation. On day 3 the ISNCSCI examination shows a neurological level of C6, preserved light touch and pin-prick at S4–5, voluntary anal contraction, and power of grade 3–4/5 in most key muscles below the level of injury. Which ASIA Impairment Scale (AIS) grade is correct?

- A. AIS A
- B. AIS B
- C. AIS C
- D. AIS D
- E. AIS E

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.**

AIS A = complete (no sensory or motor function in S4–5); B = sensory but no motor function preserved below the level (including sacral segments); C = motor function preserved below the level with more than half of key muscles below grade 3; D = motor incomplete with at least half of key muscles below the level grade ≥ 3; E = normal. Voluntary anal contraction makes him motor incomplete, and most key muscles ≥ 3 makes him AIS D. Ref: Kirshblum SC et al. *J Spinal Cord Med* 2011; Rupp R et al. *Top Spinal Cord Inj Rehabil* 2021.

</details>

**2.** A 22-year-old man has a complete C5 injury after a diving accident. In the emergency department his HR is 44/min, BP 78/40 mmHg, peripheries are warm and he is breathing with paradoxical abdominal movement. A CT shows a C5 burst fracture with no other obvious injuries, and his vital capacity is 1.4 L. All of the following are appropriate in his early management EXCEPT:

- A. Exclude occult haemorrhage with focused ultrasound and CT before attributing hypotension to the cord injury
- B. Give atropine for symptomatic bradycardia, particularly during tracheal suctioning
- C. Use suxamethonium for re-intubation on day 10 because of its rapid onset
- D. Use a vasopressor after adequate volume to maintain MAP at about 75–80 mmHg or higher for 3–7 days
- E. Monitor vital capacity closely, anticipating ventilatory failure

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.**

From about 72 h after denervation, extrajunctional acetylcholine receptors proliferate and suxamethonium can cause life-threatening hyperkalaemia; it should be avoided for ~6–9 months. Neurogenic shock (bradycardia, vasodilatation) must be distinguished from haemorrhage. Vagal reflexes are unopposed, so suction may cause asystole – atropine should be available. The 2024 AO Spine/Praxis guideline suggests MAP ≥ 75–80 (upper 90–95) mmHg for 3–7 days. VC monitoring is key since C3–5 lesions compromise the diaphragm. Ref: Kwon BK et al. *Global Spine J* 2024; Martyn JAJ, Richtsfeld M. *Anesthesiology* 2006.

</details>

**3.** A 36-year-old woman has a complete C4 spinal cord injury after a road traffic collision. Regarding her ongoing management:

- A. Lesions at or above C3–C5 commonly require early ventilatory support because of diaphragmatic weakness. — T / F
- B. An abdominal binder can improve vital capacity in a tetraplegic patient sitting upright. — T / F
- C. Autonomic dysreflexia typically complicates lesions below T10. — T / F
- D. Early surgical decompression (< 24 h) is associated with better neurological recovery. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – T.**

The phrenic nerve (C3–5) is affected, and fatigue often causes deterioration over the first days. In tetraplegia, VC is better supine; when sitting, abdominal contents drop and the diaphragm flattens – a binder restores its position. Autonomic dysreflexia (paroxysmal hypertension with reflex bradycardia, often from bladder or bowel distension) occurs with lesions at or above T6. STASCIS and later meta-analyses support decompression within 24 h. Ref: Fehlings MG et al. *PLoS One* 2012 (STASCIS); Fehlings MG et al. *Global Spine J* 2017.

</details>

**4.** A 55-year-old man is ventilated on day 3 after severe TBI and is expected to remain obtunded for several more days. On admission he had a multidetector CT of the cervical spine, reported by a consultant radiologist as normal. His rigid collar is making it hard to keep ICP under control and he has an early occipital pressure sore. What is the most appropriate management of his cervical spine?

- A. Keep the collar until he can be assessed clinically
- B. Remove the collar on the basis of the normal CT
- C. Obtain MRI of the cervical spine before removing the collar
- D. Perform dynamic flexion-extension fluoroscopy
- E. Replace the collar with sandbags and tape

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

In obtunded adult blunt trauma patients, a negative high-quality CT reported by an experienced radiologist has a very high negative predictive value for unstable injury; EAST recommends collar removal on CT alone. Prolonged collars raise ICP (jugular venous compression), cause pressure ulcers, and complicate airway care and line access. MRI rarely detects injuries that change management, and dynamic fluoroscopy is unsafe and not recommended. Ref: Patel MB et al. *J Trauma Acute Care Surg* 2015 (EAST).

</details>


## Case-style practice · W17-D2 · ICU-acquired weakness

**1.** A 64-year-old woman cannot lift her arms off the bed after 3 weeks of ventilation for septic shock. Regarding ICU-acquired weakness (ICUAW):

- A. Hyperglycaemia is a modifiable risk factor. — T / F
- B. Early supplemental parenteral nutrition reduces the incidence of ICUAW. — T / F
- C. A Medical Research Council (MRC) sum score < 48/60 defines ICUAW. — T / F
- D. Handgrip dynamometry can be used as a quick bedside screening test in cooperative patients. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – T, D – T.**

Hyperglycaemia, sepsis/multiorgan failure, immobility and prolonged ventilation are risk factors; intensive insulin reduced electrophysiological CIP/CIM in the Leuven trials (at a hypoglycaemia cost). In EPaNIC, late PN (withholding PN for a week) reduced weakness and accelerated recovery compared with early PN. MRC-SS < 48 in a cooperative patient defines ICUAW; handgrip dynamometry (< 11 kg men, < 7 kg women) is a quick screening tool. Ref: Hermans G et al. *Lancet Respir Med* 2013; Vanhorebeek I et al. *Intensive Care Med* 2020.

</details>

**2.** A 60-year-old man received high-dose hydrocortisone and 48 h of cisatracurium for septic shock with severe ARDS. On day 18 he fails repeated weaning trials and has flaccid, symmetrical limb weakness. Neurophysiology is requested to distinguish critical illness polyneuropathy (CIP) from critical illness myopathy (CIM). Which finding best supports CIP rather than CIM?

- A. Reduced compound muscle action potential (CMAP) amplitudes
- B. Normal motor conduction velocities
- C. Reduced sensory nerve action potential (SNAP) amplitudes
- D. Fibrillation potentials on needle EMG
- E. Decrement > 10 % on repetitive nerve stimulation

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.**

Both CIP and CIM reduce CMAP amplitudes with relatively preserved conduction velocities, and fibrillation potentials can occur in both. The discriminating feature is sensory involvement: SNAP amplitudes are reduced in CIP (axonal sensorimotor neuropathy) but normal in pure CIM. In CIM, CMAPs are prolonged and direct muscle stimulation shows reduced muscle excitability. A decrement on repetitive stimulation suggests a junctional disorder such as myasthenia or persisting neuromuscular block. Ref: Latronico N, Bolton CF. *Lancet Neurol* 2011; Vanhorebeek I et al. *Intensive Care Med* 2020.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W17-D3 · Alcohol-related neurological emergencies

**1.** A 52-year-old man with long-standing alcohol dependence and weight loss is admitted with pneumonia. On day 2 he is confused, has a broad-based unsteady gait, horizontal nystagmus and bilateral lateral rectus weakness. He is not tremulous, HR 88/min, glucose 6.2 mmol/L, Na 136 mmol/L. Which is the most appropriate immediate treatment?

- A. Oral thiamine 100 mg daily
- B. 50 ml of 50 % glucose IV
- C. High-dose intravenous thiamine (e.g. 500 mg three times daily)
- D. Haloperidol 2.5 mg IV
- E. Lorazepam 2 mg IV followed by a reducing regimen

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.**

Confusion, ataxia and ophthalmoplegia (the full triad is present in only ~10–20 %) in a malnourished drinker = Wernicke's encephalopathy, a medical emergency treated with high-dose parenteral thiamine (e.g. 200–500 mg three times daily for 3–5 days). Oral thiamine is poorly absorbed in alcohol users. He is normoglycaemic, so glucose is not needed – but in hypoglycaemic patients glucose must never be delayed for thiamine; give both together. There are no signs of withdrawal requiring benzodiazepines. Ref: Galvin R et al. *Eur J Neurol* 2010 (EFNS guideline); Sechi G, Serra A. *Lancet Neurol* 2007.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W18-D1 · Acute kidney injury

**1.** A 67-year-old man is admitted to ICU after emergency aortic surgery and is at high risk of AKI. Regarding measures to prevent or limit AKI:

- A. In critically ill adults, balanced crystalloids reduced major adverse kidney events at 30 days compared with saline in a large pragmatic trial. — T / F
- B. Fenoldopam is recommended to prevent AKI after major surgery. — T / F
- C. Furosemide to convert oliguric to non-oliguric AKI improves renal recovery. — T / F
- D. Avoiding hyperglycaemia is part of KDIGO-based AKI prevention bundles. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – T.**

SMART (15 802 patients) showed fewer MAKE30 events with balanced crystalloids (absolute difference ~1 %). KDIGO recommends against fenoldopam, low-dose dopamine and diuretics to prevent or treat AKI (except for volume overload). The KDIGO "bundle" – avoid nephrotoxins, optimise volume and perfusion, monitor creatinine and urine output, avoid hyperglycaemia and radiocontrast where possible – reduced AKI after cardiac surgery in PrevAKI. Ref: Semler MW et al. *N Engl J Med* 2018; KDIGO. *Kidney Int Suppl* 2012; Meersch M et al. *Intensive Care Med* 2017.

</details>

**2.** A 58-year-old woman's creatinine rises from 80 to 150 µmol/L over 36 h after septic shock. Regarding AKI staging and biomarkers:

- A. KDIGO stage 3 is defined by a creatinine 2.0–2.9 times baseline. — T / F
- B. Urinary TIMP-2·IGFBP7 is a marker of glomerular filtration rate. — T / F
- C. KDIGO stage 1 requires a creatinine rise of at least 44 µmol/L within 48 h. — T / F
- D. Cystatin C is more influenced by muscle mass than creatinine. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – F, B – F, C – F, D – F.**

Stage 3 = creatinine ≥ 3.0 × baseline, ≥ 354 µmol/L, initiation of RRT or UO < 0.3 ml/kg/h for ≥ 24 h or anuria ≥ 12 h (2.0–2.9 × is stage 2). TIMP-2·IGFBP7 is a tubular cell-cycle-arrest (stress/damage) marker, not a filtration marker. Stage 1 = rise ≥ 26.5 µmol/L (0.3 mg/dl) within 48 h or 1.5–1.9 × baseline within 7 days, or UO < 0.5 ml/kg/h for 6–12 h. Cystatin C is less dependent on muscle mass than creatinine (useful in sarcopenia). This patient's 1.9-fold rise is stage 1. Ref: KDIGO. *Kidney Int Suppl* 2012; Kashani K et al. *Crit Care* 2013; Ostermann M et al. *JAMA Netw Open* 2020.

</details>

**3.** A 79-year-old woman has had profuse diarrhoea for 4 days while continuing her ramipril. Creatinine is 245 µmol/L (baseline 85), urea 28 mmol/L. Urine output is 0.3 ml/kg/h. Urine sodium is 8 mmol/L, fractional excretion of sodium 0.4 %, urine osmolality 640 mOsm/kg; microscopy shows only a few hyaline casts. She has not received diuretics. Which is the most likely cause of her AKI?

- A. Acute tubular necrosis
- B. Acute interstitial nephritis
- C. Rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis
- D. Post-renal obstruction
- E. Prerenal (haemodynamic) AKI

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**E.**

Avid sodium and water retention (UNa < 20 mmol/L, FeNa < 1 %, urine osmolality > 500 mOsm/kg) with a bland sediment and high urea:creatinine ratio indicates preserved tubular function – prerenal AKI from volume depletion compounded by ACE inhibition. ATN typically shows FeNa > 2 %, isosthenuric urine and muddy brown granular/epithelial casts; AIN white cell casts; glomerulonephritis red cell casts and proteinuria. (After diuretics, FeUrea < 35 % is more reliable.) These indices have limited accuracy in sepsis and CKD. Ref: Kellum JA et al. *Nat Rev Dis Primers* 2021; KDIGO. *Kidney Int Suppl* 2012.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W18-D3 · Renal replacement therapy

**1.** A 61-year-old man on CVVHDF has had three filters clot in 24 h using no anticoagulation. Regarding CRRT circuit anticoagulation:

- A. Regional citrate anticoagulation prolongs filter lifespan compared with systemic heparin. — T / F
- B. A total-to-ionised calcium ratio > 2.5 suggests citrate accumulation. — T / F
- C. Citrate is absolutely contraindicated in any degree of liver dysfunction. — T / F
- D. Citrate anticoagulates the circuit mainly by chelating ionised calcium. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – T.**

In RICH (2020), regional citrate extended filter life (~47 vs 26 h) with fewer bleeding complications than systemic heparin; KDIGO recommends citrate first-line if no contraindication. Citrate chelates ionised calcium (a cofactor of the clotting cascade), keeping circuit iCa ~0.25–0.35 mmol/L; calcium is reinfused systemically. Accumulation (impaired hepatic metabolism, poor perfusion) causes a rising total/ionised Ca ratio (> 2.5), falling systemic iCa and high-anion-gap acidosis. Liver dysfunction is a relative caution, not an absolute contraindication – citrate is used with close monitoring. Ref: Zarbock A et al. *JAMA* 2020 (RICH); KDIGO. *Kidney Int Suppl* 2012.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W18-D4 · Rhabdomyolysis and contrast-associated AKI

**1.** A 33-year-old man develops rhabdomyolysis (CK 85 000 U/L) after lying on the floor for 10 hours following a drug binge. Regarding treatment:

- A. Randomised trials show that sodium bicarbonate reduces the need for RRT in rhabdomyolysis. — T / F
- B. The McMahon score (age, sex, creatinine, calcium, phosphate, bicarbonate, CK, aetiology) predicts the risk of RRT or death. — T / F
- C. Hyperkalaemia is a common early life-threatening complication. — T / F
- D. Mannitol is recommended routinely once urine output is established. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – F, B – T, C – T, D – F.**

Early, generous isotonic crystalloid to a high urine output is the mainstay; no RCT supports bicarbonate or mannitol, and they cause hypocalcaemia/alkalosis or osmotic problems. The McMahon score (≥ 6 identifies high risk) outperforms CK alone in predicting RRT or death. Potassium released from damaged muscle can cause early arrhythmias and must be monitored closely. Ref: Bosch X et al. *N Engl J Med* 2009; McMahon GM et al. *JAMA Intern Med* 2013.

</details>

**2.** A 35-year-old man has been trapped for 6 hours under a collapsed concrete wall after an earthquake, with both legs crushed. Rescuers expect to free him in about 30 minutes. He is conscious, HR 118/min, BP 105/60 mmHg, and paramedics have obtained IV access in his arm. There is no urine output to assess. Which measure is the most important to start before extrication?

- A. Apply tourniquets to both thighs and release them after extrication
- B. Intravenous isotonic crystalloid at about 1 L/h
- C. Intravenous furosemide 40 mg
- D. Intravenous mannitol 1 g/kg
- E. Oral potassium-free rehydration solution only

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

In crush injury, release of compression causes sudden hypovolaemia, hyperkalaemia and myoglobin release. IV isotonic fluid (e.g. saline ~1 L/h, 10–15 ml/kg/h) should start before extrication and continue afterwards (titrated to urine output and volume tolerance), which reduces crush-related AKI and cardiac arrest. Tourniquets are only for uncontrolled haemorrhage; diuretics and mannitol are not first-line; oral fluid alone is insufficient. Ref: Sever MS, Vanholder R. *Nephrol Dial Transplant* 2012 (Renal Disaster Relief Task Force recommendations); Bosch X et al. *N Engl J Med* 2009.

</details>

**3.** A 42-year-old woman on simvastatin develops myalgia and dark urine a week after starting gemfibrozil. Regarding the pathophysiology and causes of rhabdomyolysis:

- A. Myoglobin causes renal injury through tubular obstruction, direct haem-iron toxicity and renal vasoconstriction. — T / F
- B. Statin–fibrate (especially gemfibrozil) combinations increase the risk of rhabdomyolysis. — T / F
- C. Malignant hyperthermia and neuroleptic malignant syndrome can cause rhabdomyolysis. — T / F
- D. Myoglobin (~17 kDa) is freely filtered at the glomerulus. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – T.**

Myoglobin is small and freely filtered; in acidic, concentrated urine it precipitates with Tamm–Horsfall protein to form casts, while haem iron drives lipid peroxidation and scavenges NO, causing vasoconstriction. Gemfibrozil inhibits statin glucuronidation and raises statin levels. Hypermetabolic states (MH, NMS, serotonin syndrome, exertional heat stroke), trauma, ischaemia, drugs, toxins and infections are common causes. Ref: Bosch X et al. *N Engl J Med* 2009; Petejova N, Martinek A. *Crit Care* 2014.

</details>

**4.** A 70-year-old man with diabetes and stage 3b CKD (eGFR 35 ml/min/1.73 m²) needs a contrast-enhanced CT angiogram for suspected mesenteric ischaemia. He takes metformin and ramipril, is haemodynamically stable and clinically euvolaemic, and the radiologist plans to use iso-osmolar contrast. Which is the most appropriate measure to reduce his risk of contrast-associated AKI?

- A. Oral N-acetylcysteine 1200 mg twice daily
- B. Intravenous sodium bicarbonate instead of saline
- C. Prophylactic haemofiltration around the scan
- D. Peri-procedural IV isotonic crystalloid and minimum necessary dose of iso- or low-osmolar contrast
- E. Defer the CT until renal function improves

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.**

PRESERVE (2018) showed no benefit of IV bicarbonate over saline or of NAC over placebo. Current ACR/NKF consensus: the risk of contrast-associated AKI with modern agents at eGFR ≥ 30 is low; prophylactic isotonic fluid is reasonable for eGFR < 30 or high-risk patients, and a medically indicated urgent scan should never be withheld. Prophylactic haemofiltration is not recommended. Ref: Weisbord SD et al. *N Engl J Med* 2018 (PRESERVE); Davenport MS et al. *Radiology* 2020.

</details>

**5.** A 28-year-old soldier collapses during a hot-weather march. Twelve hours later his CK is 120 000 U/L and creatinine 260 µmol/L (normal at enlistment). His core temperature was 41.2 °C at collapse and urine is dark brown, dipstick strongly positive for blood. All of the following laboratory findings are typical of his established rhabdomyolysis-associated AKI EXCEPT:

- A. Hyperphosphataemia
- B. Hyperuricaemia
- C. High-anion-gap metabolic acidosis
- D. Hypokalaemia
- E. Creatinine rising disproportionately to urea

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.**

Muscle necrosis releases potassium, phosphate, purines (→ uric acid), organic acids and creatine (→ creatinine), so hyperkalaemia, hyperphosphataemia, hyperuricaemia, high-anion-gap acidosis and a low urea:creatinine ratio are typical. Calcium falls early (deposition in damaged muscle), with rebound hypercalcaemia in the recovery phase. Ref: Bosch X et al. *N Engl J Med* 2009.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W18-D5 · Timing of renal replacement therapy

**1.** A 30-year-old man had a fasciotomy for compartment syndrome 24 h ago and now has rhabdomyolysis (CK 80 000 U/L). After 6 L of crystalloid, urine output is 0.4 ml/kg/h, creatinine 280 µmol/L, K 5.6 mmol/L without ECG changes, bicarbonate 19 mmol/L, pH 7.31. He has no pulmonary oedema and is on 28 % oxygen. What is the most appropriate management?

- A. Start CRRT now with a high cut-off filter to clear myoglobin
- B. Start intermittent haemodialysis now to prevent hyperkalaemia
- C. Continue fluid titrated to volume status, treat potassium medically and monitor closely for urgent RRT indications
- D. Give mannitol 0.5 g/kg and furosemide to force a diuresis
- E. Give sodium bicarbonate to a urine pH > 7.5

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.**

He has no urgent indication for RRT (refractory hyperkalaemia, severe acidosis, diuretic-resistant fluid overload, uraemic complications). AKIKI and STARRT-AKI showed that pre-emptive/accelerated RRT without such indications does not improve survival and increases RRT exposure, and a large proportion of patients in the delayed arms never needed RRT. Removing myoglobin with extracorporeal therapy has no proven outcome benefit; mannitol, diuretics and bicarbonate are unproven. Ref: Gaudry S et al. *N Engl J Med* 2016 (AKIKI); STARRT-AKI Investigators. *N Engl J Med* 2020.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W19-D1 · Diabetic emergencies

**1.** A 24-year-old woman with type 1 diabetes presents with vomiting, blood ketones 5.8 mmol/L, pH 7.12, bicarbonate 8 mmol/L, glucose 26 mmol/L and potassium 3.1 mmol/L. Regarding her treatment:

- A. Insulin should be withheld until potassium is ≥ 3.3–3.5 mmol/L while potassium is replaced. — T / F
- B. Potassium should be added to IV fluids once K is 3.5–5.5 mmol/L and urine output is adequate. — T / F
- C. Sodium bicarbonate is routinely indicated at a pH of 7.12. — T / F
- D. Her usual long-acting basal insulin should be continued alongside the IV insulin infusion. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – T.**

Insulin drives potassium into cells; starting it with K < 3.3–3.5 mmol/L risks arrhythmia, so replace potassium first (2024 consensus). Add 40 mmol/L KCl to fluids when K is 3.5–5.5. Bicarbonate is not recommended unless pH < 7.0 (consensus) – it does not speed resolution and risks hypokalaemia and paradoxical CSF acidosis. Continuing basal insulin reduces rebound hyperglycaemia and ketosis when the IV infusion stops. Ref: Umpierrez GE et al. *Diabetes Care* 2024 (ADA/EASD/JBDS/AACE/DTS consensus).

</details>

**2.** A 31-year-old man is being treated for DKA with 0.9 % saline and a fixed-rate insulin infusion. Twelve hours later his chloride is 118 mmol/L. Regarding complications of DKA treatment:

- A. Hypokalaemia is a common complication of insulin therapy. — T / F
- B. Saline resuscitation typically produces a high-anion-gap acidosis. — T / F
- C. A rapid fall in plasma osmolality protects against cerebral oedema. — T / F
- D. Phosphate should be routinely replaced in all patients, even if asymptomatic. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – F.**

Insulin and correction of acidosis shift potassium intracellularly – hypokalaemia is the commonest dangerous complication. Large-volume saline causes hyperchloraemic, normal-anion-gap acidosis, which can be mistaken for persisting ketoacidosis (check ketones); balanced crystalloids resolve DKA slightly faster. Rapid osmolality falls are a suspected contributor to cerebral oedema (mainly in children). Routine phosphate replacement has no proven benefit; replace only if severe (< 0.32 mmol/L) or with cardiac/respiratory compromise. Ref: Umpierrez GE et al. *Diabetes Care* 2024; Self WH et al. *JAMA Netw Open* 2020.

</details>

**3.** A 78-year-old man with type 2 diabetes presents after a week of confusion with glucose 52 mmol/L, Na 149 mmol/L, urea 30 mmol/L, blood ketones 0.8 mmol/L and pH 7.36. Regarding the hyperglycaemic hyperosmolar state (HHS):

- A. Diagnostic criteria include glucose ≥ 33.3 mmol/L (600 mg/dl). — T / F
- B. Effective (calculated) serum osmolality is typically > 320 mOsm/kg. — T / F
- C. A modest rise in serum sodium during initial saline resuscitation is acceptable provided osmolality is falling. — T / F
- D. A fixed-rate insulin infusion of 0.1 U/kg/h should be started at the same time as IV fluids in all patients. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – F.**

HHS criteria (2024 consensus): glucose ≥ 33.3 mmol/L, effective osmolality > 300–320 mOsm/kg, no significant ketonaemia (< 3 mmol/L) and pH ≥ 7.3. Fluid alone lowers glucose; as glucose falls, sodium rises – acceptable if osmolality falls slowly (target 3–8 mOsm/kg/h). JBDS advises starting insulin only once glucose stops falling with fluids (at 0.05 U/kg/h), unless there is significant ketonaemia, because early insulin causes rapid osmotic shifts and hypovolaemia. Mixed DKA–HHS occurs in up to a third. Ref: Mustafa OG et al. *Diabet Med* 2023 (JBDS HHS); Umpierrez GE et al. *Diabetes Care* 2024.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W19-D2 · Glycaemic control in ICU

**1.** An ICU is revising its insulin protocol for ventilated adults. Regarding trials of glycaemic control in critical illness:

- A. NICE-SUGAR showed that a target of 4.5–6.0 mmol/L increased 90-day mortality compared with ≤ 10 mmol/L. — T / F
- B. The first Leuven trial (2001), which showed lower mortality with intensive insulin, was performed in medical ICU patients. — T / F
- C. TGC-fast (2023), in which early parenteral nutrition was withheld, showed lower mortality with tight glucose control. — T / F
- D. In NICE-SUGAR, moderate hypoglycaemia (2.3–3.9 mmol/L) was not associated with an increased risk of death. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – F.**

NICE-SUGAR (6104 patients) found higher 90-day mortality with intensive control (27.5 % vs 24.9 %) and far more severe hypoglycaemia. Leuven I was a surgical (mostly cardiac) ICU trial with early PN; Leuven II (medical ICU) showed no overall mortality benefit. TGC-fast (9230 patients, no early PN) found no difference in ICU dependency or mortality. In a post hoc NICE-SUGAR analysis, both moderate and severe hypoglycaemia were associated with dose-dependent increases in mortality. Current targets are ~7.8–10 mmol/L. Ref: NICE-SUGAR Investigators. *N Engl J Med* 2009 and 2012; Gunst J et al. *N Engl J Med* 2023.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W19-D3 · Thyroid emergencies

**1.** A 42-year-old woman with Graves' disease stopped carbimazole 2 months ago. She presents with temperature 40.1 °C, AF at 165/min, heart failure, agitation, vomiting and diarrhoea; Burch–Wartofsky score is 70. Free T4 is markedly raised and TSH suppressed, and no infection or other precipitant is identified. All of the following are appropriate in her management EXCEPT:

- A. Aspirin as an antipyretic
- B. Propranolol (or esmolol if heart failure limits beta-blockade), titrated to heart rate
- C. A thionamide (propylthiouracil or methimazole)
- D. Hydrocortisone
- E. Iodine (e.g. Lugol's solution) given at least 1 hour after the thionamide

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.**

Thyroid storm is treated by blocking synthesis (thionamide; PTU also inhibits peripheral T4→T3 conversion), blocking release (iodine, given ≥ 1 h after the thionamide so it is not used as substrate), beta-blockade (cautious, short-acting in heart failure), glucocorticoids, cooling and treating the precipitant; cholestyramine and plasma exchange are adjuncts. Salicylates displace T4/T3 from binding proteins and raise free hormone levels – use paracetamol and active cooling instead. Ref: Ross DS et al. *Thyroid* 2016 (ATA guidelines); Akamizu T et al. *Thyroid* 2012.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W19-D5 · Polyuria and diabetes insipidus

**1.** A 58-year-old woman who has taken lithium for 20 years for bipolar disorder is nil by mouth after bowel surgery. On day 2 she passes 5 L of urine a day, Na is 158 mmol/L, plasma osmolality 322 mOsm/kg and urine osmolality 150 mOsm/kg. After 2 µg of IV desmopressin, urine osmolality rises only to 175 mOsm/kg. Glucose, calcium and potassium are normal. Baseline copeptin is markedly raised. Which is the most likely diagnosis?

- A. Nephrogenic diabetes insipidus (arginine vasopressin resistance)
- B. Central diabetes insipidus (arginine vasopressin deficiency)
- C. Primary polydipsia
- D. Osmotic diuresis
- E. Cerebral salt wasting

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.**

Hypernatraemia with inappropriately dilute urine that fails to concentrate (< 50 % rise) after desmopressin, together with a high copeptin (> 21 pmol/L without prior stimulation), indicates renal resistance to vasopressin – classically lithium-induced. Central DI responds to desmopressin and has a low copeptin; primary polydipsia does not cause hypernatraemia; osmotic diuresis would have urine osmolality ≈ 300 or higher; cerebral salt wasting causes hyponatraemia. The 2022 nomenclature renames these AVP resistance and AVP deficiency. Ref: Fenske W et al. *N Engl J Med* 2018; Arima H et al. *Endocr J* 2022.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W20-D1 · Upper gastrointestinal bleeding

**1.** A 76-year-old man taking apixaban and aspirin presents with haematemesis, HR 115/min and BP 90/55 mmHg. Platelets are 210 × 10⁹/L, fibrinogen 1.2 g/L after 6 units of red cells. Regarding haemostatic support:

- A. Four-factor PCC (or andexanet alfa where available) may be used for life-threatening bleeding on a factor Xa inhibitor. — T / F
- B. Platelet transfusion is recommended because he takes aspirin, despite a normal count. — T / F
- C. Intravenous vitamin K alone would reverse his anticoagulant effect within 1 hour. — T / F
- D. Fibrinogen replacement is indicated with a level of 1.2 g/L during major haemorrhage. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – T.**

Andexanet or 4F-PCC (25–50 IU/kg) is suggested for life-threatening FXa-inhibitor bleeding. Routine platelet transfusion for antiplatelet-associated GI bleeding with a normal count is not recommended (no benefit, possible harm). Vitamin K has no effect on DOACs and, for warfarin, takes 6–24 h – PCC is needed for rapid reversal. In major bleeding, fibrinogen < 1.5 g/L should be replaced with fibrinogen concentrate or cryoprecipitate. Ref: Gralnek IM et al. *Endoscopy* 2021 (ESGE); Rossaint R et al. *Crit Care* 2023.

</details>

**2.** A 52-year-old man with Child–Pugh C cirrhosis has massive variceal bleeding that persists after terlipressin and two attempts at band ligation. Regarding rescue therapy:

- A. A self-expanding oesophageal metal stent is an alternative to balloon tamponade as a bridge to definitive therapy. — T / F
- B. Pre-emptive TIPS is indicated in Child–Pugh A patients with variceal bleeding. — T / F
- C. Balloon tamponade can safely remain inflated for up to 72 hours. — T / F
- D. Aspiration pneumonia and oesophageal rupture are recognised complications of balloon tamponade. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – T.**

Baveno VII: in refractory oesophageal variceal bleeding, balloon tamponade or (preferably, if available) a self-expanding metal stent can bridge to rescue TIPS; stents caused fewer adverse events in an RCT. Pre-emptive TIPS within 72 h is for high-risk patients (Child C 10–13, or Child B > 7 with active bleeding at endoscopy). Tamponade should be limited to ~24 h because of pressure necrosis. Ref: de Franchis R et al. *J Hepatol* 2022 (Baveno VII); Escorsell À et al. *Hepatology* 2016.

</details>

**3.** A 67-year-old woman presents with melaena and a Glasgow-Blatchford score of 12. Regarding evidence-based management of upper GI bleeding:

- A. Tranexamic acid did not reduce death from bleeding and increased venous thromboembolic events in HALT-IT. — T / F
- B. A Glasgow-Blatchford score of 0–1 identifies patients who may be managed as outpatients. — T / F
- C. Urgent endoscopy within 6 h reduced mortality compared with endoscopy at 6–24 h in high-risk patients. — T / F
- D. Pre-endoscopy IV erythromycin should be avoided because it increases rebleeding. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – F.**

HALT-IT (12 009 patients) found no benefit of high-dose TXA and more VTE. ESGE suggests GBS ≤ 1 as a threshold for outpatient management. Lau et al. (2020) found no mortality benefit of endoscopy within 6 h versus 6–24 h after resuscitation in high-risk patients. Erythromycin 250 mg IV 30–120 min before endoscopy improves mucosal visualisation and reduces the need for second-look endoscopy. Ref: HALT-IT Trial Collaborators. *Lancet* 2020; Lau JYW et al. *N Engl J Med* 2020; Gralnek IM et al. *Endoscopy* 2021.

</details>

**4.** A Minnesota/Sengstaken-type tube is being inserted in a ventilated patient with uncontrolled variceal bleeding. Regarding its safe use:

- A. The position of the gastric balloon in the stomach should be confirmed (e.g. by radiograph) before it is fully inflated. — T / F
- B. The oesophageal balloon should be inflated to about 80–100 mmHg. — T / F
- C. The oesophageal balloon is inflated first, before the gastric balloon. — T / F
- D. Endotracheal intubation before insertion is advisable in patients with massive haematemesis or impaired consciousness. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – T.**

Inflation of the gastric balloon in the oesophagus causes rupture, so position must be confirmed (partial inflation and X-ray) before full inflation and gentle traction. The gastric balloon alone often controls bleeding; the oesophageal balloon is inflated only if bleeding persists, to ~30–40 mmHg, and deflated periodically. Airway protection is recommended because of aspiration risk. Ref: de Franchis R et al. *J Hepatol* 2022 (Baveno VII); Tripathi D et al. *Gut* 2015 (BSG guidelines).

</details>


## Case-style practice · W20-D2 · Acute pancreatitis

**1.** A 45-year-old man presents with severe epigastric pain radiating to the back. Regarding the diagnosis and classification of acute pancreatitis (revised Atlanta 2012):

- A. Diagnosis requires two of three: typical pain, lipase or amylase > 3 × ULN, characteristic imaging findings. — T / F
- B. Moderately severe pancreatitis includes transient organ failure resolving within 48 h. — T / F
- C. Organ failure is defined as a modified Marshall score ≥ 2 in the respiratory, cardiovascular or renal system. — T / F
- D. The magnitude of the amylase rise correlates with disease severity. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – F.**

Revised Atlanta: mild = no organ failure or local complications; moderately severe = transient organ failure (< 48 h) and/or local or systemic complications; severe = persistent organ failure > 48 h. Organ failure = modified Marshall score ≥ 2 in respiratory, cardiovascular or renal systems. Enzyme levels support the diagnosis but do not predict severity. Ref: Banks PA et al. *Gut* 2013.

</details>

**2.** A 56-year-old woman with gallstone pancreatitis develops persistent respiratory and renal failure. Regarding management of severe acute pancreatitis:

- A. Aggressive early fluid resuscitation increased fluid overload without improving outcomes compared with moderate fluid therapy. — T / F
- B. For infected necrosis, a step-up approach starting with percutaneous or endoscopic drainage is preferred, ideally delaying intervention until walled-off necrosis has formed (~4 weeks). — T / F
- C. Urgent ERCP within 24–72 h improves outcome in predicted severe biliary pancreatitis without cholangitis. — T / F
- D. Nasogastric feeding is a safe alternative to nasojejunal feeding. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – T.**

WATERFALL (2022) was stopped for harm: aggressive Ringer's lactate caused more fluid overload with no benefit. PANTER and TENSION established step-up drainage over primary necrosectomy; POINTER found no advantage of immediate drainage over postponed. APEC (2020) showed no benefit of urgent ERCP in predicted severe biliary pancreatitis without cholangitis. Early enteral nutrition is preferred to PN, and nasogastric feeding is non-inferior to nasojejunal. Ref: de-Madaria E et al. *N Engl J Med* 2022; van Santvoort HC et al. *N Engl J Med* 2010; Schepers NJ et al. *Lancet* 2020.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W20-D3 · Acute liver failure

**1.** A 24-year-old woman is admitted 50 hours after a staggered paracetamol overdose. She has grade 1 encephalopathy, INR 4.8, platelets 140 × 10⁹/L, lactate 2.4 mmol/L, pH 7.36 and creatinine 110 µmol/L. She is not bleeding. A central venous catheter is needed for NAC and fluids. Which is the most appropriate approach to her coagulopathy?

- A. Insert the line under ultrasound guidance without prophylactic correction of the INR
- B. Give fresh frozen plasma 15 ml/kg before line insertion
- C. Give recombinant factor VIIa before line insertion
- D. Give four-factor prothrombin complex concentrate to normalise the INR
- E. Give two pools of platelets before line insertion

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.**

In ALF the INR overestimates bleeding risk: pro- and anticoagulant factors are both reduced and global haemostasis (viscoelastic testing) is usually rebalanced. Prophylactic correction is not recommended; it adds volume, may precipitate thrombosis and removes the INR as a key prognostic marker for transplant listing. Procedures under ultrasound guidance are safe; correct only for active bleeding or high-risk procedures (e.g. ICP bolt). Vitamin K once IV is reasonable to exclude deficiency. Ref: Wendon J et al. *J Hepatol* 2017 (EASL ALF guideline); Stravitz RT et al. *J Hepatol* 2012.

</details>

**2.** A 31-year-old man with hyperacute paracetamol-induced liver failure is intubated for grade 4 encephalopathy on day 3. Arterial ammonia is 210 µmol/L, Na 134 mmol/L, temperature 37.4 °C, MAP 80 mmHg on noradrenaline, and his pupils have become sluggish. He is listed for super-urgent transplantation. Which measure is most appropriate to reduce his risk of intracranial hypertension?

- A. Oral lactulose titrated to two to three stools per day
- B. Hypertonic saline to a serum sodium of 145–150 mmol/L
- C. Prophylactic hyperventilation to PaCO2 3.5 kPa
- D. Dexamethasone 8 mg IV 8-hourly
- E. Prophylactic hypothermia to 32 °C

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

Ammonia > 150–200 µmol/L, grade 3–4 encephalopathy, young age and vasopressor need predict cerebral oedema in hyperacute ALF. Hypertonic saline to Na 145–150 mmol/L reduced the incidence and severity of intracranial hypertension in an RCT. Early CRRT also lowers ammonia. Lactulose is ineffective and causes distension; prolonged hyperventilation is reserved for acute surges; steroids are ineffective; a trial of moderate hypothermia (33–34 °C) showed no benefit. Ref: Murphy N et al. *Hepatology* 2004; Bernal W et al. *J Hepatol* 2016; Wendon J et al. *J Hepatol* 2017.

</details>

**3.** Regarding the definition and classification of acute liver failure (ALF):

- A. In the O'Grady classification, hyperacute ALF is encephalopathy within 7 days of the onset of jaundice. — T / F
- B. Patients with acute presentation of Wilson's disease are excluded from the definition of ALF. — T / F
- C. Subacute ALF has a better prognosis without transplantation than hyperacute ALF. — T / F
- D. Encephalopathy is not required for the diagnosis of ALF. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – F.**

ALF = coagulopathy (INR ≥ 1.5) and any degree of encephalopathy in a patient without pre-existing liver disease, within 26 weeks; acute severe liver injury without encephalopathy is a separate entity. Wilson's disease, vertically acquired HBV and autoimmune hepatitis are included despite possible pre-existing disease. Hyperacute (< 7 days, typically paracetamol) carries the highest risk of cerebral oedema but the best spontaneous survival; subacute (5–26 weeks) has less cerebral oedema but worse transplant-free survival. Ref: O'Grady JG et al. *Lancet* 1993; Wendon J et al. *J Hepatol* 2017.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W20-D4 · Cirrhosis, hepatorenal syndrome and ACLF
