**1.** A 61-year-old man with alcohol-related cirrhosis and tense ascites has a creatinine rising from 90 to 210 µmol/L over 4 days. Regarding the diagnosis of hepatorenal syndrome-AKI (HRS-AKI, ICA–ADQI criteria):

- A. Diagnosis requires no improvement in renal function after 48 h of diuretic withdrawal and albumin 1 g/kg. — T / F
- B. Shock and recent nephrotoxic drug exposure must be absent. — T / F
- C. Microhaematuria > 50 red cells per high-power field is compatible with the diagnosis. — T / F
- D. A urine sodium > 40 mmol/L supports the diagnosis. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – F.**

HRS-AKI requires cirrhosis with ascites, KDIGO-defined AKI, no response to 2 days of diuretic withdrawal and albumin volume expansion, absence of shock and nephrotoxins, and no signs of structural kidney injury (proteinuria > 500 mg/day, microhaematuria > 50 RBC/hpf, abnormal ultrasound). Intense renal vasoconstriction produces avid sodium retention (UNa usually < 10–20 mmol/L, FeNa < 0.2 %). The 2024 ICA–ADQI update replaced types 1/2 with HRS-AKI and HRS-NAKI. Ref: Nadim MK et al. *J Hepatol* 2024; Angeli P et al. *J Hepatol* 2015.

</details>

**2.** A 58-year-old woman with alcohol-related cirrhosis and refractory ascites develops AKI (creatinine 95 → 240 µmol/L). After diuretic withdrawal and 2 days of albumin there is no improvement, urine sodium is 6 mmol/L, urine sediment is bland and ultrasound is normal, so she fulfils the criteria for HRS-AKI. Her SpO2 is 97 % on air and there is no evidence of volume overload. All of the following are appropriate EXCEPT:

- A. Terlipressin together with albumin
- B. Noradrenaline with albumin in the ICU if terlipressin cannot be used
- C. Assessment of oxygenation and volume status before and during terlipressin
- D. Furosemide to restore urine output
- E. Referral for liver transplant assessment

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.**

HRS-AKI is treated with a vasoconstrictor plus albumin (20–40 g/day): terlipressin (bolus or, increasingly, continuous infusion with fewer adverse effects) or noradrenaline in an ICU setting. CONFIRM (2021) showed more HRS reversal with terlipressin but more respiratory failure, so oxygenation and volume must be monitored (avoid if SpO2 < 90 % or ACLF grade 3 with high creatinine). Transplantation is the definitive treatment. Diuretics are stopped – they worsen renal hypoperfusion. Ref: Wong F et al. *N Engl J Med* 2021 (CONFIRM); EASL. *J Hepatol* 2018.

</details>

**3.** A 54-year-old man with cirrhosis is ventilated after a variceal bleed that has been controlled. Tense ascites is limiting diaphragmatic excursion and causing high airway pressures (plateau 32 cmH2O) and failure of a spontaneous breathing trial. He received terlipressin for the bleed and is on no diuretics. Under ultrasound guidance, large-volume paracentesis drains 7 L of ascitic fluid. Which is the most appropriate plasma expansion?

- A. No plasma expansion is required
- B. About 56 g of albumin (e.g. 280 ml of 20 % albumin)
- C. 20 g of albumin regardless of volume removed
- D. 1 L of 4.5 % albumin given over 24 h
- E. 500 ml of 6 % hydroxyethyl starch

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

EASL recommends 8 g of albumin per litre removed for paracentesis > 5 L to prevent post-paracentesis circulatory dysfunction (rising renin, hyponatraemia, AKI and mortality). 7 L × 8 g = 56 g ≈ 280 ml of 20 % albumin. Synthetic colloids are less effective and HES is associated with AKI; dilute albumin delivers too much volume and sodium. Ref: EASL. *J Hepatol* 2018 (decompensated cirrhosis guideline); Bernardi M et al. *Hepatology* 2012.

</details>

**4.** A 49-year-old woman with alcohol-related cirrhosis is admitted with pneumonia and develops respiratory failure requiring ventilation, AKI (creatinine 260 µmol/L) and noradrenaline-dependent shock. Bilirubin is 210 µmol/L and INR 2.4. On day 2 she remains on vasopressors and ventilated. The family asks about her chances of survival. Which tool is the most appropriate for estimating her short-term mortality?

- A. CLIF-C ACLF score
- B. Child–Pugh score
- C. Maddrey discriminant function
- D. Glasgow alcoholic hepatitis score
- E. MELD score alone

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.**

She has acute-on-chronic liver failure with ≥ 3 organ failures (ACLF grade 3). The CLIF-C ACLF score (CLIF-C OF score + age + WBC) predicts 28- and 90-day mortality more accurately than Child–Pugh or MELD; reassessment at days 3–7 refines prognosis and guides decisions about transplant or futility. Maddrey and Glasgow scores guide corticosteroid use in alcoholic hepatitis. Ref: Jalan R et al. *J Hepatol* 2014; EASL. *J Hepatol* 2023 (ACLF guideline).

</details>


## Case-style practice · W20-D5 · Intra-abdominal hypertension

**1.** A 63-year-old man is in ICU 18 h after an open repair of an incarcerated incisional hernia with small-bowel resection. He has received 7 L of fluid. Intravesical pressure measured correctly is 18 mmHg on two occasions, urine output is 1 ml/kg/h, creatinine is unchanged, MAP 80 mmHg and lactate 1.6 mmol/L. Which is the most appropriate management?

- A. Immediate decompressive laparotomy
- B. Nasogastric and rectal decompression, adequate analgesia-sedation, avoidance of positive fluid balance and serial IAP measurement
- C. Further fluid bolus to increase abdominal perfusion pressure
- D. Prophylactic continuous neuromuscular blockade for 48 h
- E. Start CRRT to remove fluid

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

IAP 18 mmHg = grade II intra-abdominal hypertension (12–15 grade I, 16–20 grade II, 21–25 grade III, > 25 grade IV). Without new organ dysfunction this is not abdominal compartment syndrome (sustained IAP > 20 mmHg with new organ failure), so medical measures are first: evacuate intraluminal contents, improve wall compliance (analgesia, sedation, avoid head-up > 30°, brief NMB only if needed), drain ascites if present, avoid fluid overload. Decompressive laparotomy is reserved for overt ACS. Ref: Kirkpatrick AW et al. *Intensive Care Med* 2013 (WSACS).

</details>


## Case-style practice · W21-D1 · Nutrition in critical illness

**1.** A 59-year-old woman with prolonged ileus after a Whipple procedure needs parenteral nutrition (PN). She was severely malnourished before surgery. Regarding PN:

- A. Peripheral PN is limited to solutions with an osmolarity of about 850–900 mOsm/L or less. — T / F
- B. In refeeding hypophosphataemia, temporary caloric restriction improved survival compared with standard feeding. — T / F
- C. ESPEN recommends full-target PN from day 1 in well-nourished ICU patients who cannot be fed enterally. — T / F
- D. Lipid emulsion is contraindicated in patients receiving a propofol infusion. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – F.**

Peripheral veins tolerate only low-osmolarity solutions (< ~850–900 mOsm/L), otherwise thrombophlebitis occurs. In Doig's RCT, caloric restriction for 48 h when refeeding hypophosphataemia developed improved survival. ESPEN advises against early full feeding; when EN is contraindicated, PN should be introduced progressively (hypocaloric < 70 % of energy expenditure early), and in EPaNIC late PN (after day 8) was superior to early PN. Propofol lipid (1.1 kcal/ml) must be counted, and lipid dose adjusted, but it is not a contraindication. Ref: Singer P et al. *Clin Nutr* 2019 and 2023 (ESPEN); Doig GS et al. *Lancet Respir Med* 2015; Casaer MP et al. *N Engl J Med* 2011.

</details>

**2.** An 80-kg man on day 4 of ventilation for ARDS, with no previous malnutrition, is receiving propofol 1 % at 20 ml/h and enteral feed providing 1400 kcal/day. His estimated energy target is 1800 kcal/day and his triglycerides are rising (3.8 mmol/L). The dietitian asks whether non-nutritional calories are being counted. Approximately how many kilocalories per day does the propofol contribute?

- A. Negligible – propofol contains no calories
- B. About 100 kcal/day
- C. About 250 kcal/day
- D. About 1000 kcal/day
- E. About 530 kcal/day

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**E.**

Propofol 1 % in 10 % lipid emulsion provides ~1.1 kcal/ml: 20 ml/h × 24 h = 480 ml × 1.1 ≈ 530 kcal/day. Together with his feed (1400 kcal) he receives ~1930 kcal – above target. ESPEN recommends counting non-nutritional calories (propofol, glucose infusions, citrate) to avoid overfeeding, and monitoring triglycerides (propofol infusion syndrome and pancreatitis risk). Reducing the feed volume would also lower protein, so a high-protein formula may be needed. Ref: Singer P et al. *Clin Nutr* 2019 (ESPEN); Bousie E et al. *Clin Nutr* 2016.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W21-D3 · Stress ulcer prophylaxis

**1.** A 68-year-old woman is ventilated for faecal peritonitis. She is on noradrenaline 0.3 µg/kg/min, platelets 40 × 10⁹/L and INR 2.1. Nasogastric feeding has been established at the full target rate. She has no history of peptic ulcer and takes no antiplatelet drugs. What is the most appropriate stress ulcer prophylaxis?

- A. None, because she is receiving full enteral nutrition
- B. Sucralfate via nasogastric tube
- C. A proton pump inhibitor (or H2-receptor antagonist)
- D. Misoprostol
- E. An alginate–antacid preparation

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.**

Shock and coagulopathy are major risk factors for clinically important GI bleeding. The 2024 SCCM/ASHP guideline recommends PPI or H2RA prophylaxis for patients with these risk factors, irrespective of enteral feeding; REVISE (2024, 4821 ventilated patients) showed pantoprazole reduced clinically important upper GI bleeding without a mortality difference or significant increase in pneumonia or C. difficile. Sucralfate is less effective; alginates and misoprostol have no role. Stop prophylaxis once risk factors resolve. Ref: MacLaren R et al. *Crit Care Med* 2024; Cook D et al. *N Engl J Med* 2024 (REVISE).

</details>


## Case-style practice · W21-D5 · Clostridioides difficile infection

**1.** A 70-year-old man on a PPI develops profuse diarrhoea 10 days after a course of co-amoxiclav. Regarding C. difficile infection (CDI):

- A. Proton pump inhibitor use is associated with an increased risk of CDI. — T / F
- B. Ribotype 027 strains produce binary toxin in addition to toxins A and B. — T / F
- C. Alcohol-based hand rub reliably kills C. difficile spores. — T / F
- D. A positive nucleic acid amplification test (NAAT) alone may overdiagnose CDI because it detects colonisation. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – T.**

Acid suppression, antibiotics (clindamycin, cephalosporins, fluoroquinolones, co-amoxiclav), age and hospitalisation are risk factors. Hypervirulent ribotype 027 produces binary toxin and more toxin A/B. Spores resist alcohol – soap-and-water hand washing, contact precautions and sporicidal (chlorine) cleaning are needed. Two-step testing (GDH or NAAT followed by toxin EIA) distinguishes infection from colonisation. Ref: McDonald LC et al. *Clin Infect Dis* 2018 (IDSA/SHEA); Crobach MJT et al. *Clin Microbiol Infect* 2016 (ESCMID).

</details>

**2.** A 74-year-old woman with C. difficile infection becomes hypotensive (MAP 58 mmHg on noradrenaline), with abdominal distension, absent bowel sounds and lactate 4.5 mmol/L. CT shows a dilated colon (8 cm) without perforation. She has an ileus and only small amounts of stool. Which is the most appropriate antimicrobial regimen?

- A. Oral metronidazole 400 mg three times daily
- B. Oral fidaxomicin 200 mg twice daily alone
- C. IV vancomycin 1 g twice daily
- D. Oral (± rectal) vancomycin 500 mg four times daily plus IV metronidazole
- E. Faecal microbiota transplantation as first-line therapy

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.**

Fulminant CDI (hypotension, ileus, megacolon) is treated with high-dose enteral vancomycin – via NG tube and, with ileus, as a retention enema – plus IV metronidazole (which reaches the colon via biliary excretion and inflamed mucosa), with early surgical review for colectomy or loop ileostomy with lavage. IV vancomycin does not reach the colonic lumen. Fidaxomicin is first-line for non-fulminant episodes but has limited data in fulminant disease; FMT is used for recurrences or refractory cases. Ref: Johnson S et al. *Clin Infect Dis* 2021 (IDSA/SHEA update); van Prehn J et al. *Clin Microbiol Infect* 2021 (ESCMID).

</details>


## Case-style practice · W22-D1 · Red cell transfusion

**1.** An ICU is updating its red-cell transfusion policy for several patient groups. Regarding randomised evidence on transfusion thresholds:

- A. In septic shock (TRISS), a threshold of 70 g/L resulted in similar 90-day mortality to 90 g/L with fewer transfusions. — T / F
- B. In acute myocardial infarction with anaemia (MINT), a liberal strategy (100 g/L) significantly reduced death or reinfarction at 30 days. — T / F
- C. In patients with acute brain injury (TRAIN), a liberal strategy reduced the proportion with unfavourable neurological outcome. — T / F
- D. In cardiac surgery (TRICS-III), a restrictive threshold of 75 g/L was inferior to a liberal one. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – T, D – F.**

TRISS (n = 1005) showed no mortality difference and halved transfusion use. MINT (n = 3504) found a non-significant trend towards fewer events with a liberal strategy (16.9 % vs 14.5 %; RR 1.15, p = 0.07) – so a liberal approach is reasonable but not proven. TRAIN (2024) found fewer unfavourable 180-day outcomes with a 90 g/L threshold in TBI, SAH and ICH; HEMOTION (TBI) showed a non-significant trend in the same direction. TRICS-III showed non-inferiority of restrictive transfusion. Ref: Holst LB et al. *N Engl J Med* 2014; Carson JL et al. *N Engl J Med* 2023; Taccone FS et al. *JAMA* 2024; Mazer CD et al. *N Engl J Med* 2017.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W22-D2 · Blood products and coagulation disorders

**1.** A 46-year-old woman is bleeding heavily after a liver resection. Regarding blood components and haemostatic factors:

- A. Two pools of cryoprecipitate (10 donor units) raise the fibrinogen of an adult by roughly 1 g/L. — T / F
- B. Four-factor prothrombin complex concentrate contains factors II, VII, IX and X. — T / F
- C. Fibrinogen concentrate also contains clinically useful amounts of factor VIII and von Willebrand factor. — T / F
- D. Ionised hypocalcaemia has no clinically relevant effect on coagulation. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – F.**

Cryoprecipitate contains fibrinogen, FVIII, FXIII, vWF and fibronectin; fibrinogen concentrate is purified fibrinogen (FXIII only in small amounts). 4F-PCC contains II, VII, IX, X (plus protein C/S); 3F-PCC lacks adequate FVII. Calcium is a cofactor for several clotting steps and hypocalcaemia (frequent with citrated products) independently predicts mortality in major haemorrhage – keep iCa > 1.1 mmol/L. Ref: Rossaint R et al. *Crit Care* 2023 (European trauma bleeding guideline); Stanworth SJ et al. *Br J Haematol* 2022 (BSH major haemorrhage).

</details>

**2.** A 66-year-old man on day 7 of unfractionated heparin after vascular surgery has a fall in platelets from 260 to 95 × 10⁹/L and a new DVT. Regarding acquired disorders of haemostasis:

- A. A low 4Ts score (≤ 3) has a high negative predictive value for heparin-induced thrombocytopenia (HIT). — T / F
- B. In strongly suspected HIT, heparin should be stopped and a non-heparin anticoagulant (e.g. argatroban) started before results are available. — T / F
- C. In acquired haemophilia A, a prolonged APTT does not correct after mixing with normal plasma. — T / F
- D. Warfarin should be started immediately when HIT is diagnosed. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – F.**

The 4Ts (thrombocytopenia, timing, thrombosis, other causes) has an NPV > 99 % when low. With intermediate/high probability, stop all heparin (including flushes) and start a non-heparin anticoagulant (argatroban, bivalirudin, fondaparinux or a DOAC). An inhibitor (autoantibody to FVIII) prevents APTT correction on mixing, unlike factor deficiency. Warfarin started in acute HIT causes protein C depletion and venous limb gangrene – start only once platelets recover (> 150 × 10⁹/L) with overlap. Ref: Cuker A et al. *Blood Adv* 2018 (ASH HIT guideline); Tiede A et al. *Haematologica* 2020.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W22-D3 · Plasma exchange and TTP

**1.** A 39-year-old woman with myasthenic crisis is started on therapeutic plasma exchange (TPE). Regarding TPE:

- A. Exchanging 1–1.5 plasma volumes removes roughly 60–75 % of an intravascular substance. — T / F
- B. Sepsis is an ASFA category I indication. — T / F
- C. Highly protein-bound drugs with a small volume of distribution (e.g. IVIg, rituximab) are best given immediately before a TPE session. — T / F
- D. Albumin replacement increases plasma fibrinogen concentration. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – F.**

Removal follows first-order kinetics: one plasma volume removes ~63 %, 1.5 volumes ~78 %. ASFA 2023 category I indications include TTP, GBS, myasthenia gravis, anti-GBM disease and hyperviscosity; sepsis is category III (individualised). Drugs with small Vd and high protein binding are removed by TPE, so they are given after a session. Albumin replacement depletes coagulation factors and fibrinogen (and immunoglobulins), sometimes requiring plasma for the last part of the exchange. Ref: Connelly-Smith L et al. *J Clin Apher* 2023 (ASFA guidelines).

</details>

**2.** A 34-year-old woman presents with confusion, Hb 78 g/L, platelets 12 × 10⁹/L, schistocytes on the blood film, LDH 1800 U/L and creatinine 105 µmol/L. Regarding thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura (TTP):

- A. ADAMTS13 activity < 10 % supports the diagnosis. — T / F
- B. The PLASMIC score helps estimate the pre-test probability of severe ADAMTS13 deficiency. — T / F
- C. A positive direct antiglobulin (Coombs) test is typical. — T / F
- D. Severe renal failure requiring dialysis is usual. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – F.**

Immune TTP is caused by autoantibodies against ADAMTS13 (vWF-cleaving protease), producing ultra-large vWF multimers and platelet microthrombi. Diagnosis rests on microangiopathic haemolytic anaemia, thrombocytopenia and ADAMTS13 < 10 %; PLASMIC (score 6–7 = high probability) guides empirical treatment while awaiting results. Haemolysis is DAT-negative. Renal impairment is usually mild – severe AKI suggests HUS or complement-mediated TMA. Ref: Zheng XL et al. *J Thromb Haemost* 2020 (ISTH); Bendapudi PK et al. *Lancet Haematol* 2017.

</details>

**3.** A 27-year-old man with confirmed immune TTP is admitted to ICU. Regarding treatment:

- A. Caplacizumab added to plasma exchange and immunosuppression shortened time to platelet normalisation and reduced recurrence. — T / F
- B. Caplacizumab increases the risk of mucocutaneous bleeding. — T / F
- C. Rituximab reduces the risk of relapse. — T / F
- D. Recombinant ADAMTS13 is the standard first-line treatment for immune TTP. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – F.**

Standard immune TTP treatment is daily plasma exchange (with plasma as replacement), corticosteroids, rituximab and caplacizumab (anti-vWF nanobody blocking vWF–platelet interaction; HERCULES). Caplacizumab causes mild bleeding (gingival, epistaxis) through vWF inhibition. Recombinant ADAMTS13 is licensed for congenital TTP; in immune TTP it is still investigational. Platelet transfusion is avoided unless life-threatening bleeding. Ref: Scully M et al. *N Engl J Med* 2019 (HERCULES); Zheng XL et al. *J Thromb Haemost* 2020.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W22-D4 · Venous thromboembolism

**1.** A 72-year-old woman with metastatic colorectal cancer presents with pleuritic chest pain and tachycardia. Regarding the diagnosis and treatment of pulmonary embolism (PE):

- A. An age-adjusted D-dimer cut-off (age × 10 µg/L above 50 years) increases specificity without compromising safety. — T / F
- B. A normal D-dimer excludes PE in a patient with high clinical probability. — T / F
- C. CT pulmonary angiography is contraindicated in pregnancy. — T / F
- D. Apixaban is non-inferior to dalteparin for treating cancer-associated VTE. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – T.**

D-dimer is used only to exclude PE in low/intermediate (PE-unlikely) probability; high-probability patients go straight to imaging. The age-adjusted cut-off (ADJUST-PE) safely increases the number of patients in whom PE can be excluded. CTPA is acceptable in pregnancy (low fetal dose; pregnancy-adapted YEARS reduces imaging). Caravaggio showed apixaban non-inferior to dalteparin without more major bleeding; DOACs are now first-line for most cancer-associated VTE (caution in luminal GI/GU cancers). Ref: Konstantinides SV et al. *Eur Heart J* 2020 (ESC 2019 PE guideline); Agnelli G et al. *N Engl J Med* 2020.

</details>

**2.** A 45-year-old man with a traumatic subdural haematoma develops a proximal femoral DVT on day 4. Regarding inferior vena cava (IVC) filters:

- A. An IVC filter is indicated for acute proximal DVT or PE when anticoagulation is absolutely contraindicated. — T / F
- B. In PREPIC2, adding a retrievable filter to anticoagulation did not reduce recurrent PE at 3 months. — T / F
- C. Retrievable filters should be removed once anticoagulation is established and the risk period has passed. — T / F
- D. The patient should be anticoagulated once the contraindication resolves, even if a filter is in place. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – T.**

Filters are reserved for patients with acute VTE who cannot be anticoagulated (here, an intracranial bleed) or rarely for recurrence despite therapeutic anticoagulation. PREPIC2 (n = 399) found no reduction in recurrent PE. Filters increase later DVT and have complications (thrombosis, migration, fracture), so anticoagulation should be started when safe and retrievable filters removed. A prophylactic filter in major trauma did not reduce PE or death at 90 days (Ho et al. 2019). Ref: Mismetti P et al. *JAMA* 2015 (PREPIC2); Ho KM et al. *N Engl J Med* 2019; Konstantinides SV et al. *Eur Heart J* 2020.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W22-D5 · Viscoelastic testing and massive haemorrhage

**1.** A 36-year-old woman with a pelvic fracture is bleeding despite 4 units of red cells and 2 units of plasma. ROTEM shows EXTEM clotting time 70 s (normal), EXTEM A5 28 mm (low), FIBTEM A5 5 mm (low) and maximum lysis 4 % (normal). Platelets are 140 × 10⁹/L. She received tranexamic acid 40 minutes after injury. Which is the most appropriate haemostatic intervention?

- A. Fibrinogen concentrate (or cryoprecipitate)
- B. Further fresh frozen plasma 15 ml/kg
- C. Platelet transfusion
- D. A second dose of tranexamic acid
- E. Protamine sulphate

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.**

A low EXTEM A5 with a low FIBTEM A5 (< 7–9 mm) indicates impaired clot firmness due to fibrinogen deficiency (FIBTEM removes the platelet contribution). A normal clotting time argues against significant factor deficiency (plasma), adequate platelet count with low FIBTEM points away from platelets, normal lysis means no extra antifibrinolytic, and there is no heparin effect (INTEM/HEPTEM would show it). European guidance recommends fibrinogen concentrate or cryoprecipitate for FIBTEM-defined hypofibrinogenaemia or fibrinogen < 1.5 g/L. Ref: Rossaint R et al. *Crit Care* 2023.

</details>

**2.** A cardiac surgical ICU uses TEG to guide transfusion. Regarding interpretation of TEG:

- A. A prolonged R time that normalises in the heparinase cup indicates residual heparin effect. — T / F
- B. A low MA with a normal functional fibrinogen result suggests platelet deficiency or dysfunction. — T / F
- C. LY30 > 3 % suggests hyperfibrinolysis. — T / F
- D. Standard kaolin TEG is insensitive to the effects of aspirin and P2Y12 inhibitors. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – T.**

R = time to initial fibrin formation (factors, heparin); comparison with a heparinase cup isolates heparin effect. MA reflects clot strength (≈ 80 % platelets, 20 % fibrinogen); a functional fibrinogen assay separates the two. LY30 > 3 % (TEG) indicates fibrinolysis in trauma. Thrombin generated in standard assays overrides the thromboxane and ADP pathways, so aspirin and clopidogrel effects are missed unless platelet mapping is used. Ref: Bolliger D et al. *Transfus Med Rev* 2012; Wikkelsø A et al. *Cochrane Database Syst Rev* 2016.

</details>

**3.** Regarding the evidence base and limitations of viscoelastic haemostatic assays (VHA):

- A. In ITACTIC, VHA-guided transfusion in trauma did not improve the primary outcome compared with conventional coagulation test-guided therapy. — T / F
- B. VHA-guided algorithms in cardiac surgery reduce blood product use. — T / F
- C. Standard VHA reliably quantifies DOAC anticoagulant activity. — T / F
- D. Because VHA is run at 37 °C, it accurately reflects coagulopathy caused by hypothermia. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – F.**

ITACTIC (n = 396) found no difference in patients alive and free of massive transfusion at 24 h. Meta-analyses (mainly cardiac surgery) show VHA-guided algorithms reduce transfusion of plasma and platelets, with low-certainty evidence on mortality. DOAC effects are detected inconsistently (dedicated assays such as anti-Xa levels are needed). Samples are warmed to 37 °C, so hypothermia-induced enzyme slowing is masked unless the analyser temperature is set to the patient's. Ref: Baksaas-Aasen K et al. *Intensive Care Med* 2021 (ITACTIC); Wikkelsø A et al. *Cochrane Database Syst Rev* 2016.

</details>

**4.** A 30-year-old man with blunt trauma and haemorrhagic shock is receiving a major haemorrhage protocol. Regarding his resuscitation:

- A. Tranexamic acid 1 g over 10 min followed by 1 g over 8 h should be given within 3 h of injury. — T / F
- B. Ionised calcium should be maintained within the normal range. — T / F
- C. A fibrinogen level of 1.5–2 g/L is a reasonable target. — T / F
- D. A haemoglobin target of 100–110 g/L is recommended during active bleeding. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – F.**

CRASH-2 showed reduced bleeding deaths when TXA is given within 3 h (harm after 3 h). Hypocalcaemia from citrate is common and should be corrected. The European guideline recommends fibrinogen replacement when < 1.5 g/L or VHA signs of deficiency, and an Hb target of 70–90 g/L during bleeding. Ref: CRASH-2 Collaborators. *Lancet* 2010; Rossaint R et al. *Crit Care* 2023.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W23-D1 · Trauma scoring and initial assessment

**1.** A trauma registry nurse is coding a 44-year-old cyclist's injuries. Regarding anatomical and physiological trauma scores:

- A. The Injury Severity Score (ISS) is the sum of the squares of the highest AIS scores in the three most severely injured ISS body regions. — T / F
- B. Any single injury coded AIS 6 automatically gives an ISS of 75. — T / F
- C. The New Injury Severity Score (NISS) uses the three highest AIS scores regardless of body region. — T / F
- D. The Revised Trauma Score (RTS) uses GCS, systolic blood pressure and respiratory rate. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – T.**

ISS (range 1–75) uses six body regions; ISS > 15 defines major trauma. An AIS 6 (currently untreatable) injury sets ISS to 75. NISS counts multiple severe injuries in one region (e.g. several abdominal injuries) and predicts mortality slightly better. RTS is a weighted physiological score; TRISS combines ISS, RTS, age and blunt/penetrating mechanism to estimate probability of survival. Ref: Baker SP et al. *J Trauma* 1974; Osler T et al. *J Trauma* 1997; Boyd CR et al. *J Trauma* 1987.

</details>

**2.** A 52-year-old man is brought to the emergency department after a rear-end car collision at low speed. He is alert, not intoxicated and complains of neck pain. Regarding clinical decision rules for cervical spine imaging:

- A. NEXUS low-risk criteria include no midline tenderness, normal alertness, no intoxication, no focal deficit and no painful distracting injury. — T / F
- B. Under the Canadian C-spine rule, a dangerous mechanism is a high-risk factor mandating imaging. — T / F
- C. The Canadian C-spine rule was validated in patients with GCS < 15. — T / F
- D. Flexion-extension radiographs are routinely recommended after a normal CT to exclude ligamentous injury. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – F.**

NEXUS (five criteria) and the Canadian C-spine rule (high-risk factors: age ≥ 65, dangerous mechanism, paraesthesia; then low-risk factors permitting range-of-motion assessment) identify patients who need no imaging; the Canadian rule is more sensitive and specific. Both apply only to alert, stable patients (GCS 15). CT is first-line in adults; flexion-extension views add little and are not recommended. Ref: Hoffman JR et al. *N Engl J Med* 2000; Stiell IG et al. *N Engl J Med* 2003; NICE NG232 2023.

</details>

**3.** A 30-year-old man is ventilated after a motorcycle crash with mid-face (Le Fort II) and left orbital floor fractures. Two hours after admission his left eye is markedly proptosed and tense to palpation, the eyelids are tight, and there is a new relative afferent pupillary defect. Intraocular pressure measured with a tonometer is 48 mmHg. Which is the most appropriate immediate action?

- A. Urgent CT of the orbit before any intervention
- B. Intravenous mannitol and wait for ophthalmology review the next morning
- C. Insertion of an ICP monitor
- D. Ocular massage to lower intraocular pressure
- E. Lateral canthotomy and inferior cantholysis at the bedside

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**E.**

Orbital compartment syndrome (usually retrobulbar haematoma) causes irreversible optic nerve ischaemia within ~90–120 min. Proptosis, a tense orbit, raised IOP (> 40 mmHg) and an afferent pupillary defect mandate immediate lateral canthotomy and cantholysis; imaging must not delay decompression. Medical measures (acetazolamide, mannitol) are adjuncts only. Ocular massage is contraindicated; ICP monitoring does not address the orbit. Ref: Lima V et al. *Surv Ophthalmol* 2009.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W23-D2 · Trauma haemorrhage and tranexamic acid

**1.** A 66-year-old man on apixaban is in haemorrhagic shock after a fall from a height with a liver laceration and a moderate TBI. Regarding his resuscitation:

- A. In patients with severe TBI, a MAP of ≥ 80 mmHg should be maintained rather than permissive hypotension. — T / F
- B. Tranexamic acid should be given as early as possible and within 3 h of injury. — T / F
- C. Idarucizumab should be used to reverse apixaban. — T / F
- D. In PROPPR, a 1:1:1 plasma:platelet:red cell ratio reduced 30-day mortality compared with 1:1:2. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – F.**

Permissive hypotension (SBP 80–90 mmHg) is acceptable until bleeding is controlled except with severe TBI, where MAP ≥ 80 mmHg is recommended to protect cerebral perfusion. TXA should be given early (each 15 min delay reduces benefit). Idarucizumab reverses only dabigatran; for apixaban use andexanet alfa or PCC. PROPPR showed no difference in 24-h or 30-day mortality, although 1:1:1 achieved haemostasis more often and reduced exsanguination deaths at 24 h. Ref: Rossaint R et al. *Crit Care* 2023; Holcomb JB et al. *JAMA* 2015 (PROPPR).

</details>

**2.** Regarding randomised trials of tranexamic acid (TXA) in trauma:

- A. CRASH-3 found that TXA within 3 h reduced head-injury-related death in patients with mild-to-moderate TBI. — T / F
- B. PATCH-Trauma found that pre-hospital TXA did not increase the proportion of patients surviving with a favourable functional outcome at 6 months. — T / F
- C. In CRASH-2, TXA given more than 3 h after injury increased the risk of death from bleeding. — T / F
- D. CRASH-1 showed that corticosteroids reduced mortality after TBI. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – F.**

CRASH-3 (12 737 patients) showed reduced head-injury death in mild-moderate TBI treated within 3 h, but not in severe TBI (GCS 3 or bilateral unreactive pupils). PATCH-Trauma (2023) showed no improvement in favourable GOS-E at 6 months, although 24-h and 28-day mortality were lower. CRASH-2 exploratory analysis found benefit within 1 h, less at 1–3 h and harm after 3 h. CRASH-1 (methylprednisolone) increased mortality. Ref: CRASH-3 Collaborators. *Lancet* 2019; PATCH-Trauma Investigators. *N Engl J Med* 2023; CRASH-2 Collaborators. *Lancet* 2011.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W23-D3 · Chest and neck trauma

**1.** A 75-year-old woman taking apixaban for atrial fibrillation (last dose 6 h ago) falls down stairs and fractures right ribs 4–9. She has no other injuries. Despite paracetamol and IV morphine she cannot cough effectively, her SpO2 is 91 % on 4 L/min oxygen and her incentive spirometry volume is falling. Which analgesic technique is most appropriate?

- A. Thoracic epidural analgesia
- B. Erector spinae (or serratus anterior) plane catheter
- C. Thoracic paravertebral catheter
- D. Increase IV morphine with a PCA and add a sedative at night
- E. Entonox (50 % nitrous oxide) during physiotherapy

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

Elderly patients with ≥ 3 rib fractures have high morbidity; effective regional analgesia improves cough and reduces pulmonary complications. With recent apixaban, neuraxial and deep blocks (epidural, paravertebral) are contraindicated under ASRA/ESAIC guidance, whereas superficial fascial plane blocks (erector spinae, serratus anterior) are compressible and considered acceptable. Escalating opioids plus sedatives risks hypoventilation; nitrous oxide can enlarge an occult pneumothorax. Ref: Horlocker TT et al. *Reg Anesth Pain Med* 2018; Kietaibl S et al. *Eur J Anaesthesiol* 2022.

</details>

**2.** A 25-year-old man arrives after a high-speed car crash with chest wall bruising, tachypnoea and hypotension. Regarding injuries addressed during the ATLS primary survey:

- A. In adults, needle decompression of a tension pneumothorax is recommended in the 4th–5th intercostal space just anterior to the mid-axillary line. — T / F
- B. An initial chest drain output of more than 500 ml mandates thoracotomy. — T / F
- C. Blunt thoracic aortic injury is reliably excluded during the primary survey by a normal supine chest radiograph. — T / F
- D. Diaphragmatic rupture must be diagnosed and repaired during the primary survey. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – F.**

ATLS 10 moved adult needle decompression to the 4th/5th ICS anterior to the mid-axillary line because the chest wall is thinner there. Massive haemothorax (≥ 1500 ml immediately or > 200 ml/h for 2–4 h) prompts thoracotomy. Aortic injury and diaphragmatic injury are secondary-survey diagnoses requiring CT; a supine CXR can be normal in aortic injury. Primary-survey thoracic problems are airway obstruction, tension/open pneumothorax, massive haemothorax, flail chest with contusion and cardiac tamponade. Ref: American College of Surgeons. *ATLS Student Course Manual*, 10th ed. 2018; ATLS Subcommittee. *J Trauma Acute Care Surg* 2013.

</details>

**3.** A 31-year-old man is stabbed in the left side of the neck, penetrating the platysma. He is haemodynamically stable. Regarding penetrating neck injury:

- A. Hard signs (expanding or pulsatile haematoma, active haemorrhage, bruit/thrill, shock, airway compromise) warrant immediate operative exploration. — T / F
- B. Minimal subcutaneous emphysema alone mandates immediate neck exploration. — T / F
- C. All zone II injuries should be explored surgically. — T / F
- D. In stable patients without hard signs, CT angiography is the initial investigation. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – T.**

The modern "no-zone" approach (WTA 2019) bases management on clinical signs rather than anatomical zones: hard signs → operating room; soft signs (minor haematoma, dysphonia, dysphagia, minor emphysema) or no signs → multidetector CTA, followed by endoscopy if aerodigestive injury is suspected. Mandatory exploration of zone II produced high negative exploration rates. Ref: Sperry JL et al. *J Trauma Acute Care Surg* 2019 (WTA algorithm).

</details>


## Case-style practice · W23-D4 · Burns

**1.** A 70-kg man is admitted after a kitchen fire with mixed-depth burns of the trunk and arms. Regarding burn size estimation and early prognosis:

- A. The patient's palm alone (excluding the fingers) represents about 1 % of total body surface area. — T / F
- B. The modified Brooke formula estimates 4 ml/kg/%TBSA in the first 24 h. — T / F
- C. The revised Baux score adds 5 points for inhalation injury. — T / F
- D. Superficial (epidermal) burns with erythema only are included in the TBSA used for fluid calculation. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – F, B – F, C – F, D – F.**

The patient's palm including the fingers ≈ 1 % TBSA (palm alone ≈ 0.5 %). Parkland = 4 ml/kg/%TBSA; modified Brooke = 2 ml/kg/%TBSA; current ABA guidance suggests starting at 2–4 ml/kg/%TBSA and titrating. Revised Baux = age + %TBSA + 17 for inhalation injury. Erythema (superficial epidermal) burns are excluded from TBSA. Ref: Osler T et al. *J Trauma* 2010; Pham TN et al. *J Burn Care Res* 2008; ISBI Practice Guidelines Committee. *Burns* 2016.

</details>

**2.** A 38-year-old man is rescued from a burning house. He has 30 % TBSA mixed-depth burns including the face and neck and was exposed to smoke for about 10 minutes. He is alert, SpO2 96 % on 15 L/min oxygen via a non-rebreathe mask, and COHb is 9 %. All of the following are indications for early tracheal intubation EXCEPT:

- A. Stridor or a hoarse, changing voice
- B. Deep circumferential burns of the neck
- C. Singed nasal hairs alone, with a normal voice, no oropharyngeal soot or swelling and normal oxygenation
- D. GCS of 7
- E. Extensive facial burns with progressive oropharyngeal swelling

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.**

Airway oedema peaks at 12–36 h and intubation becomes progressively harder, so early intubation (with an uncut tube) is indicated for signs of airway obstruction (stridor, voice change, progressive swelling), deep facial/circumferential neck burns, reduced consciousness or respiratory failure. Singed nasal hair or facial burns alone are not an indication; such patients need close observation, and many are intubated unnecessarily – unnecessary intubation has its own harms. Fibreoptic laryngoscopy helps when uncertain. Ref: Walker PF et al. *Crit Care* 2015; Romanowski KS et al. *J Burn Care Res* 2016.

</details>

**3.** A 3-year-old boy (15 kg) pulled a kettle of boiling water over himself, with 18 % TBSA partial-thickness scalds. Regarding paediatric burns:

- A. A Lund–Browder chart is preferred because the head accounts for a larger proportion of TBSA in young children. — T / F
- B. Formal burn fluid resuscitation is usually indicated in children with burns > 10 % TBSA. — T / F
- C. Young children need maintenance fluid containing glucose in addition to resuscitation fluid. — T / F
- D. Deep partial-thickness burns typically blanch with brisk capillary refill and are very painful. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – F.**

In infants the head is ~18–20 % TBSA and the legs proportionally smaller, so the rule of nines overestimates or underestimates – use Lund–Browder. Children need resuscitation from > 10 % TBSA (adults > 15–20 %), plus glucose-containing maintenance because of limited glycogen stores. Superficial partial-thickness burns are moist, pink, blanch and are painful; deep partial-thickness burns are blotchy, with sluggish or absent capillary refill and reduced sensation. Ref: ISBI Practice Guidelines Committee. *Burns* 2016; Jeschke MG et al. *Nat Rev Dis Primers* 2020.

</details>

**4.** A 70-kg man with 50 % TBSA flame burns arrived 3 h after injury. Resuscitation was started with Hartmann's solution according to the Parkland formula. At 8 h, his MAP is 78 mmHg, HR 110/min, lactate 1.8 mmol/L and urine output has averaged 1.8 ml/kg/h for the last 3 h. Which is the most appropriate adjustment?

- A. Continue the calculated Parkland rate unchanged until 24 h
- B. Increase the rate because heart rate is above 100/min
- C. Add 5 % albumin 1 L as a bolus
- D. Reduce the infusion rate by about 20–30 % and reassess hourly
- E. Titrate fluids to a CVP of 12–15 mmHg

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.**

Formulae only give a starting rate; fluids should be titrated hourly to a urine output of ~0.5 ml/kg/h (≈ 30–50 ml/h) in adults (1 ml/kg/h in children). Persistently high urine output indicates over-resuscitation ("fluid creep"), which causes abdominal and extremity compartment syndromes, pulmonary oedema and conversion of wound depth. Tachycardia is expected in burn shock and is a poor guide; CVP-targeted resuscitation increases fluid loading. Albumin as rescue may be considered when requirements escalate, not with an excessive urine output. Ref: Pham TN et al. *J Burn Care Res* 2008 (ABA guidelines); ISBI Practice Guidelines Committee. *Burns* 2016.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W23-D5 · Limb compartment syndrome

**1.** A 27-year-old sedated, ventilated man has a closed tibial shaft fracture that was nailed 12 h ago. His calf is tense. Regarding acute compartment syndrome:

- A. A perfusion pressure (diastolic BP minus compartment pressure) < 30 mmHg supports the diagnosis. — T / F
- B. Tibial shaft fractures are the commonest cause of acute compartment syndrome. — T / F
- C. The limb should be elevated well above heart level to reduce swelling. — T / F
- D. Continuous compartment pressure monitoring is useful in sedated patients at risk. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – T.**

ΔP = diastolic pressure − compartment pressure < 30 mmHg (McQueen) is the most used threshold, especially valuable in obtunded patients where pain (the key early clinical sign, particularly on passive stretch) cannot be assessed – continuous pressure monitoring is advised. Tibial fractures account for ~40 % of cases. Elevation reduces arterial inflow; the limb should be kept at heart level, with circumferential dressings removed and urgent fasciotomy if confirmed. Ref: McQueen MM, Court-Brown CM. *J Bone Joint Surg Br* 1996; von Keudell AG et al. *Lancet* 2015.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W24-D1 · Poisoning: general principles and antidotes

**1.** A 19-year-old woman (50 kg) took about 60 tablets of ferrous sulphate (65 mg elemental iron each) 3 hours ago. She has vomited twice and has abdominal pain. Regarding iron poisoning:

- A. Ingestion of more than 60 mg/kg of elemental iron is potentially severe. — T / F
- B. Serum iron is most informative when measured 1 hour after ingestion. — T / F
- C. Desferrioxamine–iron complexes can colour the urine reddish ("vin rosé"). — T / F
- D. Prolonged desferrioxamine infusion (> 24 h) has been associated with ARDS. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – T, D – T.**

She has ingested ~78 mg/kg elemental iron (3900 mg/50 kg): < 20 mg/kg rarely causes toxicity, 20–60 mg/kg moderate, > 60 mg/kg potentially severe. Serum iron should be measured at ~4 h (peak 4–6 h; later for modified-release). Desferrioxamine is given for severe toxicity (shock, acidosis, altered consciousness) or high levels; ferrioxamine is renally excreted and colours the urine. Infusions beyond 24 h are linked to pulmonary toxicity. Activated charcoal does not bind iron; whole-bowel irrigation is considered if many tablets are visible on X-ray. Ref: Chang TP, Rangan C. *Pediatr Emerg Care* 2011; Manoguerra AS et al. *Clin Toxicol* 2005.

</details>

**2.** Regarding specific antidotes in poisoning:

- A. Methylene blue is the first-line antidote for cyanide poisoning. — T / F
- B. In patients with G6PD deficiency, methylene blue is the preferred treatment for methaemoglobinaemia. — T / F
- C. Flumazenil should be given routinely to comatose patients after an unknown mixed overdose. — T / F
- D. Naloxone reverses tramadol-induced seizures. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – F, B – F, C – F, D – F.**

Hydroxocobalamin is first-line for cyanide (with sodium thiosulfate as adjunct). Methylene blue treats methaemoglobinaemia but is ineffective and can cause haemolysis in G6PD deficiency – exchange transfusion or ascorbic acid are alternatives. Flumazenil can precipitate seizures in mixed overdoses (TCA, benzodiazepine dependence) and is not used routinely. Tramadol seizures are serotonergic/monoaminergic; naloxone does not prevent them – use benzodiazepines. Ref: Dart RC et al. *Ann Emerg Med* 2018 (antidote stocking consensus); Jang DH, Hoffman RS. *Ann Emerg Med* 2011 (methylene blue).

</details>


## Case-style practice · W24-D2 · Paracetamol poisoning

**1.** A 26-year-old woman presents 9 hours after taking 40 × 500 mg paracetamol tablets. Regarding management in current UK practice:

- A. A single treatment line starting at 100 mg/L at 4 hours is used irrespective of individual risk factors. — T / F
- B. The 12-hour SNAP acetylcysteine regimen causes fewer anaphylactoid reactions and less vomiting than the traditional 21-hour regimen. — T / F
- C. After a staggered overdose the nomogram cannot be used and acetylcysteine should be given. — T / F
- D. Acetylcysteine has no benefit when started more than 24 hours after ingestion. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – F.**

Since 2012 the MHRA uses a single 100 mg/L line because "high-risk" factors (enzyme inducers, alcohol, malnutrition) are poorly predictive. SNAP showed fewer adverse reactions with a 12-h regimen, now widely adopted. Staggered or unknown-time ingestion invalidates the nomogram. Late acetylcysteine still reduces mortality in established liver failure (improved haemodynamics and oxygen delivery), so it should be given at any time with evidence of toxicity. Ref: Bateman DN et al. *Lancet* 2014 (SNAP); Keays R et al. *BMJ* 1991; MHRA. *Drug Safety Update* 2012.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W24-D3 · Tricyclic antidepressant poisoning

**1.** A 34-year-old man is intubated after an amitriptyline overdose with a QRS of 150 ms. Regarding treatment of TCA cardiotoxicity:

- A. Flecainide is appropriate for broad-complex tachycardia. — T / F
- B. Sodium bicarbonate is given to a target arterial pH of about 7.45–7.55. — T / F
- C. Physostigmine is recommended to reverse anticholinergic toxicity. — T / F
- D. Class IA antiarrhythmics such as procainamide are contraindicated. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – F, B – T, C – F, D – T.**

TCAs block fast sodium channels; sodium bicarbonate (1–2 mmol/kg boluses) provides a sodium load and alkalinisation that reduce drug binding, aiming for pH 7.45–7.55. Class IA and IC drugs (procainamide, flecainide) block sodium channels and worsen toxicity; lidocaine (IB) is a second-line option. Physostigmine may cause bradycardia, asystole and seizures in TCA poisoning. Lipid emulsion and ECMO are rescue options. Ref: Body R et al. *Emerg Med J* 2011; Bruccoleri RE, Burns MM. *J Med Toxicol* 2016.

</details>

**2.** A 17-year-old girl is brought in 2 hours after taking her mother's amitriptyline. Which of the following are typical clinical features of TCA toxicity?

- A. Sinus tachycardia — T / F
- B. Pinpoint pupils — T / F
- C. Hyperactive bowel sounds and diarrhoea — T / F
- D. Profuse sweating — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – F.**

TCAs have anticholinergic effects: tachycardia (the most common ECG finding), dilated pupils, dry flushed skin, ileus, urinary retention and agitation/delirium, plus sodium-channel blockade (wide QRS, R in aVR > 3 mm), alpha-blockade (hypotension), seizures and coma. Miosis, increased bowel activity and sweating suggest cholinergic (organophosphate) or opioid toxicity. Ref: Kerr GW et al. *Emerg Med J* 2001; Body R et al. *Emerg Med J* 2011.

</details>

**3.** A 23-year-old woman is found drowsy with an empty pack of dosulepin. GCS is 10, HR 128/min, BP 84/50 mmHg and the ECG shows sinus tachycardia with a QRS of 140 ms and a terminal R wave of 4 mm in aVR. Venous pH is 7.30. She has not had seizures. Which is the most appropriate immediate treatment?

- A. Amiodarone 300 mg IV
- B. Magnesium sulphate 2 g IV
- C. Physostigmine 1 mg IV
- D. Flumazenil 0.5 mg IV
- E. Sodium bicarbonate 8.4 % 1–2 mmol/kg IV

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**E.**

QRS > 100 ms (seizure risk) and > 160 ms (ventricular arrhythmia risk), a prominent terminal R in aVR, hypotension and acidosis indicate sodium-channel cardiotoxicity – treat with hypertonic sodium bicarbonate boluses, repeated to narrow the QRS and maintain pH 7.45–7.55, together with airway protection and fluids/vasopressors. Amiodarone prolongs QT and worsens hypotension; magnesium is for torsades; physostigmine and flumazenil can precipitate arrhythmias and seizures. Ref: Bruccoleri RE, Burns MM. *J Med Toxicol* 2016; Body R et al. *Emerg Med J* 2011.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W24-D4 · Toxic alcohols and smoke inhalation

**1.** A 49-year-old man is admitted 18 hours after drinking illicit spirits with friends. He complains of blurred vision "like a snowstorm" and abdominal pain. pH 7.05, PaCO2 2.6 kPa, bicarbonate 6 mmol/L, anion gap 32 mmol/L, lactate 3 mmol/L, measured osmolality 330 mOsm/kg (calculated 295). Ethanol is undetectable. Which is the most appropriate treatment?

- A. Fomepizole and haemodialysis
- B. Intravenous ethanol alone
- C. Sodium bicarbonate alone
- D. Thiamine and pyridoxine alone
- E. Acetylcysteine

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.**

Visual disturbance, severe high-anion-gap acidosis and an osmolar gap of 35 suggest methanol poisoning (formic acid toxicity to the retina and basal ganglia). Fomepizole (or ethanol) blocks alcohol dehydrogenase to stop formation of toxic metabolites; EXTRIP recommends haemodialysis for methanol poisoning with severe acidosis (pH ≤ 7.15), visual or neurological signs, or high levels, as it removes both parent alcohol and formate. Bicarbonate and folinic acid are adjuncts; thiamine/pyridoxine are adjuncts for ethylene glycol. Ref: Roberts DM et al. *Crit Care Med* 2015 (EXTRIP); McMartin K et al. *Br J Clin Pharmacol* 2016.

</details>

**2.** A 46-year-old firefighter is rescued unconscious from an enclosed-space fire. He is intubated, BP 82/40 mmHg, PaO2 32 kPa on FiO2 1.0, lactate 11 mmol/L, COHb 18 % and central venous saturation 88 %. There are no external burns. Carbon monoxide and cyanide toxicity are both suspected. All of the following are appropriate EXCEPT:

- A. Hydroxocobalamin 5 g IV
- B. Continue 100 % oxygen
- C. Sodium thiosulfate as an adjunct
- D. Sodium nitrite as first-line antidote
- E. Vasopressor support

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.**

In smoke inhalation, combined CO and cyanide toxicity is common; coma, shock and lactate > 8–10 mmol/L despite high PaO2 and a raised ScvO2 (impaired cellular O2 extraction) suggest cyanide. Hydroxocobalamin is first-line because it does not impair oxygen transport. Nitrites induce methaemoglobinaemia, which further reduces oxygen-carrying capacity already compromised by carboxyhaemoglobin, so they are avoided in fire victims. Thiosulfate (rhodanese substrate) is an acceptable adjunct. Ref: Anseeuw K et al. *Eur J Emerg Med* 2013; Baud FJ et al. *N Engl J Med* 1991.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W25-D1 · Liver disease in pregnancy

**1.** A 34-year-old primigravida at 35 weeks presents with a week of nausea, vomiting, polydipsia and malaise. She is mildly jaundiced and confused. BP 138/86 mmHg, glucose 2.4 mmol/L, ALT 320 U/L, bilirubin 95 µmol/L, INR 2.1, fibrinogen 1.1 g/L, platelets 110 × 10⁹/L, creatinine 175 µmol/L, urate high, ammonia raised. Ultrasound shows no haematoma. Which is the most likely diagnosis?

- A. HELLP syndrome
- B. Intrahepatic cholestasis of pregnancy
- C. Acute fatty liver of pregnancy
- D. Acute hepatitis E infection
- E. Thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.**

Third-trimester hepatic failure with hypoglycaemia, coagulopathy with low fibrinogen, encephalopathy, AKI, raised urate and ammonia, polydipsia and only modest transaminase rise fulfils the Swansea criteria (≥ 6) for AFLP. Treatment is prompt delivery after stabilisation (glucose, blood products) with intensive care. HELLP typically has marked hypertension, haemolysis and severe thrombocytopenia with preserved glucose and fibrinogen; cholestasis has pruritus and raised bile acids without liver failure; hepatitis E gives much higher transaminases; TTP has severe thrombocytopenia and MAHA. Ref: Ch'ng CL et al. *Gut* 2002; Knight M et al. *Gut* 2008.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W25-D2 · Obstetric emergencies

**1.** A 39-year-old woman is having a caesarean section at term under spinal anaesthesia. Immediately after delivery of the placenta she becomes acutely dyspnoeic, SpO2 falls to 78 %, BP drops to 60/30 mmHg and she becomes unresponsive. Echocardiography shows a dilated, poorly contracting right ventricle. Thirty minutes later she is oozing from all puncture sites with fibrinogen 0.6 g/L. Which is the most likely diagnosis?

- A. Pulmonary thromboembolism
- B. Amniotic fluid embolism
- C. Anaphylaxis to oxytocin
- D. High spinal block
- E. Placental abruption

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

Sudden cardiorespiratory collapse during labour or delivery (or within 30 min of placental delivery) with acute RV failure, hypoxaemia and early overt DIC (fibrinogen often < 2 g/L) in the absence of fever meets the SMFM diagnostic criteria for amniotic fluid embolism. Diagnosis is clinical; there is no confirmatory laboratory test. PE rarely causes early consumptive coagulopathy; anaphylaxis has bronchospasm/urticaria and no DIC; high spinal causes bradycardia and weakness without DIC; abruption precedes delivery with pain and bleeding. Management is supportive: CPR, RV support, early aggressive correction of coagulopathy and, if needed, ECMO. Ref: Clark SL et al. *Am J Obstet Gynecol* 2016; Pacheco LD et al. *Am J Obstet Gynecol* 2016 (SMFM).

</details>


## Case-style practice · W25-D3 · Maternal sepsis

**1.** A 29-year-old woman, 3 days after an emergency caesarean section, presents with fever 39.4 °C, severe abdominal pain out of proportion to signs, offensive lochia; her toddler has had a sore throat this week. HR 132/min, BP 82/45 mmHg, RR 28/min, lactate 4.6 mmol/L. Which is the most appropriate immediate action?

- A. Oral co-amoxiclav and review in 6 hours
- B. CT abdomen and pelvis before starting antibiotics
- C. Await blood culture results to target therapy
- D. Immediate laparotomy and hysterectomy before resuscitation
- E. IV broad-spectrum antibiotics including clindamycin within 1 hour, with fluids and critical care referral

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**E.**

This is septic shock, most likely from invasive group A streptococcal endometritis (household contact with pharyngitis, pain out of proportion). Antimicrobials should be given within 1 hour (after cultures, without delaying), with fluid resuscitation and early critical care input. Clindamycin is added to suppress toxin production in suspected GAS. Imaging and source control (which may include surgery) follow resuscitation; oral therapy and waiting for cultures are inappropriate. Ref: Royal College of Obstetricians and Gynaecologists. *Green-top Guideline 64b* 2012; Evans L et al. *Intensive Care Med* 2021 (Surviving Sepsis Campaign).

</details>


## Case-style practice · W25-D4 · Oncological emergencies

**1.** A 22-year-old man with newly diagnosed Burkitt lymphoma starts chemotherapy. Regarding tumour lysis syndrome (TLS):

- A. Rasburicase is contraindicated in G6PD deficiency. — T / F
- B. Allopurinol rapidly lowers an already raised serum uric acid. — T / F
- C. Urinary alkalinisation with sodium bicarbonate is recommended to prevent TLS. — T / F
- D. Hypercalcaemia is a typical laboratory feature of TLS. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – F.**

Rasburicase converts uric acid to allantoin, producing hydrogen peroxide that causes haemolysis and methaemoglobinaemia in G6PD deficiency. Allopurinol only prevents new uric acid formation (and increases xanthine); rasburicase is preferred for high-risk or established hyperuricaemia. Alkalinisation is no longer recommended – it promotes calcium phosphate and xanthine precipitation. TLS causes hyperkalaemia, hyperphosphataemia, hyperuricaemia and secondary hypocalcaemia; clinical TLS adds AKI, arrhythmia or seizures (Cairo–Bishop). Ref: Howard SC et al. *N Engl J Med* 2011; Cairo MS, Bishop M. *Br J Haematol* 2004.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W26-D1 · Perioperative risk assessment

**1.** A 71-year-old man is referred for cardiopulmonary exercise testing (CPET) before an elective open oesophagectomy. Regarding CPET and functional capacity:

- A. Peak oxygen consumption is most reliably estimated from the peak heart rate achieved. — T / F
- B. A ventilatory equivalent for CO2 (VE/VCO2) slope above about 34 at the anaerobic threshold is associated with increased postoperative risk. — T / F
- C. The anaerobic threshold can be identified non-invasively using the V-slope method. — T / F
- D. In the METS study, subjective clinician assessment of functional capacity accurately identified patients with poor fitness. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – F, B – T, C – T, D – F.**

CPET measures VO2 directly from expired gas (heart rate–based estimates are unreliable). The anaerobic threshold is detected when VCO2 rises disproportionately to VO2 (V-slope); AT < ~10–11 ml/kg/min and high VE/VCO2 (ventilatory inefficiency) predict complications. METS (1401 patients) showed that subjective assessment had poor sensitivity for detecting low fitness, whereas DASI and NT-proBNP added predictive value. Ref: Levett DZH et al. *Br J Anaesth* 2018 (POETTS); Wijeysundera DN et al. *Lancet* 2018 (METS).

</details>

**2.** A surgical team is choosing a risk model to counsel a 78-year-old woman before an emergency laparotomy. Regarding perioperative risk scores:

- A. P-POSSUM uses the same physiological and operative variables as POSSUM with a different equation to reduce overprediction of mortality. — T / F
- B. The NELA risk model is designed for patients undergoing emergency laparotomy. — T / F
- C. The ASA physical status classification incorporates the complexity of the planned surgery. — T / F
- D. A preoperative creatinine > 177 µmol/L (2 mg/dl) is one of the six Revised Cardiac Risk Index (RCRI) predictors. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – T.**

POSSUM (12 physiological + 6 operative variables) overpredicts mortality in low-risk patients; P-POSSUM recalibrated the equation. The NELA model (UK National Emergency Laparotomy Audit) is specific to emergency laparotomy. ASA grades the patient's pre-existing condition only. RCRI predictors: high-risk surgery, ischaemic heart disease, heart failure, cerebrovascular disease, insulin-treated diabetes and creatinine > 177 µmol/L; age is not included. Ref: Prytherch DR et al. *Br J Surg* 1998; Lee TH et al. *Circulation* 1999; Eugene N et al. *Br J Anaesth* 2018 (NELA model).

</details>


## Case-style practice · W26-D2 · Cardiac surgical ICU

**1.** A 69-year-old man with diabetes and CKD stage 3 is scheduled for urgent CABG and aortic valve replacement. Regarding cardiac surgery-associated AKI (CSA-AKI):

- A. Pre-existing CKD is one of the strongest risk factors. — T / F
- B. Emergency surgery increases the risk. — T / F
- C. Off-pump surgery eliminates the risk of AKI. — T / F
- D. Perioperative high-dose atorvastatin prevented AKI in a randomised trial. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – F.**

Risk factors include CKD, older age, diabetes, heart failure/low EF, emergency or combined surgery, long cardiopulmonary bypass and transfusion. Off-pump surgery reduced mild AKI in CORONARY but did not change dialysis or longer-term renal outcomes, and AKI still occurs. High-dose perioperative atorvastatin did not reduce AKI (Billings 2016). The KDIGO bundle in high-risk patients (biomarker-guided) reduced AKI in PrevAKI. Ref: Billings FT et al. *JAMA* 2016; Garg AX et al. *JAMA* 2014; Meersch M et al. *Intensive Care Med* 2017.

</details>

**2.** A 64-year-old woman is in the ICU 4 h after mitral valve repair, with epicardial ventricular wires connected to a temporary pacemaker set to VVI 80/min, output 5 mA, sensitivity 2 mV. Her own rhythm is now sinus at 95/min, but pacing spikes continue to appear at regular intervals, including one falling on a T wave. Which is the most appropriate adjustment?

- A. Increase the sensitivity by lowering the mV setting
- B. Increase the output to 10 mA
- C. Increase the pacing rate to 100/min
- D. Reverse the polarity of the pacing wires
- E. Switch to asynchronous VOO mode

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.**

Pacing spikes despite an intrinsic rhythm faster than the set rate = undersensing; spikes on the T wave risk R-on-T ventricular fibrillation. Making the pacemaker more sensitive (lower mV number) allows it to detect native QRS complexes and inhibit. Increasing output treats failure to capture; asynchronous mode is used to avoid inappropriate inhibition from oversensing (e.g. diathermy) and would worsen the competition. If undersensing persists, the generator should be set to a rate below the intrinsic rate or switched off with wires kept ready. Ref: Reade MC. *Anaesthesia* 2007; Gammage MD. *Heart* 2000.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W26-D3 · Organ donation

**1.** Regarding organ donation after circulatory (DCD) and brainstem (DBD) death:

- A. In UK DCD practice, the functional warm ischaemic time begins when systolic BP falls below 50 mmHg (or SpO2 < 70 %). — T / F
- B. Normothermic regional perfusion after DCD restores oxygenated blood flow to the brain as well as abdominal organs. — T / F
- C. The heart tolerates a longer period of cold ischaemia than the kidney. — T / F
- D. Lungs cannot be donated if the PaO2/FiO2 ratio is below 40 kPa at any time. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – F.**

Functional WIT starts with significant hypotension/hypoxia after withdrawal; organ-specific limits apply. NRP (abdominal or thoracoabdominal) deliberately excludes cerebral circulation by clamping/occluding the aortic arch vessels. Cold ischaemia tolerance: kidney ~24–36 h, liver ~12 h, heart ~4–6 h (ex-situ perfusion extends this). Lungs with poor initial oxygenation can often be optimised with recruitment, bronchoscopy and protective ventilation (or ex-vivo lung perfusion), so a low ratio is not an absolute exclusion. Ref: Meyfroidt G et al. *Intensive Care Med* 2019; Thuong M et al. *Transpl Int* 2016 (DCD consensus).

</details>

**2.** A 35-year-old man has been confirmed dead by neurological criteria and his family supports organ donation. Over 2 hours his urine output rises to 400 ml/h, serum sodium increases from 145 to 156 mmol/L and urine osmolality is 110 mOsm/kg. MAP is 72 mmHg on low-dose noradrenaline. Which is the most appropriate treatment for his polyuria?

- A. Intravenous hydrocortisone
- B. Insulin infusion
- C. Desmopressin (DDAVP) IV
- D. Triiodothyronine (T3) infusion
- E. Replace urine output ml-for-ml with 0.9 % saline

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.**

Central diabetes insipidus develops in most DBD donors; untreated hypernatraemia (> 155 mmol/L) is associated with graft dysfunction, particularly of the liver. Desmopressin (V2-selective) treats DI without vasoconstriction; low-dose vasopressin is an alternative that also supports BP. Losses should be replaced with hypotonic fluid (e.g. 5 % glucose), not saline, which worsens hypernatraemia. Hydrocortisone is part of donor management (and for vasopressor-refractory shock) and T3 is no longer routinely recommended, but neither treats DI. Ref: Meyfroidt G et al. *Intensive Care Med* 2019; Kotloff RM et al. *Crit Care Med* 2015.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W26-D4 · Transplant recipients in ICU

**1.** A 50-year-old man who had an orthotopic heart transplant 2 years ago is admitted with sepsis. Regarding the physiology of the denervated heart:

- A. Atropine does not increase the heart rate. — T / F
- B. Isoprenaline is an effective chronotrope. — T / F
- C. The resting heart rate is typically higher than normal (about 90–110/min). — T / F
- D. Indirect-acting sympathomimetics such as ephedrine have a reduced chronotropic effect. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – T.**

The transplanted heart lacks autonomic innervation: vagal tone is absent (higher intrinsic rate, no response to atropine or vagal manoeuvres), and it relies on circulating catecholamines and the Frank–Starling mechanism to raise output. Direct-acting agents (isoprenaline, adrenaline, dobutamine) work normally (beta-receptors may even be upregulated), whereas indirect agents relying on neuronal noradrenaline release (ephedrine) are less effective. Neostigmine can still cause bradycardia (and has caused asystole) owing to direct cardiac muscarinic effects. Ref: Costanzo MR et al. *J Heart Lung Transplant* 2010 (ISHLT guidelines); Cheng DCH, Ong DD. *Can J Anaesth* 1993.

</details>

**2.** A 56-year-old woman received a kidney transplant 7 months ago; her co-trimoxazole prophylaxis was stopped at 6 months and she recently had pulsed methylprednisolone for rejection. She presents with 5 days of dry cough and breathlessness. SpO2 is 86 % on air; CT shows bilateral diffuse ground-glass opacities with sparing of the periphery. LDH and serum beta-D-glucan are elevated. Which is the most appropriate treatment?

- A. Atovaquone suspension
- B. High-dose intravenous trimethoprim–sulfamethoxazole
- C. Voriconazole
- D. Ganciclovir
- E. Amoxicillin plus clarithromycin

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.**

Recent augmented immunosuppression, stopped prophylaxis, hypoxaemia with diffuse ground glass, high LDH and beta-D-glucan point to Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia; diagnosis is confirmed by BAL PCR/immunofluorescence but treatment should not be delayed. TMP-SMX (15–20 mg/kg/day of trimethoprim) is first-line; atovaquone is for mild disease or intolerance. Adjunctive steroids are standard in HIV with hypoxaemia but their benefit in non-HIV patients is uncertain. Voriconazole has no activity against Pneumocystis; ganciclovir is for CMV. Ref: Maschmeyer G et al. *J Antimicrob Chemother* 2016 (ECIL); Fishman JA, Gans H. *Clin Transplant* 2019 (AST).

</details>


## Case-style practice · W28-D1 · Pharmacokinetics in critical illness

**1.** A 70-year-old woman with combined hepatic and renal failure needs several drugs whose elimination does not depend on these organs. Regarding organ-independent drug elimination:

- A. Remifentanil is hydrolysed by non-specific blood and tissue esterases. — T / F
- B. Cisatracurium is eliminated mainly by Hofmann degradation. — T / F
- C. Suxamethonium is hydrolysed by plasma (butyryl)cholinesterase. — T / F
- D. Clevidipine is cleared mainly by hepatic cytochrome P450 metabolism. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – F.**

Remifentanil, esmolol and clevidipine are ester drugs hydrolysed by blood/tissue esterases, giving short, organ-independent half-lives (clevidipine t½ ~1 min). Cisatracurium undergoes temperature- and pH-dependent Hofmann elimination (producing laudanosine, less than atracurium). Suxamethonium and mivacurium are hydrolysed by plasma cholinesterase – prolonged block occurs with genetic variants or severe liver disease. Ref: Egan TD. *Clin Pharmacokinet* 1995; Kisor DF, Schmith VD. *Clin Pharmacokinet* 1999; Varon J, Marik PE. *Crit Care* 2008.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W28-D2 · Sedative drug toxicity

**1.** A 19-year-old man with severe TBI has received propofol at 5 mg/kg/h for 3 days to control ICP, with noradrenaline for CPP. He develops new right bundle branch block with coved ST elevation in V1–V2, progressive bradycardia, rising noradrenaline requirement, lactate 6 mmol/L, CK 9000 U/L, triglycerides 6.5 mmol/L and dark urine. Which is the most appropriate immediate action?

- A. Increase the propofol dose to suppress cerebral metabolism further
- B. Start an amiodarone infusion
- C. Give a 1 L crystalloid bolus and continue propofol
- D. Stop propofol, switch to an alternative sedative and provide organ support (including RRT or ECMO if needed)
- E. Add a ketamine infusion while continuing propofol

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.**

Propofol infusion syndrome (PRIS) – metabolic acidosis, rhabdomyolysis, hypertriglyceridaemia, Brugada-like ECG, bradyarrhythmias progressing to asystole, AKI and cardiac failure – is associated with doses > 4 mg/kg/h for > 48 h, young age, TBI, catecholamines and steroids. Mitochondrial fatty-acid oxidation is impaired. Management is immediate cessation of propofol, supportive care, pacing for bradycardia, and RRT or ECMO in refractory cases; carbohydrate supply may help. Amiodarone worsens bradycardia. Ref: Hemphill S et al. *Br J Anaesth* 2019; Kam PCA, Cardone D. *Anaesthesia* 2007.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W28-D4 · Receptor pharmacology

**1.** A septic patient on noradrenaline and dobutamine is also given several antagonists. Regarding drug–receptor interactions:

- A. An inverse agonist reduces the constitutive (baseline) activity of its receptor. — T / F
- B. A non-competitive antagonist increases the maximal effect (Emax) of an agonist. — T / F
- C. A partial agonist always has a lower affinity for its receptor than a full agonist. — T / F
- D. A drug's potency is determined only by its intrinsic efficacy. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – F.**

Inverse agonists stabilise the inactive receptor state and reduce constitutive activity. Competitive antagonists cause a parallel rightward shift (surmountable, Emax unchanged); non-competitive or irreversible antagonists decrease Emax. Partial agonists have submaximal intrinsic efficacy but may have high affinity (e.g. buprenorphine), so they can antagonise full agonists. Potency (EC50) depends on both affinity and efficacy (and on receptor reserve). Ref: Neubig RR et al. *Pharmacol Rev* 2003 (IUPHAR nomenclature); Rang HP. *Br J Pharmacol* 2006.

</details>

**2.** Regarding receptor types and second-messenger systems relevant to ICU drugs:

- A. Beta-1 adrenoceptors couple to Gs, increasing intracellular cAMP. — T / F
- B. Corticosteroids act mainly through membrane tyrosine-kinase receptors. — T / F
- C. The GABA-A receptor is a ligand-gated chloride channel. — T / F
- D. Muscarinic M2 receptors in the heart couple to Gq. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – T, D – F.**

Beta-1 (and beta-2) → Gs → adenylate cyclase → cAMP (dobutamine; PDE3 inhibitors such as milrinone prevent cAMP breakdown). Steroids and thyroid hormones act on intracellular nuclear receptors that alter gene transcription; insulin acts via a tyrosine-kinase receptor. GABA-A (benzodiazepines, propofol) and nicotinic receptors are ligand-gated ion channels. Cardiac M2 receptors couple to Gi (reducing cAMP and opening K channels → bradycardia); M1/M3 and alpha-1 couple to Gq. Ref: Alexander SPH et al. *Br J Pharmacol* 2023 (Concise Guide to Pharmacology).

</details>


## Case-style practice · W28-D5 · Anticoagulant pharmacology

**1.** Several ICU patients are receiving different anticoagulants. Regarding their mechanisms and reversal:

- A. Argatroban is eliminated mainly by the kidneys and should be avoided in renal failure. — T / F
- B. Idarucizumab reverses rivaroxaban. — T / F
- C. Andexanet alfa reverses dabigatran. — T / F
- D. Protamine fully reverses fondaparinux. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – F, B – F, C – F, D – F.**

Argatroban (direct thrombin inhibitor) is hepatically cleared and is therefore suitable in renal failure (dose-reduce in liver failure); bivalirudin is partly renally cleared. Idarucizumab is a humanised antibody fragment that binds dabigatran (direct thrombin inhibitor). Andexanet alfa is a decoy factor Xa that binds FXa inhibitors (apixaban, rivaroxaban, edoxaban). Fondaparinux (indirect, antithrombin-mediated FXa inhibitor) has no protamine binding; protamine fully reverses UFH and only partially LMWH. Ref: Pollack CV et al. *N Engl J Med* 2017 (RE-VERSE AD); Connolly SJ et al. *N Engl J Med* 2019 (ANNEXA-4); Cuker A et al. *Am J Hematol* 2019.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W29-D1 · CVC, arterial line, complications; ultrasound guidance

**1.** A 58-year-old man in the ICU needs invasive arterial monitoring and several limb vascular access sites are considered. Regarding the surface anatomy of peripheral arteries:

- A. At the wrist, the radial artery is palpated lateral to the tendon of flexor carpi radialis. — T / F
- B. In the antecubital fossa, the brachial artery lies lateral to the biceps tendon. — T / F
- C. At the inguinal ligament, the femoral artery lies at the mid-inguinal point, halfway between the anterior superior iliac spine and the pubic symphysis. — T / F
- D. On the dorsum of the foot, the dorsalis pedis artery runs just lateral to the tendon of extensor hallucis longus. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – T, D – T.**

The radial artery lies between the brachioradialis and flexor carpi radialis tendons (lateral to FCR). The brachial artery lies MEDIAL to the biceps tendon, with the median nerve medial to the artery – hence the nerve-injury risk of brachial cannulation. The femoral artery is at the mid-inguinal point (the mid-point of the inguinal ligament is slightly more lateral). Dorsalis pedis runs lateral to EHL and is absent or impalpable in a minority of people. Ultrasound guidance improves first-pass success at all of these sites. Reference: Lamperti M et al. International evidence-based recommendations on ultrasound-guided vascular access. **Intensive Care Med** 2012;38:1105–17.

</details>

**2.** A 71-year-old woman requires an ultrasound-guided right internal jugular central venous catheter. Regarding the relevant neck anatomy:

- A. In the lower neck the internal jugular vein usually lies lateral (often anterolateral) to the common carotid artery. — T / F
- B. The anterior border of the posterior triangle of the neck is formed by the posterior border of sternocleidomastoid. — T / F
- C. The external jugular vein runs deep to sternocleidomastoid throughout its course. — T / F
- D. The vagus nerve lies outside the carotid sheath, anterior to the internal jugular vein. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – F.**

The IJV lies lateral/anterolateral to the carotid within the carotid sheath; excessive head rotation increases arterial overlap, so a neutral or slightly rotated head is preferred with real-time ultrasound. The posterior triangle is bounded by SCM (anterior), trapezius (posterior) and clavicle (base). The external jugular vein is SUPERFICIAL, crossing over SCM beneath platysma. The vagus lies INSIDE the carotid sheath, posterior and between the artery and vein. Reference: Lamperti M et al. International evidence-based recommendations on ultrasound-guided vascular access. **Intensive Care Med** 2012;38:1105–17.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W29-D2 · Chest drain, pericardiocentesis, paracentesis, lumbar puncture

**1.** A 45-year-old man with suspected encephalitis in the ICU is being considered for lumbar puncture. Which statements about safety of the procedure are correct?

- A. If he is on twice-daily therapeutic-dose enoxaparin, at least 24 h should elapse after the last dose before needle insertion. — T / F
- B. If he had a GCS of 9 with a new unilateral fixed dilated pupil, brain imaging should precede the LP. — T / F
- C. Cellulitis over the lumbar puncture site is a contraindication to puncture through that area. — T / F
- D. A platelet count of 70 × 10⁹/L mandates platelet transfusion before LP. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – F.**

ASRA guidance (adopted widely for neuraxial needle placement) recommends ≥24 h after therapeutic LMWH. Signs of brain shift (reduced consciousness, focal signs, pupillary abnormality) require CT first; empirical antimicrobials must not be delayed for imaging/LP. Local skin/soft-tissue infection is a contraindication at that site. Most guidance accepts LP with platelets ≥40–50 × 10⁹/L, so 70 × 10⁹/L needs no transfusion. Reference: Horlocker TT et al. Regional anesthesia in the patient receiving antithrombotic or thrombolytic therapy (ASRA 4th ed.). **Reg Anesth Pain Med** 2018;43:263–309; van de Beek D et al. ESCMID guideline: diagnosis and treatment of acute bacterial meningitis. **Clin Microbiol Infect** 2016;22:S37–62.

</details>

**2.** In ICU bedside procedures, landmarks matter even when ultrasound is available. Regarding procedural anatomy:

- A. For paracentesis, a left lower quadrant site lateral to the rectus sheath reduces the risk of injuring the inferior epigastric vessels. — T / F
- B. In the femoral triangle the femoral nerve lies medial to the femoral vein. — T / F
- C. Echocardiography-guided pericardiocentesis chooses the entry point where the effusion is largest and closest to the skin, which is not necessarily subxiphoid. — T / F
- D. The inferior epigastric arteries run in the linea alba, so the midline infra-umbilical approach carries the greatest risk of abdominal wall bleeding. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – T, D – F.**

The inferior epigastric vessels run deep to the rectus muscles (roughly at the junction of the medial two-thirds and lateral third of a line from umbilicus to mid-inguinal point), so the lateral LLQ (and the avascular linea alba) are the safer sites; ultrasound guidance is recommended. In the femoral triangle the order from lateral to medial is Nerve–Artery–Vein (NAVY). Echo-guided pericardiocentesis (apical, parasternal or subxiphoid) has a lower complication rate than blind subxiphoid puncture. Reference: Adler Y et al. 2015 ESC Guidelines for the diagnosis and management of pericardial diseases. **Eur Heart J** 2015;36:2921–64; Biggins SW et al. AASLD practice guidance on ascites. **Hepatology** 2021;74:1014–48.

</details>

**3.** A 33-year-old woman has an LP for suspected idiopathic intracranial hypertension. Regarding lumbar anatomy and CSF physiology in adults:

- A. The line joining the iliac crests (Tuffier's line) usually crosses the L4 spinous process or L4–5 interspace. — T / F
- B. Total CSF volume is about 150 mL, with production of roughly 500 mL/day. — T / F
- C. Normal CSF glucose is about 60–70 % of the simultaneous plasma glucose. — T / F
- D. Opening pressure should be measured with the patient sitting upright, where the normal range is 25–35 cmH₂O. — T / F
